Real Estate Practice - Lesson Assignment 01

California practice standards and the regulated professional

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Explain what the Department of Real Estate regulates, which activities require a license, and how the broker and salesperson licenses differ in authority.
  • Describe the responsible broker's supervisory obligations and distinguish delegating a task from transferring responsibility.
  • Apply the competence standard to unfamiliar work and identify the boundary between explaining real estate practice and practicing law.
  • Evaluate whether an office's written policies actually control the conduct they are meant to govern.
  • Distinguish verified fact, unverified report, and professional opinion in communications with consumers.
  • Assemble a transaction file that shows what was done, when, by whom, and on what basis.

1. DRE authority and license categories

1.1 Why a license exists at all

Most commercial activity in California requires no occupational license. Real estate is different for a specific reason: the transactions are infrequent, the sums are large relative to the participants' net worth, the documents are technical, and the consumer usually cannot evaluate the quality of the service until long after it has been performed. A buyer who is sold a defective refrigerator learns quickly. A buyer who receives careless advice about a disclosure deadline may not learn for years, and by then the loss is unrecoverable.

Licensing responds to that asymmetry. It does not certify that a licensee is skilled, and it does not guarantee outcomes. What it does is create an identifiable, accountable person standing behind the advice, subject to standards of conduct, backed by a regulator with the authority to investigate and to remove that person from practice.

Everything in this course follows from that idea. When a rule seems burdensome, the productive question is not "is this required?" but "what consumer harm is this rule preventing?" A licensee who understands the second question usually gets the first one right without looking it up.

1.2 The Real Estate Law and the Commissioner's Regulations

Two bodies of authority govern licensed conduct in California, and they are not the same thing.

The Real Estate Law is statute — enacted by the Legislature, found in the Business and Professions Code. It establishes the Department, defines what activity requires a license, sets the grounds on which a license may be denied, suspended, or revoked, and creates the obligations that carry the most serious consequences.

The Commissioner's Regulations are administrative regulations adopted by the Real Estate Commissioner under authority the statute grants, found in the California Code of Regulations. They supply operational detail the statute leaves open: how trust funds must be handled, what records must be kept, what advertising must contain.

Three practical consequences follow:

  1. Both bind you. A licensee who complies with the statute but violates a regulation has still violated the law.
  2. They change on different schedules. Statutes change through legislation; regulations change through the rulemaking process. A licensee who learned a rule five years ago cannot assume it still reads the same way.
  3. Neither is the same as brokerage policy. Your office manual may be stricter than the law — and often should be — but it can never be looser, and following it is not a defense if it permits something the law forbids.

Beyond these two sit case law, which interprets both, and local ordinances, which can impose additional requirements on transactions in a particular jurisdiction. A licensee is not expected to be a lawyer. A licensee is expected to know which category a question falls into and to recognize when the answer requires someone else's expertise.

A useful habit: when you cannot immediately say whether a rule comes from statute, regulation, office policy, or local custom, treat that uncertainty itself as the signal to check before acting.

1.3 What activity requires a license

The licensing requirement turns on a combination of elements rather than on a job title. Broadly, a license is required when a person:

  1. acts for another person — not for their own account;
  2. for compensation or the expectation of compensation; and
  3. performs one of the activities the statute lists, which include selling, buying, offering, soliciting, negotiating, or leasing real property or a business opportunity, and soliciting borrowers or lenders for loans secured by real property.

Each element does work. An investor buying property for their own account is acting for themselves and needs no license. A person who genuinely volunteers with no expectation of compensation is outside the compensation element — though "compensation" is read broadly and includes things of value other than cash. And an activity not on the statutory list is not licensed activity, however close to real estate it feels.

The exemptions are specific rather than general. An attorney performing legal services, a person acting under a properly executed power of attorney, and certain trustees and public officials operate under defined exemptions. What matters for practice is the habit of mind: exemptions are narrow, and "this seems like it should be exempt" is not an analysis.

Why this matters to a licensee

You will be asked to pay or receive referral fees, to let an unlicensed assistant handle "just the paperwork," and to help a friend market a property informally. Each of those is an unlicensed-activity question. Paying an unlicensed person for activity that requires a license is a violation by the licensee, regardless of whether the unlicensed person understood the rule.

1.4 The two license levels

California issues two individual real estate licenses, and the difference between them is not seniority. It is legal authority.

A broker holds an independent license. A broker may operate a brokerage, employ salespersons, hold trust funds, advertise under the brokerage's identity, and contract with clients in the brokerage's own name.

A salesperson holds a derivative license. It authorizes licensed activity only for and under the supervision of an employing broker. A salesperson cannot hold client trust funds in their own right, cannot contract with clients in their own name, cannot be compensated directly by anyone other than their employing broker, and cannot operate independently no matter how much experience they accumulate.

That single distinction resolves a large fraction of the questions new licensees ask:

QuestionResolution
May a salesperson be paid directly by a lender for a referral?No. Compensation for licensed activity flows through the employing broker.
May two salespersons at different brokerages split a fee between themselves?No. The split is broker to broker.
May a salesperson open an office and advertise under a personal brand?Not independently. Advertising must identify the employing brokerage.
May a salesperson hold a buyer's deposit in a personal account?No. Trust funds are the broker's responsibility and belong in the broker's trust account.

A corporation may also hold a real estate license, acting through a designated officer who must be a licensed broker and who bears the supervisory responsibility for the corporation's licensed activity. The corporate form changes who signs and who is liable in contract; it does not dilute the supervisory obligation, which attaches to the designated officer personally.

1.5 License status, renewal, and discipline

A license has a term and must be renewed, with continuing education required for renewal. A license that is not renewed does not merely become inconvenient — the person may not perform licensed activity while it is inactive or expired, and performing licensed activity without a current license is itself a violation.

The Commissioner's disciplinary authority extends to suspension, revocation, and restriction of a license. Two features of that authority regularly surprise licensees:

  • It reaches conduct outside licensed activity. Conduct unrelated to a transaction can still bear on fitness to hold a license.
  • It does not require that a consumer suffered a loss. A trust fund violation with no shortage, an advertisement that misled no one, a record destroyed inside the retention period — each is complete as a violation whether or not anyone was harmed.

The Department also maintains a public license lookup, which reports license status, expiration, employing broker, and any public disciplinary record. Consumers use it. So should licensees, before agreeing to work with an unfamiliar counterpart on the other side of a transaction.

1.6 What the license does not authorize

Section 1.3 states what activity requires a license. The reciprocal question is asked less often and produces more discipline: what does holding one not permit?

The activityWhy the license does not reach it
Giving legal adviceSection 3.2 draws the boundary. Interpreting a contract's legal effect, advising on how title should be taken, or opining on the enforceability of a restriction is the practice of law.
Preparing legal instruments beyond filling in approved formsDrafting provisions that alter legal rights is authorship of a legal document.
Giving tax adviceHolding periods, basis, exclusions, exchanges, and the treatment of credits belong to a tax advisor.
AppraisingLesson 05, Section 5.1. A comparative market analysis is not an appraisal and may not be presented as one.
InspectingOpinions about the condition or remaining life of building systems require a qualification the license does not confer.
Loan origination activity requiring its own authorityMortgage activity carries its own licensing and endorsement framework.
Acting outside the brokerageA salesperson's authority flows through the responsible broker; there is no independent practice.
Escrow activity beyond what the framework permitsEscrow is regulated separately, and holding funds is the accounting duty in Section 6, not a service to be improvised.
Practicing in another jurisdictionA California license is a California license.

Two of these are worth stating with more force.

The legal boundary is crossed most often by accident, in answers to ordinary questions: what a clause means, whether a contingency still protects the client, what happens if someone breaches. These sound like transaction questions and they are legal questions, and Section 3.4's "I don't know" is the professional answer.

The scope of the salesperson's authority is a structural fact, not a formality. Every agreement is the brokerage's, every client is the brokerage's client, every trust fund is subject to the broker's control, and every decision to release, escalate, or settle is the broker's. A salesperson who thinks of the brokerage as an administrative arrangement has misunderstood what his own license is.

The practical consequence of this table: a licensee who can name the eight boundaries above will route eight categories of question rather than answering them. Section 3.4's argument is that routing is competence. This table is the list of what gets routed.

1.7 Checkpoint

Before continuing, you should be able to state: which two bodies of authority govern licensed conduct and how they differ; the three elements that make an activity require a license; and four specific things a salesperson may not do that a broker may.

2. Broker supervision

2.1 The responsible broker

Every brokerage has a broker who is responsible for its licensed activity. That responsibility is the structural feature that makes the whole licensing system work: a salesperson's derivative authority only means something if someone is actually supervising it.

The obligation is best understood by what it covers rather than by a definition. Reasonable supervision extends at minimum to:

  1. Transactions and documents — that agreements, disclosures, and notices are prepared, delivered, and retained correctly.
  2. Advertising — that what goes out under the brokerage's identity is truthful and properly identified.
  3. Trust funds — that money belonging to others is handled and accounted for correctly.
  4. Familiarity with the law — that licensees know the requirements applicable to the work they are doing.
  5. Complaint and dispute handling — that problems reach the broker rather than being managed quietly by the licensee who caused them.
  6. Records — that the brokerage's files are complete and retained.

2.2 Delegation does not transfer responsibility

A broker with forty licensees cannot personally review every document, and no one expects that. Delegation is normal, necessary, and expected in any brokerage of size. What a broker cannot do is delegate the responsibility.

The distinction is concrete. A broker who assigns file review to an experienced office manager has delegated a task. If a disclosure is missed, the broker is still answerable — and the questions asked will be about the supervision system: Was there a written procedure? Was the person trained? Was the delegation documented? Did the broker monitor whether the review was actually happening, or simply assume it was?

That last question is where most supervision failures live. A procedure that no one checks is not supervision; it is a document about supervision.

A worked example

A brokerage's policy requires broker approval of all advertising before publication. A salesperson posts a property video to social media without approval. The post is accurate, identifies the brokerage correctly, and generates two showings.

Is there a problem? Yes, and it is not about this post. The post's accuracy is luck, not control. If approval is optional in practice, the broker cannot demonstrate supervision of the office's advertising at all — and the next post may be the one that misstates square footage or omits the brokerage identity. A policy enforced only after harm occurs is not a supervision system.

What should happen now? The deviation is reported and reviewed, the process gap is addressed, and the review is documented. Quietly deleting the post would leave the gap in place for the next licensee.

2.3 Independent contractor status is not an exception

Many California salespersons work as independent contractors for tax and compensation purposes. That arrangement is common and lawful. It does not reduce the broker's supervisory obligation by one degree.

This confuses people because the two frameworks use similar words for different purposes. Tax law asks who controls the manner and means of the work in order to decide how income is reported. Real estate law asks who is responsible for supervising licensed activity in order to protect consumers. A licensee can be an independent contractor for the first purpose and a supervised licensee for the second at the same time, and routinely is.

A broker who responds to a compliance question with "they're an independent contractor" has misidentified the framework.

2.4 Branch offices and scale

As a brokerage grows, supervision must be designed rather than assumed. Branch offices, teams operating semi-autonomously, and licensees working remotely each create distance between the responsible broker and the work. The obligation does not shrink with distance; the systems have to grow to cover it.

Practical indicators that a supervision system is real:

  • New licensees receive documented onboarding on the office's procedures before they take a file.
  • Files are reviewed against a checklist at defined points, and the review is recorded.
  • Advertising has a named approver and an archive of what was published.
  • There is a stated escalation path, and licensees can name it without looking it up.
  • Exceptions are logged and resolved rather than handled informally.

2.5 Checkpoint

You should be able to explain why a broker remains answerable for a task properly delegated to a competent person, and why independent contractor status does not change the supervisory analysis.

3. Professional competence and scope

3.1 Competence is an obligation, not a credential

A real estate license is not divided by property type. The same license permits a licensee to work on a suburban house, a commercial ground lease, a probate sale, and a mobile home in a rented park. The license permits it. Competence is a separate question, and it is a professional obligation the licensee owes the client.

The obligation does not mean refusing unfamiliar work — that would leave every licensee permanently confined to their first transaction type. It means being honest with the client about what you do and do not know, and arranging real support before you take on work at the edge of your experience.

A defensible approach to unfamiliar work has three parts:

  1. Disclose plainly what you have and have not done before. Clients accept "I have not handled a ground lease assignment; my broker has, and she will be working the file with me" far better than they accept discovering it later.
  2. Arrange actual supervision or co-representation — not an offer to answer questions if you have any, which puts the burden of knowing what to ask on the person who does not know.
  3. Identify the specialists the transaction will need and involve them early.

What is not competence: relying on the other side's broker to catch what you miss. The opposing broker owes duties to their own client, not to yours.

3.2 The boundary with the practice of law

This boundary produces more discipline than almost any other issue in daily practice, and the line is not where most licensees assume.

Within a licensee's role:

  • Completing the blanks of a standard form agreement in the ordinary course of a transaction.
  • Explaining in general terms what a common provision — a financing contingency, an inspection period — is for and how it customarily operates.
  • Describing the ordinary sequence of a transaction and what typically happens at each stage.
  • Recommending that the client consult an attorney, and identifying the issue that prompts the recommendation.

Outside it:

  • Drafting a custom provision to address a particular party's legal risk.
  • Advising on the legal effect of an executed contract — whether a party is bound, whether a breach occurred, what remedies exist.
  • Advising whether a client may cancel, rescind, or refuse to perform.
  • Opining on how a boundary dispute, a title claim, or an estate matter would be resolved.
  • Explaining the legal consequences of how a buyer takes title.

The distinction is not about difficulty and not about the licensee's experience. A licensee with twenty years of practice is no more permitted to advise on the legal effect of a contract than one with two weeks. The distinction is about whose professional judgment the client is relying on.

Two situations that look similar and are not

Situation A. A buyer asks, "What does this inspection contingency do?" You explain that it gives him a defined period to investigate the property's condition and a path to respond within the contract's terms if what he finds is unacceptable. This is within your role.

Situation B. A buyer asks, "The inspection period ended yesterday and I didn't send anything. Am I still able to cancel?" This asks about his legal position under an executed contract. This is outside your role. The correct response identifies the issue, states plainly that the answer depends on the contract's mechanism and on legal questions you are not able to resolve, refers him to an attorney, and records the referral in the file.

Note what the correct response in Situation B does not do: it does not refuse to engage. Saying nothing leaves the client without direction at exactly the moment direction matters.

3.3 Other scope boundaries

The law is not the only boundary a licensee must respect.

Valuation. A licensee may prepare a comparative market analysis and give an opinion of value to advise a client on pricing. A licensee may not hold that analysis out as an appraisal, and may not perform an appraisal where one is required. The difference is not the data used; it is who prepares it, under what standards, and for what purpose.

Property condition. A licensee conducts a reasonably competent and diligent visual inspection of accessible areas and discloses material facts it reveals. A licensee does not perform an inspection, does not evaluate the adequacy of a repair, and does not opine on whether a structural condition is serious. The correct response to reaching that boundary is a referral to the appropriate professional and a record of it.

Tax and estate consequences. How a buyer takes title, what a sale will cost an owner in taxes, and whether a transfer triggers reassessment are questions with consequences a licensee cannot evaluate and should not attempt.

3.4 The value of "I don't know"

New licensees often feel that admitting ignorance undermines their credibility. In practice the opposite is true, and the professional formulation has four parts:

  1. Say plainly that you do not know.
  2. Say where the answer comes from.
  3. Say when you will have it.
  4. Follow up in writing — with the answer, or with a note that it remains unresolved.

A licensee who does this consistently becomes the person clients trust with hard questions. A licensee who improvises becomes the person whose file contains a misrepresentation.

3.5 Checkpoint

You should be able to classify a given question as within scope, legal advice, appraisal, inspection, or tax advice — and to state the correct response for each.

4. Office policies and delegation

4.1 What written policies are for

A brokerage's written policies serve two audiences. For licensees, they answer "how do we do this here" without requiring a conversation each time. For a regulator or a court, they are evidence that the brokerage designed a system rather than improvised.

A policy that exists only on paper serves neither audience. The test of a policy is not whether it is written but whether it controls conduct — whether licensees know it, follow it, and are noticed when they do not.

4.2 What a working policy set covers

At minimum:

  • Intake — what is collected and verified before a listing or representation begins, including authority to sell.
  • Disclosure handling — who prepares, who reviews, how delivery is evidenced.
  • Advertising — what must be included, who approves, where the archive lives.
  • Trust funds — who may receive funds, where they go, how quickly, who reconciles.
  • Recordkeeping and retention — what belongs in a file, where files live, how long they are kept.
  • Escalation — what must reach the broker immediately, and how.
  • Complaints — how a consumer complaint is received, recorded, and answered.
  • Departure — what happens to files, clients, and pending transactions when a licensee leaves.

That last one is neglected and expensive. Transaction records belong to the brokerage. A departing licensee cannot decide unilaterally what leaves with them, and a brokerage without a written answer will discover this during an argument.

4.3 Delegation done properly

Delegation is not simply assignment. A delegation that will survive review has four components:

  1. A named person — not a role that three people believe belongs to someone else.
  2. A defined scope — what the person is deciding, and what must come back up.
  3. Training — evidence the person was equipped for the task.
  4. Monitoring — a way the broker learns whether it is actually being done.

Missing the fourth is the most common failure, and it is the one that converts an ordinary error into a supervision finding.

4.4 Stricter than the law is a legitimate choice

Brokerages frequently adopt policies stricter than the legal minimum: longer retention, mandatory second review on certain transactions, prohibitions on practices that are lawful but risky. This is sound, and consistency is what makes it valuable.

It also creates a trap worth naming. When a licensee discovers that office policy exceeds the legal requirement, the temptation is to follow the law instead. That is the wrong move for two reasons. The policy binds the licensee's conduct within the brokerage regardless of the legal floor, and the licensee's reading of the legal minimum may simply be wrong. Changing a policy is a policy-level decision that starts with confirming the current requirement — not a decision an individual licensee makes case by case.

4.5 Reading a policy manual as a new licensee

Section 4.2 lists what a working policy set covers. The obligation running the other direction is rarely stated: the licensee has to read it, and reading it is a specific task rather than a gesture.

What to look for, in order:

  1. What must be submitted for review, and when. Advertising, agreements, disclosures, trust deposits. Anything requiring approval has a turnaround time, and knowing it changes how you plan a listing.
  2. The escalation path. Who you contact for a question, who you contact for a problem, and what the after-hours answer is. A licensee who does not know this before he needs it will improvise on the day he most needs not to.
  3. What the brokerage prohibits beyond what the law requires. Section 4.4 establishes that stricter is legitimate. A prohibition you did not know about is one you will violate.
  4. Trust fund handling — where funds go, how fast, and who is authorized. Never improvised, ever.
  5. The transaction file standard. What documents, in what form, by when.
  6. How supervision actually happens — at which points a file is reviewed, and what a reviewer looks at.
  7. Departure. What happens to your files, your pending transactions, and your clients if you leave. Read this on the day you join, not on the day you leave, because the terms are the same either way and your reading of them will not be.

Then the follow-up that most licensees skip: ask the broker the questions the manual does not answer. A manual is written against the transactions the brokerage has already seen; the situation you are in is often not one of them.

And the obligation that has no manual: where a written policy conflicts with what you are being told to do in practice, that is a question, not a discrepancy to resolve on your own. Section 2.2 establishes that delegation does not transfer responsibility; the corollary is that an informal practice does not amend a written policy, and a licensee following the informal version is exposed on both.

What a policy set is actually for. It is not primarily a compliance artifact. It is the accumulated answer to problems the brokerage has already had, which means every provision that seems excessive is a scar. Reading it as history rather than as rules makes it considerably more interesting and much easier to follow.

4.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to name the four components of a defensible delegation and explain why "we have a written policy" is not by itself evidence of supervision.

5. Consumer-first communication

5.1 Three categories a consumer must be able to tell apart

Most communication failures in real estate are not lies. They are collapses of category — a consumer who cannot tell which of three things they have just been told:

  1. A verified fact — something you know and can support.
  2. An unverified report — something you were told, attributed to its source.
  3. A professional opinion — your judgment, identified as judgment.

Consider three answers to "How old is the roof?"

  • "The permit record shows it was replaced in 2019." — verified fact, with its source.
  • "The seller says it was replaced about six years ago; I have not confirmed that." — unverified report, attributed and flagged.
  • "From the appearance and the material, it looks like a roof with some life left, but a roofer should evaluate it." — opinion, identified as opinion, with a referral.

Each is proper. What is improper is the fourth answer — "It's about six years old" — which conveys a fact while resting on an unverified report, and which becomes a misrepresentation the moment it turns out to be wrong.

5.2 Timeliness and the written record

Consumers make decisions on deadlines. Information that arrives after a decision is worth almost nothing, and a correction that arrives after closing is worth less than that.

Two practices carry most of the weight:

  • Confirm materially important conversations in writing. Not every exchange — a licensee who emails a summary of every phone call becomes noise. But when something was decided, when a deadline was discussed, when advice was given that the client may act on, the written confirmation is what makes it real.
  • Correct errors immediately, in writing, to everyone who received the original. The instinct to wait and see whether an error matters is the instinct that turns a mistake into a misrepresentation. An error you know about and leave uncorrected is no longer a mistake.

5.3 Plain language and access

A consumer who does not understand an explanation has not been informed, whatever the file says. Practical obligations follow:

  • Explain in plain language, then confirm understanding by asking what the client plans to do next rather than asking "does that make sense?"
  • Recognize when a consumer's primary language is not English and address it properly — through appropriate translation or interpretation, not by simplifying to the point of inaccuracy.
  • Accommodate disability-related needs in how information is delivered.
  • Never let time pressure become the substance of a recommendation. "You need to decide today" is sometimes a true statement about a deadline and sometimes a sales tactic; a consumer cannot tell the difference, so the licensee must be scrupulous about which one they are doing.

5.4 What consumer-first does not mean

It does not mean telling the consumer whatever they want to hear, and it does not mean substituting your judgment for theirs. A client who wants to list above the supported range is entitled to do so; the licensee's obligation is to give the analysis and the advice in a form the client can act on, then follow the client's informed decision and record that it was informed.

Nor does it mean answering every question asked. Some questions — about the characteristics of a neighborhood's residents, about a prior occupant's illness, about whether a neighbor has a legal claim — must be declined, and declining them well is itself a professional skill: explain why you cannot answer, and point to where a proper answer comes from.

5.5 The confirming message

Section 5.2 requires timeliness and a written record. The instrument that satisfies both, on almost every file, is one habit: after any consequential conversation, send a short message confirming what was said.

What it contains: the date, what the client asked or decided, what you told him, what happens next, and by when. Four or five sentences.

What it accomplishes, all at once:

  • It creates the record, contemporaneously and in a form the client received rather than one you wrote for yourself.
  • It catches the misunderstanding while it is small. A client who read the conversation differently replies immediately, which is the cheapest moment for that to happen.
  • It reduces the number of calls, because a client who has the summary in writing does not call to re-ask.
  • It communicates competence more effectively than any other single practice available to a new licensee.

What it must not do: state legal conclusions, characterize other parties, predict outcomes, or restate an assurance you should not have given verbally. Section 5.1's three categories govern the message as much as the conversation, and the message is the version that survives.

The version that fails is the long one, sent two days later, hedged into ambiguity. Short, same day, specific.

One extension worth adopting. Where a client instructs you to do something you advised against, the confirming message records both — the advice, and the instruction. Not as a defensive maneuver and not phrased as one: "As we discussed, my recommendation was X and you've decided on Y — I'll proceed with Y." That sentence protects the client's autonomy and the licensee's record in the same breath, and it is the sentence Lesson 04's pricing section and Lesson 07's investigation section both ultimately depend on.

5.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to take a consumer question and produce an answer that is correctly categorized as fact, report, or opinion, and that identifies its source.

6. Transaction file accountability

6.1 The file is the record of your professional conduct

Every part of this lesson converges here. Supervision is demonstrated through files. Competence and scope decisions are evidenced by documented referrals. Communication is proven by what was confirmed in writing. When a transaction is questioned — by a client, a broker, a regulator, or a court — the file is what answers.

The governing standard is reconstructability: could a person who was not there determine what happened, when, and on what basis?

6.2 What belongs in it

  • Agency disclosures and representation agreements, with dates.
  • The purchase agreement, every counteroffer, and every signed amendment.
  • All disclosures, with evidence of delivery and its date — not merely signatures.
  • Notices, contingency removals, and demands, with delivery evidence.
  • Material communications: advice given, decisions made, corrections issued.
  • Referrals made and the client's response.
  • Records of the licensee's own observations, including the visual inspection.
  • Advertising published for the property.
  • Escrow and closing documents.

6.3 Dates and delivery evidence

Two file defects account for a large share of disputes, and both concern timing rather than content.

A signature without a delivery date. The signature proves the consumer received the document. It does not prove when. Because rights and deadlines commonly run from delivery, a file that cannot establish the delivery date cannot establish that the consumer's response period was honored.

An undated notice. A contingency removal, a notice to perform, or a cancellation without a date is evidence of an event with no evidence of when it occurred — which is precisely the fact in dispute.

6.4 Version control, and why superseded drafts stay

The instinct when closing a file is to tidy it: discard the drafts, keep the executed versions. Resist it. The sequence of versions is frequently what shows when a term changed and who agreed to the change. A file containing only final documents can prove what was agreed but not how the parties got there.

Preserve the submitted version, the timestamp, the actor, and any later amendment, so that a correction does not erase the history it corrects.

6.5 The line that must never be crossed

A file gap discovered later is a problem. Filling it retroactively is a different category of problem entirely.

When an audit finds a missing document, the correct actions are: obtain it if it legitimately exists; record the gap if it does not; escalate to the broker. The incorrect actions — creating a document now and dating it to match the transaction, or removing surrounding records so the inconsistency does not show — are falsification. They convert a compliance failure that a brokerage can survive into conduct that ends careers.

The same principle governs correcting records generally. Corrections are made visibly, with the original preserved and the change attributable. A record that can be silently altered is not a record.

6.6 Post-closing audit

The most useful review of a file happens shortly after closing, while the participants still remember what happened. The audit asks:

  1. Is every required document present?
  2. Is each one dated, and is delivery evidenced where timing matters?
  3. Are the documents internally consistent — do names, dates, amounts, and property descriptions reconcile?
  4. Does the chronology make sense against the contract's deadlines?
  5. Is anything present that should not be — an unexecuted draft mistaken for final, a superseded figure?

Findings are recorded, not quietly repaired. A brokerage that can show it audits its own files and acts on what it finds is in a materially different position from one that cannot.

6.7 The first ninety days

A new licensee is unlikely to be asked to do anything in his first three months that this course has not covered. He is very likely to be asked to do all of it at once, on a file where he does not yet know which part matters. These are the practices to install before the first transaction rather than after.

Set up before the first client:

  1. A file structure, the same for every transaction, matching the brokerage's standard. Section 6.2 supplies the contents.
  2. A deadline tracker that is not your memory and not one calendar entry per transaction.
  3. A delivery log — what was sent, to whom, when, how, and with what confirmation. Section 6.3 explains why.
  4. An advertising archive, with captures as published.
  5. A contact and consent record, so that Lesson 02's channel rules can be answered from a record rather than a recollection.
  6. A place to put notes the same day, which is the habit that either forms in the first month or does not form.

Ask your broker, in the first week:

  • Which documents require review, and how long review takes.
  • Who I contact for a question, and who I contact for a problem.
  • What this brokerage prohibits that the law permits.
  • At what points a file is reviewed, and what the reviewer looks at.
  • What happens to a file if I am unavailable.

Decline, without exception, in the first ninety days and afterward:

  • Any question whose answer is legal, tax, structural, or valuation-professional. Route it. Section 3.4 makes this competence rather than weakness.
  • Any assurance about approval, appraisal, value, or outcome.
  • Any instruction you believe is unlawful, from any source, including a manager. Section 2.2 governs.

The one measure of whether the first ninety days went well is not the number of transactions. It is whether the files you opened can be read by someone else. A new licensee with two well-documented files is in a better professional position than one with six that depend on his recollection, and the difference is entirely front-loaded — it is decided by what was set up before the first client called.

6.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to state the reconstructability standard, explain why a signature without a delivery date is insufficient, and describe the correct response to a gap found in a closed file.

Key terms

These terms recur throughout the course. A student who can define each one in a sentence, and say why it matters, is prepared for the assignments that follow.

TermDefinition
Real Estate LawThe body of California statute governing real estate licensure and licensed conduct, enacted by the Legislature and found in the Business and Professions Code.
Commissioner's RegulationsAdministrative regulations adopted by the Real Estate Commissioner under statutory authority, supplying operational detail the statute leaves open.
Licensed activityConduct that requires a real estate license: acting for another, for compensation, in one of the activities the statute lists.
BrokerThe holder of an independent license, who may operate a brokerage, employ salespersons, hold trust funds, and contract with clients in the brokerage's name.
SalespersonThe holder of a derivative license, authorized to perform licensed activity only for and under the supervision of an employing broker.
Responsible brokerThe broker accountable for a brokerage's licensed activity, including supervision of everyone acting under the license.
Designated officerThe licensed broker through whom a corporate licensee acts, and who carries the supervisory responsibility personally.
SupervisionThe responsible broker's non-delegable obligation covering transactions, documents, advertising, trust funds, familiarity with the law, complaints, and records.
DelegationAssigning a supervisory task to another person. Transfers the work, never the responsibility.
Independent contractor statusA tax and compensation arrangement common for salespersons. It does not reduce the broker's supervisory obligation by any degree.
CompetenceThe professional obligation to perform work one is equipped to perform, or to obtain real supervision and disclose the limits honestly.
Unauthorized practice of lawPerforming services reserved to attorneys — drafting custom legal provisions, advising on the legal effect of executed documents, or opining on rights and remedies.
Comparative market analysisA licensee's opinion of value prepared to advise a client on pricing. Not an appraisal, and never to be held out as one.
Visual inspection dutyThe licensee's own obligation to conduct a reasonably competent and diligent visual inspection of accessible areas and disclose material facts it reveals.
Material factA fact that would affect a reasonable buyer's decision to buy or the price they would pay.
Verified factInformation the licensee knows and can support, stated with its source.
Unverified reportInformation the licensee was told, attributed to its source and flagged as unverified.
Professional opinionThe licensee's judgment, identified as judgment rather than as fact.
Trust fundsMoney belonging to others received in the course of licensed activity, which must be held separately and fully accounted for.
ComminglingMixing trust funds with the brokerage's own funds. Complete on deposit into the wrong account, regardless of internal tracking.
ReconstructabilityThe standard a transaction file must meet: could a person who was not there determine what happened, when, and on what basis?
Delivery evidenceProof of when a document reached a party — distinct from a signature, which proves only that it was received at some point.
EscalationRouting a matter to the responsible broker, or to counsel, rather than resolving it at the licensee's own level.
FalsificationCreating, backdating, altering, or removing a record so that the file shows something other than what happened. Distinct in kind from a documented gap.
Public license lookupThe Department's public record of license status, expiration, employing broker, and disciplinary history.

Practice problems

Work each problem before reading the solution. The reasoning matters more than the conclusion — in practice you will meet variations, and what transfers is the analysis rather than the answer.

Problem 1

A salesperson has worked at a brokerage for six years and has closed more transactions than anyone else in the office. She wants to begin taking listings under her own trade name, with her own signage, paying the brokerage a flat fee per transaction. Her broker is agreeable.

Is this permissible?

Solution. No. The salesperson license authorizes licensed activity only for and under the employing broker. Advertising under her own trade name presents her as an independent principal, which the identification requirements exist to prevent, and taking listings in her own name means contracting with clients in her own right, which a salesperson may not do.

The broker's agreement does not cure it. A broker cannot authorize a salesperson to do what the license does not permit, and the arrangement would leave the broker answerable for activity he has structurally given up the ability to supervise.

What transfers: experience never converts a derivative license into an independent one, and "my broker approved it" is not an analysis of what the license permits.

Problem 2

A licensee's client asks her to review a contract he signed last year with a contractor, tell him whether the contractor breached it, and advise whether he can withhold the final payment.

What may she do?

Solution. Very little of what was asked. Determining whether a party breached an executed contract and advising on withholding performance is advising on legal rights and remedies — the practice of law.

What she may do: acknowledge the question, explain plainly that it is outside what a real estate licensee may advise on, refer him to an attorney, and note the referral in the file.

What transfers: the boundary is not about the subject matter being real-estate-adjacent. It is about whether the answer would be a legal conclusion. Her twenty years of experience with contractors does not extend the license.

Problem 3

A broker delegates advertising approval to a licensed office manager, trains him, and documents the delegation. Eight months later a regulator finds that a quarter of the office's social posts carried no brokerage identification.

Where does responsibility sit, and what was missing?

Solution. Responsibility sits with the broker. Delegation transferred the task, not the obligation.

What was missing is the fourth component of a defensible delegation: monitoring. The broker had a named person, a defined scope, and training — and no way of learning whether approval was actually happening. A delegation nobody checks is a document about supervision rather than supervision.

What transfers: when a supervision failure is examined, the questions are about the system. Three of the four components present is still a failure.

Problem 4

A consumer calls about a listing and asks whether the property is on a septic system. The licensee does not know. The seller mentioned something about it at the listing appointment but the licensee did not write it down.

What should she say?

Solution. That she does not know, when she will confirm it, and from what source — then follow up in writing with the answer, or with a note that it remains unverified.

What she must not do is repeat her recollection of the seller's remark as a fact. That is an unverified report delivered as a verified one, and if it turns out to be wrong it is a misrepresentation regardless of her good faith.

What transfers: the four-part formulation — I don't know, here is where the answer comes from, here is when, and here it is in writing — is the professional response, and it builds rather than costs credibility.

Problem 5

An office policy requires transaction files to be submitted within five business days of closing. A licensee routinely submits at three weeks, and no one has said anything. She learns the law's retention requirement does not specify a submission deadline at all.

May she follow the law rather than the policy?

Solution. No. Office policy binds her conduct within the brokerage, and a brokerage may hold itself to a stricter standard than the legal floor. Consistency is what makes a policy defensible; individual licensees deciding which parts to follow is exactly what creates the gaps.

If the policy is wrong for the business, that is a policy-level conversation that starts with confirming the current requirement — not a decision made at a filing cabinet.

What transfers: "the law doesn't require this" is not a reason to depart from office policy. The two operate at different levels, and the stricter one governs conduct.

Problem 6

A licensee gives a buyer a square footage figure taken from the prior listing. It is wrong by 180 square feet. The buyer relied on it in setting his offer. Closing is in a week and the licensee realizes the error.

What must happen, and what is the risk if she waits?

Solution. Correct it in writing, immediately, to everyone who received the original — and notify her broker.

The risk of waiting is that the character of the problem changes. Right now it is an error made without reasonable care: a negligent misrepresentation, serious but survivable. An error she knows about and leaves uncorrected while the buyer proceeds becomes something worse, because she now has knowledge.

What transfers: copying a figure from a prior listing is not verification. It launders an unverified number into an apparently authoritative one, and it is one of the most common sources of claims against licensees.

Problem 7

A file contains a disclosure signed by the buyer. There is no date next to the signature and no record of when it was sent. The transaction closed without incident nine months ago.

Is there a problem, and what should be done now?

Solution. Yes. The signature proves the buyer received the disclosure; it proves nothing about when. Because rights and response periods run from delivery, the file cannot show that the buyer's period was honored.

What to do now: record the gap, escalate to the broker, and obtain any evidence that legitimately exists — a transmittal email, a delivery receipt, a platform audit trail.

What must not happen: writing a date on it now. A documented gap is a compliance problem the brokerage can survive. A date added after the fact is falsification.

What transfers: "the transaction closed without incident" describes the past, not the retention period. The dispute that tests this file has not happened yet.

Problem 8

A licensee is asked by a friend to help sell a house. There is no compensation discussed. He shows the property twice, advises on price, and helps the friend evaluate an offer.

Has he performed licensed activity, and does it matter that he was not paid?

Solution. The compensation element is genuinely in question — licensed activity generally requires compensation or the expectation of it, and "compensation" is read broadly to include things of value beyond cash.

But the more important point is that the analysis does not end there. He has been acting as an agent: advising on price, evaluating offers, exercising judgment his friend is relying on. Agency can arise from conduct, and the duties that come with it do not wait for a payment.

The professional answer is to run it through his broker rather than treating it as a favor outside the brokerage. Informal arrangements between friends are where the file that would have protected everyone does not exist.

What transfers: the absence of payment answers one question and leaves several open.

Chapter summary

The license exists because of an information asymmetry. Consumers cannot evaluate the quality of real estate advice until long after they have relied on it. Licensing responds by creating an identifiable, accountable person subject to standards and to a regulator. When a rule seems burdensome, ask what consumer harm it prevents; the answer usually produces the right conduct.

Two bodies of authority govern, and brokerage policy operates inside them. Statute and the Commissioner's Regulations both bind, they change on different schedules, and office policy may be stricter but never looser. Local custom authorizes nothing.

The salesperson license is derivative. Activity, compensation, advertising identity, contracting, and trust funds all run through the employing broker. Experience does not change this, and neither does the broker's agreement.

Supervision is a non-delegable obligation. The task can be assigned; the responsibility cannot. A defensible delegation has a named person, a defined scope, training, and monitoring — and the fourth is the one usually missing.

Competence is an obligation rather than a credential. Unfamiliar work is permitted with honest disclosure and real support. The boundary with legal advice is not about difficulty or experience; it is about whether the answer would be a legal conclusion.

Consumers must be able to tell three things apart: verified fact, unverified report, and professional opinion. Nearly every misrepresentation in residential practice is a collapse of those categories, and most are negligent rather than dishonest.

The file is the record of your professional conduct. The standard is reconstructability. Dates and delivery evidence matter as much as content, superseded versions stay, and corrections are made visibly.

One line separates a survivable problem from a career-ending one. A documented gap is a compliance failure. A manufactured document is falsification. Everything in this course is recoverable except that.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. Identify the issue, the category of controlling source, the facts that remain unverified, the parties affected, the action required, the applicable deadline, the communication you would send, the escalation if any, and the evidence you would retain. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Practice - Lesson Assignment 02

Building a lawful real estate practice

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Construct a business plan in which growth targets are tied to the compliance work each transaction actually requires.
  • Apply solicitation restrictions to calling, texting, emailing, and door-to-door prospecting, and identify what consent each requires.
  • Evaluate advertising for licensee and brokerage identification, including team names and social media.
  • Recognize price fixing, group boycotts, and market allocation, and state the correct response when a competitor raises them.
  • Apply access, transmission, and disposal controls to consumer personal information, and identify the wire fraud pattern.
  • Design a records workflow that routes complete documentation into the brokerage's system and survives a licensee's departure.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: business planning and solicitation55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: advertising and antitrust50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: information security and records45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Business planning

1.1 The problem with a volume target

Most new licensees write a business plan that begins and ends with a number: transactions per quarter, gross commission income for the year. That number is not a plan. It is a wish with arithmetic attached, and it creates a specific hazard.

A transaction consumes a fixed quantity of work that cannot be compressed without cutting something. Intake and verification of authority. Disclosure preparation and delivery. Deadline tracking. Written confirmations. File assembly. When a licensee sets a target that exceeds their capacity to do that work, the target is still met — by skipping steps. The steps that get skipped are invisible until something goes wrong, which is precisely why they are the ones that get skipped.

A professional plan therefore works backward from capacity rather than forward from ambition:

  1. Estimate the hours a transaction of your type actually requires, including the compliance work, not just the client-facing time.
  2. Determine how many hours you can genuinely give to transactions after prospecting, administration, and continuing education.
  3. Divide. That is your capacity.
  4. If capacity is below your income requirement, the honest responses are to change your market, your price point, your efficiency, or your support — not to plan on doing less work per file.

1.2 Choosing a brokerage as a compliance decision

New licensees usually evaluate brokerages on commission split and brand. Those matter. They are not the whole question, because the brokerage you join determines the supervision you receive, and supervision is what protects you in your first two years when you do not yet know what you do not know.

Questions worth asking before signing:

  • Who reviews my files, at what points, and what happens when something is missing?
  • What is the written policy set, and may I read it before I join?
  • What is the escalation path when I have a question I cannot answer?
  • Who approves advertising, and how long does approval take?
  • What training exists beyond the required licensing course?
  • What happens to my files and my pending transactions if I leave?

A brokerage that cannot answer these is telling you something about the supervision you would receive.

1.3 Budgeting for the work nobody bills for

A realistic plan carries line items that new licensees routinely omit:

ItemWhy it belongs in the plan
Errors and omissions coverageTerms and limits vary; the cost is real and the coverage is conditional on prompt notice
Continuing educationRequired for renewal, and the deadline arrives whether or not the business is good
Records storage and securityRetention obligations run for years past closing
Transaction management toolsThe deadline tracking that prevents most disputes
Time for correctionsEvery practice generates errors; the plan should assume some

1.4 What a defensible plan looks like

Rather than "twelve transactions this quarter," a defensible plan reads: twelve transactions this quarter requires roughly N client hours and M compliance hours; that is X hours per week against my available Y; the gap is closed by adding transaction coordination support at Z cost, which the plan funds from the projected income.

That version can be evaluated, adjusted, and defended. The number by itself cannot.

1.5 The revenue-timing problem

Capacity planning answers how much work you can do. It does not answer the question that ends most new practices, which is when the money arrives.

A transaction pays at closing. The work, the expenses, and the obligations begin long before that, and a purchase that opens in one month and closes two months later has consumed twelve weeks of a licensee's capacity before producing a dollar. A plan built on annual income against annual expenses will balance on paper and fail in month five, because the expenses are monthly and the income is lumpy.

The honest version of the plan carries three additional lines:

LineThe question it answers
Months of fixed costs held in reserveHow long can the practice operate before the first closing pays?
Expected lag from first client contact to first closingMeasured for your market and your price point, not assumed
What happens if the first two transactions fall outWhich they may, for reasons having nothing to do with your work

The failure this prevents is not financial. It is ethical. A licensee who is out of money in month five is a licensee under pressure to close a transaction that should not close, to skip a disclosure conversation that would slow things down, or to accept a client whose situation he is not equipped to handle. Every enforcement matter involving a new licensee has a version of this pressure somewhere behind it.

Illustrative only — the figures are yours to determine: a licensee who calculates fixed monthly costs of a given amount, an expected lag of ninety to one hundred twenty days from first contact to first closing, and a reserve covering three months, can see immediately that the reserve is short by the length of one transaction cycle. That is a solvable problem in month zero and an unsolvable one in month four.

The plan is revisited quarterly, against what actually happened rather than what was projected. A plan that is never compared to results is a document, not a plan — and the comparison is most valuable in the quarters where the projection was wrong.

1.6 A worked plan

Facts. A newly licensed salesperson intends to work resale residential in a market where the median price supports a typical side commission of roughly nine thousand dollars at his brokerage's split. He wants sixty thousand dollars in his first year. He writes: "Ten transactions. Eight hundred contacts. Two open houses a week."

What the plan does not say.

Capacity. He has not estimated the hours a transaction takes. Working backward from Section 1.1: a resale purchase in his market consumes client-facing time — showings, negotiation, walkthrough — plus compliance time that does not vary with the price of the house. Disclosure preparation, delivery and receipt tracking, deadline management, written confirmations, and file assembly are the same work on a modest condominium as on an estate. The compliance hours are the ones his plan has silently valued at zero.

Sequence. Ten transactions in twelve months is not ten transactions spread evenly. Buyers cluster, escrows overlap, and two files closing in the same week consume the compliance time of both at once. A plan that averages is a plan that has not asked what the worst week looks like.

Cash. Eight hundred contacts produce a first client at some point, and that client closes some weeks after that. Section 1.5 asks the question this plan does not: what does he live on until then, and what does he do if the first two files fall out?

Support. He has assumed he does everything himself. That is a decision, not a default, and it is the decision that determines whether the tenth transaction is done to the same standard as the first.

The rewritten plan.

Ten sides at roughly nine thousand net is ninety thousand gross against a sixty-thousand income requirement, leaving thirty thousand for costs. Each transaction requires an estimated client-facing block plus a compliance block; ten transactions therefore consume a total that, spread across my available weekly hours, leaves a specific number of prospecting hours. Two open houses weekly plus follow-up consumes most of them. The gap closes by engaging transaction coordination at a per-file cost, funded from the thirty thousand, which also carries errors and omissions coverage, continuing education, records storage, and a correction allowance. I hold three months of fixed costs in reserve against an expected ninety-to-one-hundred-twenty-day lag to first closing. I will compare this to actuals at the end of each quarter.

The difference between the two versions is not optimism. It is that the second one can be wrong in a way that is visible in time to fix, and the first one can only be wrong in a way that is discovered in month nine.

2. Prospecting and solicitation

2.1 Prospecting is lawful; solicitation is regulated

Building a client base by reaching out to people is a normal and necessary part of the business. The regulation sits on how you reach out, and the rules come from several directions at once: telephone solicitation restrictions, requirements for identifying yourself, consent requirements for text and email, and the general prohibition on misleading representations.

A licensee who thinks of this as one topic — "am I allowed to contact this person?" — will get it wrong, because the answer differs by channel.

2.2 Telephone contact

Two obligations dominate.

Do-not-call restrictions. Registries exist and must be respected. A licensee cannot rely on a lead vendor's assurance that a list is clean; the obligation is the caller's. Established business relationships and inquiries create defined, limited exceptions — and their limits matter, because an exception that has lapsed is not an exception.

Identification. A caller must identify who they are and on whose behalf they are calling. A licensee who opens with a question rather than an identification has already created a problem.

Expired listings and unrepresented sellers

Contacting owners whose listings recently expired, or who are selling without representation, is a standard prospecting approach and is not prohibited as such. What the licensee must confirm first is whether the number may lawfully be called, and what identification the call requires. "The listing expired, so they are fair game" is not an analysis of either question.

2.3 Text and email

Text messaging carries consent requirements that are stricter than most licensees assume, and the consent is defined by what the consumer was actually told when the information was collected — not by the fact that a number was written down.

This is where open house sign-in sheets become a compliance question rather than a marketing one. If a visitor provides a phone number on a sheet that says nothing about future contact, that number has not been consented for a marketing text campaign. If the sheet says plainly what the visitor is agreeing to receive, it has.

Two further points:

  • An opt-out mechanism is necessary but not sufficient. Including "reply STOP" does not create the consent required to send the first message.
  • Commercial email carries its own requirements — accurate headers and subject lines, identification, a functioning opt-out, and honoring opt-outs promptly.

2.4 In-person and door-to-door

Local ordinances may regulate door-to-door solicitation, and some communities require permits. Common-interest developments frequently prohibit solicitation on the property entirely. A licensee working a neighborhood should know which rules apply before knocking, and should never represent that they are calling on behalf of a neighbor, an association, or any party who has not authorized it.

2.5 The representation that must not be made

Across every channel, one category of statement is uniformly prohibited: a representation that creates a false impression of urgency, endorsement, or authority. "The neighbors asked me to reach out." "Values in this area are about to drop." "I have a buyer for your house" — when no such buyer exists. Each is a misrepresentation, and prospecting pressure is not a defense.

2.6 The record that makes a contact defensible

Every rule in this section is proved or disproved by one thing: what the file can show about where the contact came from and what the consumer agreed to.

A licensee who cannot answer "why was this person contacted, and on what basis" is in the same position regardless of whether the contact was lawful. The complaint is answered by the record or it is not answered.

What a defensible contact record contains:

  1. The source of the contact information — the specific open house, the specific inquiry, the specific referral, with its date. "Purchased list" is a source, and it is the one that carries the licensee's own obligation to verify.
  2. What the person was told when the information was collected — the actual language of the sign-in sheet or the web form, retained as it appeared at that time.
  3. The registry check, where the channel requires one, with its date.
  4. The exception relied on, if any, and the date the relationship or inquiry that created it began — because an exception has a duration, and a lapsed exception is not an exception.
  5. Every opt-out received, with the date and the channel, honored across all channels rather than only the one it arrived through.

The sign-in sheet deserves a note of its own. The version in the file must be the version the visitor actually signed. A brokerage that revises its sheet language and retains only the current version has destroyed the evidence for every contact collected under the prior wording — and the prior wording is precisely what a complaint about an older contact will turn on.

The practical test: hand your contact records to someone who was not present and ask them to determine, for any single contact, whether it was permissible. If they cannot, neither can a regulator, and the burden of the ambiguity does not fall on the consumer.

2.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to state, for a phone call, a text, and an email, what makes the contact permissible and where the consent or exception comes from.

3. Advertising identification

3.1 Why identification is required

A consumer looking at an advertisement needs to know two things that the property description does not tell them: who is responsible for this, and are they licensed. Identification requirements exist so a consumer can answer both, and so the supervising brokerage can be held accountable for what goes out under its name.

That purpose explains the shape of the rules. California requires a licensee's identification — including license identification — to appear on solicitation materials, and requires that advertising identify the brokerage under which the licensee operates. A salesperson may not advertise in a way that presents them as an independent principal.

3.2 The most common defects

DefectWhy it fails
Personal brand and mobile number, no brokerage namedThe consumer cannot tell there is a supervising brokerage, or which one
License identification omitted from solicitation materialThe consumer cannot verify licensure
Team name displayed more prominently than the brokerage, styled as a separate firmCreates the impression of an independent company that is not licensed
Social media property posts treated as personal expressionPromoting listings is advertising regardless of the platform
Photographs no longer showing the property's conditionTruthfulness is measured against the property now, not when the photo was taken
Photographs of a different, nicer unitMisrepresents the unit actually offered, however similar the units are

3.3 Team names

Teams are ordinary and useful. The compliance question is whether the team's presentation misleads a consumer about who the responsible brokerage is. A team identity that appears to be a separate company obscures accountability just as effectively as omitting the brokerage entirely, and the internal organizational chart is not the test — the impression created for a consumer is.

Practical guidance: the brokerage's identity should be present and legible wherever the team's identity appears, and the team's name should not use words suggesting it is itself a brokerage or an independent entity.

3.4 Approval and archive

Two office controls make advertising compliance real rather than aspirational.

Pre-publication approval. A named approver, a defined turnaround, and no exceptions. If approval is optional in practice, the brokerage cannot demonstrate control of its own advertising.

An archive. What ran, when, on what platform, with what approval. An advertisement that cannot be produced cannot be reviewed — and a consumer complaint about an advertisement from eight months ago is exactly when the archive earns its cost.

3.5 Truthfulness beyond identification

Identification is necessary and not sufficient. An advertisement must also be accurate about the property, and accuracy is measured at the time it runs. Square footage should trace to a source and the source should be stated. Features described must exist. Photographs must represent the property as it is. "Priced below market" is an opinion presented as fact unless it can be supported.

3.6 Advertising where advertising actually happens

The identification requirements were written for a world of signs, print, and mailers, and they apply without modification to every place a licensee now actually advertises. The difficulty is not that the rules are unclear. It is that these channels do not feel like advertising while you are using them.

ChannelWhere licensees go wrong
Social profileThe profile itself is advertising. A bio naming the licensee and the service without the required identification is a defect on every post it appears above.
Short videoIdentification burned into a caption that the platform crops, or spoken so quickly it is not conveyed. Presence is not the standard; conveyance is.
Direct messagesFeel like conversation, function as solicitation, and carry the same identification and consent obligations as any other channel.
Review and referral sitesProfiles written by the platform from scraped data still advertise the licensee's services. The obligation is the licensee's, not the platform's.
Syndicated listingsContent is reformatted by systems the licensee does not control, and identification can be stripped in the process.
Paid targetingRestricting who sees a housing advertisement is a fair housing question before it is a marketing one.

Two habits close most of these gaps.

First, the archive. Capture what was actually published, as published, with its date — a screenshot of the post as it rendered, not the draft that was submitted. Platforms edit, crop, expire, and delete, and a licensee whose only record is the text he wrote cannot show what the consumer saw.

Second, the review point. Advertising review that happens only for print and signage will never see the channels that produce the volume. If approval is required, it is required for the medium where most of the advertising occurs, and a policy that exempts social media in practice has exempted advertising in practice.

A licensee who would not put an unidentified sign in a yard should not put an unidentified video on a platform. The consumer's confusion is identical and so is the rule.

3.7 A worked advertising review

Submitted for approval: a thirty-second video for a social platform. On screen: exterior and interior footage of a listing, the licensee on camera for the last eight seconds. Overlaid text reads "JUST LISTED — 3BR — $749,000 — DM me!" The caption below the video reads: "Another one! My clients LOVE this neighborhood — best schools in the area and values here are only going up. Serving the whole county. Call or text me anytime — Jordan." The licensee's profile bio reads "Jordan — Real Estate — Let's find your home."

The findings, in order of seriousness.

1. No license identification anywhere. Not in the video, not in the overlay, not in the caption, not in the bio. The video, the caption, and the profile are each advertising, and none of them identifies the licensee as a licensee or names the brokerage. This is the defect that appears in every channel in Section 3.6 and it is present here three times.

2. "Best schools in the area." A statement about the composition and quality of a neighborhood, attached to a housing advertisement. This is a fair housing problem before it is an accuracy problem — school-quality claims are among the most common ways an advertisement conveys a preference about who a neighborhood is for. It comes out, and if the client's interest is in schools, the licensee directs the client to the district's own published information.

3. "Values here are only going up." A prediction stated as a fact, in an advertisement, about the thing the consumer is being invited to spend money on. It is not defensible in any market, and it is the sentence a complaint quotes.

4. "Serving the whole county." Only if true. A claim about service area is a representation, and one that is aspirational today is a misrepresentation today.

5. "DM me" and "text me anytime." An invitation to contact is not consent to be contacted. The messages the licensee sends after a consumer responds are governed by Section 2.3, and an inbound direct message does not create standing consent for a marketing campaign.

6. No archive. Nothing in the submission captures how the post will render — where the platform crops the overlay, whether the caption truncates behind a "more" link, and whether the identification, once added, survives either. The archive requirement in Section 3.6 is what makes the approval meaningful, because approval of a draft is not evidence about the publication.

The corrected version carries the required identification in the caption's first line where truncation cannot hide it and in the profile bio, drops the school claim and the value prediction entirely, states the service area accurately, and is archived as published with its date. It is not a worse advertisement. The two removed sentences were the two that could not be defended.

4. Antitrust and competition

4.1 Why this is the most serious topic in the lesson

Most compliance failures in real estate produce a fine, a correction, or discipline. Antitrust violations can produce criminal exposure and very large civil liability, and they attach to conduct that feels social rather than commercial — a conversation at a lunch, a comment at an association meeting, a thread in a group chat.

The core principle is short: each brokerage sets its own prices and chooses its own business partners independently. Anything that looks like coordination among competitors on those decisions is dangerous.

4.2 The four patterns to recognize

  1. Price fixing — an agreement among competitors on what to charge, on a minimum, on a "customary" rate that everyone will observe, or even on future pricing intentions. Commission rates are negotiable between a brokerage and its client, and always have been. A licensee who tells a seller that a rate is "standard," "set by the MLS," or "what everyone charges" has both misstated the law and edged toward the violation.
  1. Group boycotts — an agreement among competitors to refuse to deal with a particular brokerage, typically one competing on price or on a different model. "We shouldn't show their listings" is the sentence to recognize.
  1. Market allocation — an agreement among competitors to divide territories, property types, or customer segments so as not to compete for them.
  1. Tying — conditioning the availability of one service on the customer's purchase of another.

4.3 Agreements do not require documents

The single most dangerous misconception is that an agreement requires something signed, or explicit. It does not. Agreement can be inferred from conduct, from parallel behavior following a discussion, and from the exchange of information about future pricing intentions.

This is why simply listening is a risk. If competitors at a table discuss coordinating rates and you stay, your presence can be treated as participation.

The required response

Leave the conversation immediately and visibly. Say why. Report it to your broker.

Not "change the subject." Not "stay and say nothing." Leaving is what creates the record that you did not participate, and reporting it is what protects both you and the brokerage.

4.4 What is lawful

The rules do not prohibit competition or discussion of the industry. A brokerage may set whatever rates it chooses, may change them, may advertise them, and may decide independently which brokerages it prefers to work with and why. Licensees may discuss market conditions, inventory, and general practice. The line is coordination with competitors on price or on refusing to deal.

4.5 What to tell a seller about compensation

When a seller asks why the compensation is stated as a negotiated figure:

"Compensation is negotiable between you and our brokerage. There is no standard rate and no rate set by any association or listing service. Here is what our brokerage charges and what it covers."

That answer is accurate, it is the law, and it forecloses the follow-up question that a wrong answer invites.

4.6 Leaving a conversation that has turned
