Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Property and client intake

1.1 What intake is for

Intake is not a formality performed before the real work begins. It establishes three things without which nothing that follows can be relied on:

  1. Authority — who has the legal power to sell this property.
  2. Subject matter — what exactly is being sold, including its boundaries and what conveys with it.
  3. Constraints — what encumbrances, conditions, obligations, or third-party rights will affect the transaction.

A listing that is beautifully marketed and priced correctly still fails if it was signed by someone without authority to convey, or if it describes property whose boundaries were never checked. Intake failures are expensive precisely because they surface late — usually in escrow, when the cost of unwinding is highest.

1.2 Establishing authority to sell

The single most common intake failure is assuming that the person in the house is the person who can sell it. Ownership and occupancy are different facts, and so are ownership and authority.

Ownership situationWho must sign, and what to verify
Sole ownerThe owner, verified against title and identification
Married coupleBoth spouses where the property is community property or both are on title; marital status is a transaction fact, not a personal question
Co-owners (tenancy in common or joint tenancy)All owners whose interests must be conveyed
Property held in trustThe trustee, acting within the authority the trust document grants — confirm rather than assume
Entity ownership (LLC, corporation, partnership)The person authorized under the entity's governing documents, whose authority must be verified
Estate or probateAuthority derives from court appointment; confirm what the appointment permits and whether court confirmation of a sale is required
Power of attorneyThe attorney-in-fact, under a power that is currently valid and that actually authorizes real property conveyance
Divorcing co-ownersBoth, or a court order — a listing from one co-owner cannot deliver the whole

Three practical rules follow:

  • Verify against a source, not against a statement. A preliminary title report early in the listing process is inexpensive relative to what it prevents.
  • The tax roll is not evidence of authority. It reflects who is billed, which is often but not always the owner.
  • When authority is unclear, stop and involve the broker or counsel. Postponing the question to escrow guarantees the conflict surfaces at the worst possible moment.

1.3 Establishing what is being sold

  • The legal description, not the street address, defines the property. Obtain it and confirm it matches what the seller believes they own.
  • Boundaries. Fences, hedges, and long-standing use frequently do not match the recorded description. Where the seller intends to represent lot size or where a buyer will build near a line, a survey is the answer and the licensee's role is to recommend it.
  • What conveys. Fixtures versus personal property is a genuine source of post-closing disputes. Walk the property and name the items that could reasonably be argued either way — chandeliers, mounted televisions, appliances, window coverings, outbuildings, above-ground installations — and address each by name in the agreement.
  • Included and excluded items should be listed, not assumed. The purpose of naming them is to make the fixture tests unnecessary.

1.4 Establishing constraints

Intake should surface, at minimum:

  • Existing loans, liens, and whether any are in default.
  • Homeowners association membership, governing documents, assessments, and any pending litigation or special assessment.
  • Leases or occupancy by anyone other than the owner, including month-to-month arrangements.
  • Easements, encroachments, and shared facilities such as driveways or wells.
  • Known property conditions, prior repairs, insurance claims, and permit history — especially unpermitted work.
  • Any pending legal matter affecting the property or the owner's ability to convey.

Unpermitted work deserves its own attention

Unpermitted additions and conversions are common and consequential. They can affect value, insurability, financing, and the buyer's ability to use the space, and they are a disclosure item. Discovering one at intake gives the seller options — obtain permits, price accordingly, disclose clearly. Discovering it during the buyer's investigation gives everyone a problem.

1.5 The intake conversation itself

Intake is also where the professional relationship is set. Two habits pay for themselves:

  • Ask open questions and listen for what is not said. "Is there anything about the property that has surprised you since you bought it?" surfaces more than a checklist question about defects.
  • Record what you were told, and by whom. Seller-provided information is unverified report, and the file should show that you treated it as such.

1.6 A worked intake

The appointment. A licensee arrives at a single-family home. The person who called is Diane. During the first twenty minutes the following emerges:

  • The home was her mother's. Her mother died fourteen months ago. Diane believes the house "is hers now."
  • She has a brother who lives out of state. She says he "doesn't care about the house."
  • The garage was converted to a bedroom "years ago, by my dad."
  • There is a shared driveway with the neighbor. "We've always just worked it out."
  • She wants to list next week because she has already put a deposit on a condominium.
  • Tenants have been living in the converted bedroom since March, paying cash, with no written lease.

What the intake has to establish, in order.

1. Authority. "It's hers now" is a belief, not a fact the file can rely on. The property may have passed by a trust, by a will requiring administration, by intestate succession, or by an instrument that has not been recorded. Fourteen months and a brother are the two facts that make this urgent — if title stands in more than one name, one owner cannot list alone. The listing does not proceed on Diane's characterization. The licensee asks how title is currently held and asks to see what document she has. Where the answer is unclear, this is a legal question and it goes to counsel, not to the licensee's best guess.

2. What is being sold. The converted garage is now the central question of the file. Was it permitted? Does the county record it as living area? A licensee who advertises the bedroom count without knowing has made a representation he cannot support, and square footage is exactly the number Section 3.6 of Lesson 03 warns about. The answer comes from the records, not from Diane's recollection of her father's work.

3. Constraints. The shared driveway is either a recorded easement, an unrecorded arrangement, or a boundary the parties have been wrong about for thirty years. All three are disclosable and the third is a dispute. "We've always just worked it out" describes a relationship, not a right, and it does not transfer to a buyer.

4. Occupancy. There are tenants. Their rights do not disappear because the tenancy is oral and paid in cash, and Section 5.5 governs access to their space. Their existence changes the listing, the showing plan, and the disclosures.

5. The seller's timeline. Diane has committed money to a purchase before establishing she can sell. This is the fact that will apply pressure to every decision in the transaction, and naming it now is a service to her.

What the licensee does not do: take the listing today and sort it out later. Every one of these five is easier to resolve before the property is on the market and nearly impossible to resolve once a buyer is in contract and Diane's condominium deposit is at risk.

What the licensee does: write it all down, tell Diane plainly which items must be answered before listing and which can run in parallel, identify the two that require counsel, and set a date to reconvene. An intake that ends without a listing is not a failed appointment. It is the appointment doing its job.

2. Listing agreement structure

2.1 What the agreement actually does

A listing agreement is a contract between the seller and the brokerage — not the individual licensee — that engages the brokerage to market the property and sets the terms of the engagement. Reading it as a form to be completed rather than as a contract to be understood is how licensees end up unable to answer a seller's question about their own agreement.

2.2 The operative provisions

ProvisionWhat it doesWhat a change to it means
PartiesIdentifies the seller(s) and the brokerageMust match everyone whose interest is being conveyed
PropertyLegal description and address, with included and excluded itemsAmbiguity here becomes a dispute at closing
Price and termsThe seller's asking price and termsThe seller's decision; the licensee advises
TermBeginning and definite ending dateA definite end protects the seller from an indefinite engagement
CompensationAmount and when it is earned and payableNegotiable between seller and brokerage; never "standard"
Cooperation and compensation to other brokeragesWhether and how the brokerage shares with a cooperating brokerageAffects how the property is marketed to other brokerages
Authority grantedMarketing, signage, lockbox, listing service submission, photographyEach authorization is separate; a seller may withhold some
Seller representationsWhat the seller states about ownership, condition, and encumbrancesThe seller is making statements the brokerage relies on
Protection or safety clauseCompensation where a buyer produced during the term buys shortly after it endsTypically depends on the buyer having been identified to the seller
Dispute resolutionMediation and arbitration provisionsSubstantive rights; must be explained, not initialed past
Agency disclosure confirmationRecords the relationshipMust follow the disclosure, not precede it

2.3 Why the term must be definite

An engagement without a defined end binds the seller indefinitely. A definite termination date is what allows a seller to reassess, and it is why a listing does not simply continue by inertia.

Note what the term does not do: it does not eliminate the seller's ability to seek cancellation before expiry. Expiration and cancellation are different mechanisms, addressed in section 6.

2.4 The compensation conversation

When a seller asks about compensation, the accurate answer is short and must be given without hedging:

"Compensation is negotiable between you and our brokerage. There is no standard rate, and no rate set by any association or listing service. Here is what our brokerage charges, and here is what it covers."

Describing a rate as standard, customary, or set by a listing service is both factually wrong and an antitrust exposure. It is worth being precise about this even when the seller is not pressing, because a casual "everyone charges about this" is the sentence that later gets quoted.

2.5 The protection clause, explained properly

Sellers frequently misunderstand this provision and react badly to a poor explanation. The purpose is straightforward: if the brokerage introduces a buyer to the property during the listing term, and that buyer purchases shortly after the listing ends, the brokerage's work produced the sale. The clause addresses compensation in that situation.

Its ordinary limits matter and should be explained:

  • It typically applies only to buyers identified to the seller, usually in writing, within a defined period after the term ends.
  • It is generally not an automatic claim on any sale in the following period.
  • It commonly does not apply where the seller lists with another brokerage after expiration.

A seller who understands it accepts it. A seller who is told "it's standard, just initial here" is the seller who disputes it later.

2.6 Changing a term after the agreement is signed

Listings get amended. A price changes, a term is extended, a compensation arrangement is restructured, a property is withdrawn and returned. Each of those is a change to a contract between the seller and the brokerage, and the discipline is the same one that governs the original.

In writing, signed, dated, and retained — with the prior version kept.

The changeWhat it touches
PriceThe marketing, the listing service record, and the seller's net analysis. The reduction is documented as the seller's decision, on the same basis as the original price in Section 3.5.
Term extensionThe expiration date, and therefore what Section 6 does at the end. An extension agreed verbally leaves two parties with different beliefs about when the engagement ends.
CompensationA term the brokerage is party to. Not a licensee-level change.
ScopeWhat is included in the sale — a change here changes what is being advertised.
Withdrawal and returnListing status in the service, which Section 6.4 notes must be accurate.

Two failures recur.

The verbal amendment. A seller says "go ahead and drop it to 699" in a phone call, the licensee changes the listing service, and no document is created. The marketing now reflects a price the file cannot show the seller authorized. The correction is a confirming email the same day at minimum, and a signed amendment as soon as practical.

The overwritten record. A change made by editing the original agreement, or by replacing the prior listing service entry, destroys the evidence of what the terms were before. Prior versions are retained in sequence, because the question a later dispute asks is almost always "when did this change, and who agreed?"

The test for whether a change needs a document: would a person reading only the file know that this term is now different, when it changed, and that the seller agreed? If not, the change is undocumented regardless of how clearly both parties understood it at the time.

3. Pricing discussion

3.1 Whose decision, and whose obligation

The list price is the seller's decision. The analysis and the advice are the licensee's obligation. Both halves matter, and licensees fail in both directions: some refuse listings priced above their recommendation, overstating their role; others take any price without comment, abandoning the advisory duty.

The professional position is: give the analysis, give the advice, in writing, and then follow the seller's informed decision — with the file showing it was informed.

3.2 Building the recommendation

A defensible pricing recommendation rests on:

  1. Closed sales — evidence of what buyers actually paid, adjusted for differences from the subject.
  2. Pending sales — where the market is moving now, since a price has been agreed but is not yet in the closed data.
  3. Active listings — the competition, and evidence of what sellers are asking, which is not evidence of value.
  4. Adjustments — made to the comparables to make them resemble the subject, in the direction that answers "what would this comparable have sold for if it were like the subject?"
  5. Market conditions — a time adjustment where values have moved between the comparable sale date and today.

3.3 The conversation that actually happens

Seller: "Another brokerage said it's worth $1.3 million. You're saying $1.15."

Weak response: "They're just buying the listing." — attacks the competitor, gives the seller nothing to evaluate.

Weak response: "I can do $1.3." — abandons the analysis to win the listing.

Professional response: "Let's compare what each analysis rests on. Theirs uses three homes currently listed, two of which have been on the market over ninety days without an offer — those are asking prices, which tell us what sellers hope for. Mine uses four closed sales in the last four months, adjusted for your smaller lot and the original systems. Here are the adjustments and why. You set the price; I want you to set it knowing what each number is built on."

That version can be evaluated by the seller, and it can be defended later.

3.4 What a seller net sheet does

Sellers ask about price; what they actually want to know is proceeds. An estimated seller net sheet answers the real question by showing anticipated costs and payoffs against the sale price.

Three requirements:

  • Itemize. A range without components invites the seller to distrust all of it.
  • Label it an estimate. The final figures are set at closing.
  • Update it when the price, the terms, or the payoff changes materially.

3.5 Documenting the pricing decision

Where the seller chooses a price above the supported range, the file should contain:

  1. The analysis, with the data used and the adjustments made, dated.
  2. The written advice about the likely consequences of pricing above the market — longer time on market, the risk of a stale listing, appraisal risk if a buyer needs financing.
  3. The seller's decision.

What should not be in the file: the seller's reasoning adopted as the brokerage's own opinion of value. That misattributes whose opinion it is.

3.6 When the seller's price is above your recommendation

This is the most common disagreement in listing practice, and it has a correct handling that is neither capitulation nor refusal.

What the licensee owes: an honest recommendation, the data behind it, and a clear statement of the consequences of pricing above it. What the seller owns: the decision.

The consequences, stated plainly and in advance:

  1. The first two weeks are the property's best exposure, and an overpriced property spends them being seen and passed over by exactly the buyers most ready to act.
  2. Days on market accumulate and are visible. A property that reduces after eight weeks is negotiated against differently than a property priced correctly at the start, because the market now has information about the seller.
  3. A reduction later usually lands below where the original recommendation was, which is the outcome the seller was trying to avoid.
  4. Appraisal is a separate gate. A buyer willing to pay a number does not end the question where financing is involved.

Then the licensee documents the conversation — Section 3.5's requirement — and, if the seller proceeds above the recommendation, prices it there and works it professionally. Pricing at the seller's number and then marketing it half-heartedly is a breach of the duty of reasonable care, and it is a common quiet response to being overruled.

Two things the licensee should not do.

Do not adopt the seller's number as your own opinion. If asked later — by the seller, by a buyer's agent, by a reviewer — the honest answer is that the recommendation was one number and the seller chose another, which is entirely his right. A licensee who has stated the seller's price as his own professional opinion has made a representation he cannot support.

Do not agree to a price you believe is unsupportable in order to win the listing, planning to "get a reduction in three weeks." That is a plan built on telling the seller something now and something different later, and Section 3.3 already named it: the strategy of buying a listing is the strategy of misleading a client at the outset.

A useful framing for the conversation: "I'll list it at your number and work it hard. Here is what I expect to see in the first two weeks, and here is the point at which I'll come back and ask you to look at it again. Let's agree now on what we'll do if that happens." The seller has made an informed decision and a review date exists, which is what turns a disagreement into a plan.

4. Seller disclosures

4.1 The line that is not negotiable

At a listing appointment a seller may want many things: a high price, weekend-only showings, a listing they can cancel at will, and to defer the disclosure paperwork until an offer arrives. Three of those four are negotiable business terms. The fourth is not.

Disclosure obligations attach to the transaction and cannot be scheduled around. Deferring them until an offer arrives compresses the buyer's review period, creates pressure at exactly the wrong moment, and puts both the seller and the brokerage at risk.

The professional move is to separate the two categories openly at the appointment rather than to postpone the conversation:

"Your price, your showing schedule, and how the listing can be ended are all things we set together — tell me what you want. The disclosure work is different: it runs on its own schedule because the buyer's rights depend on when it is delivered. Let's start it now, while there's no deadline pressure."

4.2 What the seller discloses

The seller's disclosure obligation covers known material facts affecting the value or desirability of the property. The California residential framework includes a transfer disclosure completed by the seller, natural hazard disclosure, and other disclosures depending on the property, its age, and its location.

Three principles the licensee must be able to explain:

  1. Materiality is measured by effect on a reasonable buyer's decision — not by the seller's opinion of importance, not by whether it is currently causing a problem, and not by how often it appears.
  2. A repaired condition is still disclosed. The adequacy of the repair is exactly what a buyer wants to evaluate.
  3. Obviousness does not excuse disclosure. "They could see it themselves" is the assumption that produces post-closing disputes.

4.3 Counseling a reluctant seller

Sellers resist disclosure for predictable reasons, and each has a professional answer:

Seller's positionThe counseling response
"It's fixed, so there's nothing to disclose."The repair is what the buyer wants to evaluate. Disclosing it with the repair records is stronger than silence.
"It only happens in heavy rain."Materiality is about whether a buyer would consider it important, not about frequency.
"It'll kill the sale."An undisclosed condition discovered later is far more likely to kill the sale — or produce a claim after closing.
"The buyer's inspector will find it anyway."Then it will be disclosed by someone; better that it comes from you, with the repair history.
"I don't actually know for certain."Then say so. "Unknown" is an honest and available answer.

4.4 Completing the disclosure properly

  • Every item answered. A blank is ambiguous and invites a later argument about whether it was addressed. "Unknown" is a real answer; a blank is not.
  • The seller completes the seller's disclosure. A licensee filling it in on the seller's behalf misattributes the statement.
  • Uncertain items are not answered "no." Marking an uncertain item "no" asserts knowledge the seller does not have.
  • The agent's own observations are separate. The licensee's visual inspection disclosure is the licensee's statement, not the seller's, and it is an independent obligation.

4.5 Delivery and timing

Content is only half of it. Delivery, and evidence of when delivery occurred, is what rights run from. A file with a signed disclosure and no delivery date cannot show that the buyer's response period was honored.

Where a disclosure is delivered late — after contingencies have been removed, for example — the correct response is to identify the issue immediately rather than proceeding as though the transaction were firm. Rights that run from delivery are not extinguished by the buyer having acted earlier without the information.

4.6 The disclosure conversation that goes wrong

Section 4.3 supplies answers to the seller's predictable objections. This section addresses the harder case: the seller who has heard the answers and still instructs the licensee to leave something out.

The facts. A seller discloses to his agent, privately, that the master bathroom shower leaked into the ceiling below "a few years ago," that he had a handyman open the ceiling and dry it out, and that there was "some black stuff" which the handyman "cleaned with bleach." No permit, no invoice, no professional remediation. He says the ceiling has been fine since. He does not want it on the disclosure, because "it will scare buyers into thinking there's mold."

What has just happened. The seller has told the licensee about a known material fact affecting the value or desirability of the property. It concerns water intrusion, a concealed area, and a possible biological contaminant remediated informally. It is not close to the line.

What the licensee does not do.

  • Leave it off because the seller directed it. Lesson 03, Section 3.3: an instruction to conceal a material fact is not a lawful instruction, and following it while documenting that the seller asked does not transfer responsibility.
  • Suggest phrasing that technically discloses while conveying nothing — "minor prior moisture, resolved." A disclosure that a reasonable buyer would not understand as describing what happened is not a disclosure.
  • Rely on the buyer's inspector to find it. Section 4.2's third principle governs, and the ceiling has been closed.
  • Say nothing and hope the seller reconsiders while the file moves forward.

What the licensee does.

First, explain the exposure to the seller in his own terms — not as a rule, but as the consequence: a buyer who discovers this after closing has a claim, and the fact that it was disclosed to his agent and left off the form is the worst possible version of the record.

Second, offer the stronger path. Disclose it fully with the remediation history and an invitation to inspect. Section 4.3 already establishes why this is stronger than silence: a buyer evaluating a disclosed, documented condition negotiates; a buyer discovering a concealed one litigates. Where the seller is willing, a professional assessment before listing converts the unknown into a fact.

Third, involve the broker immediately if the seller holds his position. This is not a conversation the licensee finishes alone, and the broker will decide whether the brokerage can continue with the listing.

Fourth, record it — what the seller said, when, what the licensee advised, and what was escalated. The note is written the day it happens, because it is the only evidence that the licensee did not participate.

And the licensee's own disclosure is independent. Section 4.4's last point governs: the licensee's observations are the licensee's statement. A seller's refusal does not make the licensee's own obligation disappear.

5. Showing and access controls

5.1 Two obligations at once

Access arrangements serve two purposes that pull against each other: making the property easy to show, and keeping the property and its occupants safe. The licensee's job is to raise the conflict before it produces a loss, not after.

5.2 The intake conversation about access

Before agreeing to any access method, address:

  • Valuables and firearms. Secure or remove them. A seller who has been told this and declines has made an informed choice; a seller who was never asked has not.
  • Medications and personal documents. Frequently overlooked and frequently taken.
  • Occupants. Tenants have possessory rights and notice requirements; children, elderly residents, and pets each change the arrangements.
  • Pets and hazards. Both a safety issue and a liability issue.
  • Security systems and cameras. Recording devices in the home raise legal questions about recording conversations, and a seller who records buyer discussions may create problems for themselves. Disclose the presence of recording devices.

5.3 Uniformity is the control

The most important principle in access management is that the practice must be the same for every visitor.

This is not only about property security. Judgments about which visitors seem trustworthy or serious are precisely where bias operates. A licensee who accompanies some visitors and lets others wander alone has created a fair housing exposure alongside a security failure — and cannot explain the distinction afterward in terms that sound neutral.

Practical protocol:

  1. Visitors are accompanied, or supervised, consistently.
  2. Identification is requested consistently, or not at all.
  3. Showings are logged: who, when, with what brokerage.
  4. The property is secured after each showing, with a defined check.
  5. The same protocol applies at open houses, where the exposure is highest.

5.4 Open houses

Open houses concentrate the risk: multiple strangers, an unsecured property, and often a single licensee. Working practices:

  • Do not work an open house alone where that can be avoided.
  • Have a plan for the property's layout, including where you position yourself and how you would leave.
  • Secure the areas that do not need to be shown.
  • Register visitors consistently, and be accurate with them about what registering means for future contact — that consent is what governs later texts and emails.

5.5 Tenant-occupied property

Where a tenant occupies the property, the tenant's rights govern access. Entry for showings requires the notice the law provides, for a permitted purpose, at a reasonable time. A seller's desire to show the property does not override it, and a licensee who enters without proper notice exposes the seller as well as themselves.

Practical approach: agree a showing protocol with the tenant in advance, honor it, and document notices given.

5.6 When something goes missing

Access controls exist because sometimes they fail. What a licensee does in the first hours after a report determines whether the situation is handled or compounded.

The report arrives — a seller says jewelry is gone, medication is missing, or a laptop that was on the desk is not there.

What the licensee does, in order:

  1. Take it seriously and say so. The instinct to reassure ("it's probably just misplaced") reads to the seller as dismissal, and it is the response that turns an incident into a complaint about the licensee.
  2. Notify the broker immediately. This is a brokerage matter from the first minute, and it may involve insurance, the listing service, and law enforcement.
  3. Tell the seller to contact law enforcement, and do not discourage it. A licensee who steers a seller away from a report — for any reason, including protecting a colleague — has made himself part of the problem.
  4. Preserve the access record. Section 5.3's uniformity requirement produces the log that answers who entered and when. Print it, export it, and preserve it now, before an electronic system rolls over its retention window.
  5. Do not conduct your own investigation. Do not call the agents who showed the property to ask what happened, do not accuse anyone, and do not repeat a seller's suspicion about a named person to anyone other than the broker. A statement about an identified person's honesty carries its own exposure.
  6. Do not alter the log or the file. Whatever it shows, it is the record.

What the file should contain afterward: the seller's report with its date and what was said, the access record, the notification to the broker with its time, the referral to law enforcement, and any report number.

The prevention is the log, and the log is only useful if it was uniform. Section 5.3 makes the point in terms of fair treatment; this section makes it in terms of evidence. An access process that was relaxed for people the licensee knew produces a record that cannot answer the question, and the seller's loss becomes an argument about the licensee's diligence.

One related situation. Where a licensee is the one who discovers something — a door left unlocked, a window open, a lockbox missing — the same sequence applies, starting with the seller and the broker. The instinct to quietly fix it and say nothing is the instinct to create an undocumented event, and undocumented events are the ones that reappear as accusations.

6. Listing cancellation, expiration, and protection

6.1 Three different things

Licensees conflate these, and they operate differently:

  • Expiration — the term ends on its stated date. The engagement simply ends.
  • Cancellation — the engagement is ended before the term expires, under the agreement's terms.
  • Withdrawal — the property is taken off the market while the listing remains in force.

A seller who says "I want to cancel" may mean any of the three, and the first job is to find out which.

6.2 Whose decision cancellation is

The listing is a contract between the seller and the brokerage. Releasing the seller from it is therefore a broker-level decision, not one the individual licensee makes — however sympathetic the circumstances and however much the licensee wants to accommodate the client.

The licensee's role is to understand what the seller wants and why, explain what the agreement provides, and take it to the broker.

6.3 What governs the outcome

The terms of the agreement. Not the licensee's assessment of whether the seller has a good reason, not the number of showings so far, and not the marketing already spent — though those may inform a negotiation, they do not decide the question.

Where a seller is dissatisfied, a productive path is often to identify the actual problem. "I want to cancel" frequently means "nothing is happening and no one is telling me why." A cancellation request that follows a communication failure is sometimes resolved by fixing the communication.

6.4 Withdrawal is not cancellation

A seller who needs the property off the market temporarily — a family situation, a repair, a change of plan — may want withdrawal rather than release. The listing continues; the marketing pauses. This should be documented, and the listing service status updated accurately, since misrepresenting a property's status in a listing service is its own problem.

6.5 After expiration

Two obligations survive:

  1. Confidentiality continues. The former listing agent may not use or disclose the seller's confidential information — including the lowest price the seller would have accepted — afterward, including for another client, and including after the property is relisted elsewhere.
  2. The protection clause may apply, on its terms, to buyers identified to the seller during the term.

6.6 Documenting the ending

Whatever the mechanism, the file should show:

  • The date and method by which the engagement ended.
  • Who authorized it on the brokerage's side.
  • Any agreement about compensation, protection-period buyers, or the return of materials.
  • The listing service status change and its date.

An engagement that ends informally, by everyone ceasing to act, is the one that generates a dispute six weeks later when the property sells.

6.7 Three listings that ended

Listing A — the seller who wants out. Four weeks into a six-month term, the seller calls: "Nothing is happening. I want to cancel and list with someone else."

The first move is to find out which of the three things he means, and Section 6.1 supplies the question. He means cancellation.

The second move is to find out what actually happened. Four weeks with what activity? How many showings, what feedback, what has the licensee reported and how often? Section 6.3 notes that a cancellation request frequently follows a communication failure rather than a marketing failure, and this one has the shape of it — the seller does not say the price is wrong or the photographs are bad. He says nothing is happening, which is what a seller says when nobody has told him anything.

The third move is the broker. Whatever the licensee concludes about the merits, the release is the brokerage's decision. The licensee does not tell the seller "I'll let you out."

The outcome is documented either way — released on stated terms, or continued with an agreed communication schedule and a review date.

Listing B — the expiration nobody noticed. A six-month listing expires on a Sunday. On Wednesday the licensee shows the property to a buyer, having not noticed. On Friday the buyer writes an offer.

The licensee is acting under no agreement. The engagement ended Sunday, and there is no compensation agreement, no authority to market, and no current listing service authorization.

What must not happen is a listing agreement signed Friday and dated to the prior term. Section 6.6's principle and Lesson 03's closing line both govern: a documented gap is survivable, a manufactured document is not.

What happens instead: tell the seller immediately, present the offer — which the seller is entitled to see regardless — and address the engagement going forward with a new agreement dated today, on terms the broker approves. The calendar failure is the real finding, and it is a systems problem: expirations belong in a tracker, not in memory.

Listing C — the protection period. A listing expires. Six weeks later the property sells to a buyer who toured it during the term, now working with a different brokerage.

The protection clause may apply, on its terms, and the terms are the ones in the agreement rather than the ones the licensee remembers. Whether the buyer was identified to the seller in the manner the clause requires, and within the period it specifies, are factual questions answered by what was actually delivered during the term.

The file is what decides it. A licensee who provided the seller a written list of protected buyers before expiration has an answer. One who did not is arguing from recollection against a seller who has already paid another brokerage.

And the claim is the brokerage's, not the licensee's, which means the decision whether to assert it is the broker's.

6.8 The listing file, at a glance

The documents a listing file should hold, in the order they normally arise:

  1. The agency disclosure, dated before the listing agreement.
  2. The listing agreement, with every blank completed and the term definite.
  3. Evidence of authority — how title is held, and the instrument or authority under which anyone signing on another's behalf acts.
  4. The pricing analysis and the net sheet, with the seller's decision recorded, including where it departed from the recommendation.
  5. The seller's disclosures, completed by the seller, with delivery evidenced.
  6. The licensee's own visual inspection disclosure, as the licensee's separate statement.
  7. Advertising as published, with dates, and any required approvals.
  8. The access record, uniform and complete.
  9. Amendments, each signed and dated, with prior versions retained in sequence.
  10. Vendor and conflict disclosures, each dated before the seller acted on the recommendation.
  11. Correspondence evidencing decisions — the price change, the showing instruction, the disclosure conversation.
  12. The ending — expiration, cancellation, or withdrawal, with its date, authorization, and listing service status change.
  13. The protected-buyer list, where the agreement provides for one, delivered before the term ended.

Two of these are the ones missing from most files: item 4, because the pricing conversation feels like a conversation rather than a decision, and item 13, because the protection clause is remembered only after it matters.

Run the list at the listing appointment and again at the ending. At the appointment it tells you what to collect. At the ending it tells you what is still obtainable — which, as Lesson 02's close-out routine establishes, is a category that shrinks every week.

6.9 Checkpoint

You should be able to distinguish expiration, cancellation, and withdrawal; state who decides a cancellation and why; and name the two obligations that survive the end of a listing.

Key terms

TermDefinition
IntakeThe process establishing who has authority to convey, what is being sold, and what constraints affect the transaction — before marketing begins.
Authority to conveyThe legal power to sell the property, which rests with the owners, the trustee, the authorized entity representative, or the court-appointed personal representative — not with the occupant or the person on the tax bill.
TrusteeThe person holding legal title under a trust, who may convey only within the authority the trust document grants.
Attorney-in-factA person acting under a power of attorney, whose authority to convey real property must be currently valid and expressly granted.
Legal descriptionThe description that defines the property in law. The street address does not.
Included and excluded itemsPersonal property and disputable fixtures named expressly in the agreement, so the fixture tests never have to be applied.
Listing agreementThe contract between the seller and the brokerage engaging it to market the property, on negotiated terms.
Definite termination dateThe stated end of the listing term, which protects the seller from an indefinite engagement.
Protection (safety) clauseThe provision addressing compensation where a buyer the brokerage introduced during the term purchases shortly after it ends, ordinarily limited to buyers identified to the seller.
Negotiable compensationCompensation set independently by the brokerage and negotiated with the seller. Never "standard," and never set by an association or listing service.
Comparative market analysisThe licensee's opinion of value, built from closed sales with adjustments, prepared to advise on pricing.
Seller net sheetAn itemized estimate of the seller's proceeds after costs and payoffs, which answers the question the seller is actually asking.
Payoff demandThe lender's statement of what satisfies the loan, including accrued interest and other amounts owed — routinely higher than the principal balance.
MaterialityThe standard for disclosure: whether a fact would affect a reasonable buyer's decision or the price they would pay.
UnknownThe honest answer to a disclosure item the seller cannot answer. Distinct from a blank, which is ambiguous.
Delivery evidenceProof of when a disclosure reached the buyer. Rights run from delivery, so a signature without a date proves the wrong thing.
Access controlThe showing and access protocol, applied identically to every visitor for both security and fair access reasons.
ExpirationThe listing ending on its stated date.
CancellationThe listing being ended before its term expires, under the agreement's terms — a broker-level decision.
WithdrawalTaking the property off the market while the listing remains in force.

Practice problems

Problem 1

A woman calls to list the home she has lived in for twelve years. At the appointment she explains that the house is held in her late mother's trust, that she is one of three children named as beneficiaries, and that she has paid the taxes and upkeep since her mother died.

Whose signature is required, and what must be verified?

Solution. The trustee's, acting within the authority the trust document grants. Not the beneficiaries', however many there are and however clearly they benefit.

What must be verified: who the current trustee is, that the trust authorizes sale of real property, and what the trust requires of the trustee in doing so. Occupancy, payment of taxes, and beneficial interest are all evidence of connection to the property and none establishes authority to sell it.

What transfers: intake failures surface in escrow, where the cost of unwinding is highest. Ten minutes with the trust document at the appointment prevents it.

Problem 2

A seller shows the licensee a competing brokerage's estimate $150,000 above her supported range. It uses three homes currently listed, two of which have sat for over ninety days, and makes no adjustments.

How should the licensee respond?

Solution. By making both analyses legible rather than competing on the number or attacking the competitor.

Active listings show what sellers are asking; they are not evidence of value — and two of the three have been declined by the market for three months. Closed sales show what buyers actually paid. Present the closed evidence, the adjustments, and the reasoning, and let the seller decide with the basis of each number in front of her.

What transfers: "they're just buying the listing" attacks a competitor and gives the seller nothing to evaluate. "I can do that number" abandons the analysis. Neither is advice.

Problem 3

At the listing appointment a seller says he wants the highest possible price, weekend-only showings, a listing he can cancel any time, and to leave the disclosure paperwork until an offer arrives.

Which requests are negotiable?

Solution. The first three. Price, showing windows, and cancellation terms are business terms the parties set together.

The fourth is not. Disclosure obligations run on their own schedule, and deferring them until an offer arrives compresses the buyer's review, creates pressure at the worst moment, and puts both the seller and the brokerage at risk.

The professional move is to separate the categories openly at the appointment rather than defer the conversation — and to start the disclosure work while there is no deadline pressure.

Problem 4

A seller's transfer disclosure comes back with four items left blank. He says he genuinely does not know the answers.

What should the licensee advise?

Solution. Complete every item, answering "unknown" where that is the truth.

A blank is ambiguous: it invites a later dispute about whether the item was addressed at all. "Unknown" is a real, honest, and complete answer.

Two things the licensee must not do: mark the uncertain items "no," which asserts knowledge the seller does not have; or complete the seller's disclosure on his behalf, which misattributes the statement.

Problem 5

A seller wants a lockbox so any licensee can show while he is at work. He keeps firearms, prescription medication, and a coin collection in the house.

What must be addressed, and what if he declines?

Solution. Secure or remove the firearms, medications, and valuables; agree how showings are logged; confirm the access method the seller authorizes; and disclose any recording devices in the home.

If he is told and declines, he has made an informed choice and the file should show the conversation happened. A seller who was never asked has not made a choice at all — and that is the licensee's failure rather than his.

What transfers: convenient access and secure access are different problems. The licensee's job is to raise the conflict before it produces a loss.

Problem 6

A licensee accompanies some open-house visitors through the property and lets others walk through alone, based on her sense of who seems serious.

What is wrong with this?

Solution. Two things, and the second is the more serious.

First, the property is unsupervised for some visitors, which is the security failure.

Second, and worse: judgments about which visitors seem serious or trustworthy are exactly where bias operates, and the distinction cannot be explained afterwards in neutral terms. Selective supervision is a fair housing exposure alongside a security one.

The control is uniformity. Everyone is accompanied, or the same supervision applies to everyone.

Problem 7

Two weeks into a ninety-day listing the seller calls and says she wants to cancel. The licensee has spent $1,400 on photography and marketing and has held two open houses.

Who decides, and what governs?

Solution. The broker decides, because the listing is the brokerage's contract with the seller — not the individual licensee's, however sympathetic he is or however much he wants to accommodate her.

What governs is the agreement's cancellation and compensation terms. The marketing spend and the showings may inform a negotiation; they do not decide the question, and neither does the licensee's view of whether her reason is good.

A useful first step: find out what she actually wants. "I want to cancel" often means "nothing is happening and no one is telling me why," which is a communication problem with a different fix.

Problem 8

A listing expires unsold. Three weeks later the property sells to a buyer the brokerage introduced and identified to the seller in writing during the term.

What applies, and what else survives the expiration?

Solution. The protection clause may apply on its terms — typically to buyers identified to the seller in writing within a defined period after the term ends.

Also surviving: confidentiality. The former listing agent may not use or disclose the seller's confidential information, including her lowest acceptable price, afterwards — including for another client and including after the property is relisted elsewhere.

What transfers: explain the protection clause properly at signing. A seller who understands it accepts it; a seller told "it's standard, just initial here" disputes it later.

Chapter summary

Intake establishes three things: who can convey, what is being sold, and what constrains the transaction. Verify authority against a source rather than assuming it from occupancy or the tax roll, and stop and escalate where it is unclear.

Name the disputable items in the agreement — chandeliers, mounted televisions, appliances, outbuildings — so the fixture tests never have to be applied after the fact.

The listing agreement is the brokerage's contract, and the licensee should be able to explain every operative provision. Compensation is negotiable and never described as standard. The definite termination date protects the seller. The protection clause is explained rather than initialed past.

Price is the seller's decision; the analysis and advice are the licensee's obligation. Both halves matter, and licensees fail in both directions. Where the seller prices above the supported range, the file shows the analysis, the written advice, and the seller's decision — and does not adopt the seller's reasoning as the brokerage's opinion of value.

A net sheet answers the seller's real question, which is proceeds rather than price. Itemize it, label it an estimate, and update it — and warn about the payoff, which includes accrued interest.

Disclosure obligations cannot be scheduled around. Separate them from the negotiable preferences openly at the appointment, complete every item, answer "unknown" honestly, and evidence delivery.

Access protocols must be uniform, because selective supervision is a fair housing exposure as well as a security failure.

Expiration, cancellation, and withdrawal are three different things. Cancellation is a broker-level decision governed by the agreement, and confidentiality survives whichever way the engagement ends.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. Identify which of the seller's requests are negotiable business terms and which are obligations that cannot be deferred, the authority you would verify and how, the advice you would give in writing, the applicable deadline, the communication you would send, the escalation if any, and the evidence you would retain. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Practice - Lesson Assignment 05

Market analysis and pricing strategy

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Define a defensible market area and comparable-selection criteria for a subject property, and justify each inclusion and exclusion.
  • Construct an adjustment grid, apply adjustments in the correct direction, and reconcile the indicated values into a supported range.
  • Distinguish what active, pending, and closed data each prove, and weight them accordingly.
  • Prepare an estimated seller net sheet and explain how price, terms, and concessions move the seller's proceeds.
  • State the legal and practical boundary between a comparative market analysis and an appraisal.
  • Assemble the pricing file so the opinion can be reconstructed as of the date it was given.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: market definition and comparable selection55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: adjustment method and evidence types50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: net proceeds, appraisal boundary, and file support45Sections 5-6 viewed

Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Market selection

1.1 The question a market analysis answers

A comparative market analysis answers one question: what would a willing, informed buyer most likely pay for this property, in this market, within a reasonable marketing period?

Everything in the method follows from that sentence. "Most likely" means the analysis produces a range and a most-probable point, not a single certainty. "Willing and informed" excludes forced and uninformed transactions from the evidence. "This market" requires defining what the relevant market actually is. "Within a reasonable marketing period" ties the estimate to time — a price that would eventually be achieved after two years is not the answer to the question a seller is asking.

New licensees often skip straight to pulling comparables. The definition of the market is the step that determines whether those comparables mean anything.

1.2 Defining the market area

The relevant market is the area within which a buyer for this property would consider alternatives. That is a behavioral definition, not a geographic one, and it is why mechanical radius searches mislead.

Boundaries that genuinely separate markets:

  • School attendance areas, where they materially affect buyer demand.
  • Physical barriers — a freeway, a river, a ridgeline, a rail corridor — that separate neighborhoods a buyer treats as different places.
  • Jurisdictional lines where they change taxation, services, or regulation.
  • Development patterns — a tract of similar-vintage homes is a different market from the custom homes across the arterial, even at the same distance.
  • Common interest developments, where governing documents and assessments create a distinct submarket.

Boundaries that usually do not: a fixed one-mile radius, a ZIP code, or a listing service's district, each of which is an administrative convenience rather than a description of buyer behavior.

A worked judgment

The subject is a 1962 single-story ranch in a tract of about 200 similar homes. Half a mile away, across a six-lane arterial, is a newer development of two-story homes on smaller lots.

A radius search captures both. A market definition does not: a buyer choosing among 1962 ranches on this side of the arterial is not weighing the two-story tract as an equivalent alternative, and vice versa. Sales from across the arterial can still be used — with a location adjustment and an explanation — but they are secondary evidence, and treating them as directly comparable overstates confidence.

1.3 Defining the competitive set

Within the market area, comparables should resemble the subject in the characteristics that drive buyer decisions:

CharacteristicWhy it matters
Living areaThe dominant driver in most residential markets
Lot size and usabilityA large but unusable slope is not a large lot
Bedroom and bathroom countAffects the pool of buyers who will consider it
Age and effective ageEffective age reflects condition and updating, not the year built
Style and storiesSingle-story homes command different demand in many markets
Condition and updatingFrequently the largest single adjustment
Garage and parkingEspecially where street parking is constrained
View, orientation, and exposureCan dominate all other factors in some markets
Site influencesCorner lot, arterial frontage, adjacency to commercial or rail

1.4 Recency and market movement

How recent is recent enough depends on how fast the market is moving. In a stable market, sales from the past six months may all be usable with little time adjustment. In a market that has moved appreciably in a quarter, a six-month-old sale requires a market conditions adjustment or it will mislead.

The test is not the age of the sale but whether the market has changed since it closed. That is why a licensee must have an independent view of market direction — from months of supply, days on market, and the ratio of sale price to list price — before pulling a single comparable.

1.5 Data sources and their limits

  • Listing service data is the primary source and is generally reliable on price and dates, less reliable on square footage, lot size, and condition descriptions written to sell.
  • Public records are useful for ownership, sale history, and characteristics, but assessor square footage and the licensee's measurement frequently disagree, and the assessor's parcel record is not a boundary determination.
  • The licensee's own observation is the only source for current condition, which is often the largest adjustment.

Where sources conflict, say so in the analysis rather than silently choosing one. A note that "the listing service reports 1,840 square feet and the assessor reports 1,790; the analysis uses 1,840 with the discrepancy noted" is stronger than an unexplained number.

1.6 Property types where the standard method breaks down

The market-area and competitive-set method in Sections 1.2 and 1.3 assumes something that is true of most suburban resale housing and false of several common property types: that enough genuinely similar properties sold recently nearby.

Where that assumption fails, the method does not stop working — it starts producing a number with false precision, which is worse than producing none.

Property typeWhy the assumption failsWhat the analysis does instead
Attached housing in a common-interest developmentTwo units with identical floor plans can differ materially by association dues, reserves, pending assessments, litigation, and rental restrictions. The physical comparison is the easy part and the least important.Obtain the association's documents and compare on total monthly cost and restriction, not on price per square foot
Acreage and rural propertySales are few, far apart, and driven by water, access, zoning, and usable versus total acreageWiden the area deliberately and adjust for the drivers rather than the improvements; state the widening in the analysis
A genuinely unique propertyNo competitive set exists in any meaningful sensePresent a range with its reasoning rather than a point, and say plainly that the data does not support precision
New construction in an active subdivisionThe builder's prices are list prices set by a single seller, and the incentives are frequently not in the recorded priceTreat builder pricing as active-listing evidence under Section 3.3, and pursue what the incentives actually were
Deed-restricted or income-restricted housingPrice is constrained by a governing instrument rather than by the marketRead the restriction. The analysis is bounded by it, and this is a legal document rather than a market fact
Mixed residential and income useValue derives partly from income, which the residential method does not captureRecognize the limit and say so; an income analysis is a different discipline

The professional obligation in every row is the same: state what the data supports and what it does not.

The failure this prevents: a single-page analysis with three comparables, a confident number, and no acknowledgment that the subject is a five-acre parcel with a shared well and the nearest similar sale is eleven months old and four miles away. The number is not wrong because the licensee was careless. It is wrong because the method was applied where its assumptions did not hold, and nothing on the page tells the seller that.

1.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to define a market area for a given subject and justify one comparable you included and one you excluded, in terms of buyer behavior rather than distance.

2. Comparable adjustment reasoning

2.1 The direction of adjustment

This is the most-missed mechanic in the entire method, and it is worth stating in a form that removes ambiguity.

Adjustments are made to the comparable, never to the subject.

The question each adjustment answers is: what would this comparable have sold for if it had been like the subject?

Therefore:

  • If the comparable is superior to the subject in some respect, its price is adjusted downward.
  • If the comparable is inferior to the subject, its price is adjusted upward.

A memory aid used widely: CBS — Comparable Better, Subtract. And its complement: comparable worse, add.

2.2 A worked adjustment grid

Subject: 1,800 square feet, 3 bedrooms, 2 baths, 6,000 square foot lot, 2-car garage, updated kitchen, no view.

Comp AComp BComp C
Sale price$845,000$902,000$810,000
Closed2 months ago5 months ago1 month ago
Living area1,8001,9801,700
Bedrooms / baths3 / 24 / 23 / 2
Lot6,1006,0005,400
Garage2-car2-car1-car
KitchenUpdatedUpdatedOriginal
Adjustments
Market conditions—+$9,000—
Living area—−$27,000+$15,000
Bedroom count—−$10,000—
Lot size−$1,000—+$4,000
Garage——+$12,000
Kitchen——+$20,000
Net adjustment−$1,000−$28,000+$51,000
Adjusted value$844,000$874,000$861,000

Read the grid carefully and notice what each line is doing:

  • Comp B closed five months ago in a rising market, so it is adjusted up — it would have sold for more today.
  • Comp B is larger, so it is adjusted down toward the subject's size.
  • Comp C has an original kitchen and a one-car garage, both inferior, so both are adjusted up.

2.3 Reconciling the indications

Three adjusted values — $844,000, $874,000, $861,000 — are not averaged. Averaging treats a comparable requiring $51,000 of adjustment as equally reliable as one requiring $1,000, which it is not.

Reconciliation weights the indications by reliability:

  1. Net adjustment size. Comp A required almost none; it is the strongest indicator. Comp C required $51,000 gross; it is the weakest.
  2. Recency. Comp C is the most recent, which partly offsets its adjustment burden.
  3. Similarity in the characteristics that drive this market.

A defensible reconciliation here: the supported range is approximately $845,000 to $870,000, with the most probable value near $850,000 to $855,000, weighted toward Comp A as the least-adjusted and most similar indicator.

Note that the conclusion is a range with a most-probable point and a stated reason for the weighting — not a single number presented as fact.

2.4 Where adjustment amounts come from

Adjustment values are not invented. They are derived, in order of preference:

  1. Paired sales analysis — comparing sales that differ in one characteristic to isolate its contribution.
  2. Market-derived rates — an established price per square foot of difference within this submarket, applied to modest differences only.
  3. Cost with depreciation — for a specific improvement, recognizing that contribution rarely equals cost.
  4. Reasoned judgment, disclosed as such.

The last one is legitimate when the first three are unavailable, provided it is identified as judgment rather than presented as measurement.

2.5 Contribution is not cost

The most common seller misconception, and one the licensee must be able to explain without giving offense:

Money spent on a property does not become value in the property. The market decides what an improvement contributes, and for specialized improvements the contribution is usually well below the cost.

A $150,000 recording studio built into a garage in a neighborhood of family homes may contribute a fraction of that, because the pool of buyers who want it — and who would pay for it — is small. This is over-improvement relative to the neighborhood, and it is a real and recognized phenomenon rather than a rhetorical device for arguing a seller down.

The complement is also true: some modest improvements contribute more than they cost, because they remove a specific buyer objection.

2.6 Common adjustment errors

ErrorWhy it is wrong
Adjusting the subject instead of the comparableReverses the entire logic of the method
Averaging adjusted valuesTreats unreliable indicators as equal to reliable ones
Discarding every comparable with a differenceLeaves nothing to analyze; differences are what adjustments exist for
Adjusting for price per square foot across large differencesContribution per square foot is not linear across a large gap
Making a lot of small adjustments to reach a targetThe grid becomes an argument for a predetermined number
No adjustment for a distressed sale, or automatic exclusionWhether it reflects this market is an analytical question, not a preference

2.7 Bracketing, and the grid that proves too much

Two disciplines separate a defensible grid from a persuasive one.

Bracketing. For each significant characteristic, the comparable set should contain properties both above and below the subject. A subject with four bedrooms compared only against three-bedroom sales produces an adjusted range built entirely on upward adjustments, and an adjustment applied in only one direction cannot be checked. Where the set brackets, an error in the adjustment amount tends to show itself: the grid's indications spread apart rather than converging.

Net and gross adjustment. Two figures, and they answer different questions.

  • Net adjustment is the sum of the adjustments with their signs, and it tells you how far the comparable sits from the subject overall.
  • Gross adjustment is the sum of their absolute values, and it tells you how much work the grid is doing.

A comparable with a small net and a large gross has been adjusted heavily in both directions — six thousand up for size, six thousand down for condition, and so on — and the small net conceals it. A comparable requiring large gross adjustment is a weak comparable regardless of how close its net lands to the subject.

The grid that proves too much. Three comparables, each adjusted by amounts approaching a fifth of its sale price, all landing within a few thousand dollars of each other and of the number the seller mentioned on the phone. Convergence produced by heavy adjustment is not evidence. It is the analyst's judgment restated three times, and a reviewer will read it that way.

What to do when the grid does not converge:

  1. Re-examine the competitive set before touching the adjustments. A wide spread usually means one comparable does not belong.
  2. Check for a characteristic not in the grid — a corner lot on an arterial, a view, a functional obsolescence, a school attendance boundary the price data reflects and the property card does not.
  3. Report the range honestly where the spread is real. Section 2.3 reconciles; it does not average. A wide supported range is a more useful answer than a narrow unsupported one, and Section 6.1 makes it defensible only if the reasoning is on the page.

3. Active, pending, and sold evidence

3.1 What each category proves

CategoryWhat it isWhat it provesWhat it does not prove
Closed salesCompleted transactionsWhat buyers actually paidCurrent conditions, if the market has moved
Pending salesPrice agreed, not yet closedWhere the market is moving nowThe final price, since terms can change
Active listingsCurrently offeredWhat sellers are asking; the competitionValue — asking prices are hopes
Expired and withdrawnFailed to sellWhat the market rejectedMuch on its own, but a useful ceiling indicator

3.2 Why closed sales dominate

A closed sale is evidence of a completed exchange between a buyer and a seller — the only direct evidence of price the market produces. Everything else is intention.

That does not make closed sales sufficient. In a market that has moved, closed data is a picture of the recent past, which is why market conditions adjustments and pending evidence matter.

3.3 Why active listings mislead when misused

Active listings are the subject's competition and belong in the analysis for that reason. What they cannot do is establish value, because a seller can ask any number at all.

This is the core defect in the pricing dispute licensees encounter most often:

Seller: "The other brokerage said $1.3 million. Three homes near here are listed at that."

The analysis: Those three homes have been on the market 94, 112, and 130 days without an offer. That is not evidence that the market pays $1.3 million — it is evidence that the market has declined to pay $1.3 million three separate times.

The framing that works: "Those listings tell us what our competition is asking. What they also tell us is that at that price, buyers have walked past three homes like yours for over three months. Here is what buyers actually paid, in the last four months, for homes like yours."

That version does not attack the competitor and does not argue with the seller. It reads the same data more carefully and lets the seller draw the conclusion.

3.4 Days on market and absorption

Two market indicators every licensee should be able to compute and explain:

Months of supply — the inventory currently available divided by the average monthly rate of sales. It answers: at the current pace, how long would it take to sell everything now listed? Lower supply indicates a market favoring sellers; higher supply, one favoring buyers.

Days on market — how long properties take to go under contract. Rising days on market is often the earliest visible sign that a market is turning, well before closed prices reflect it.

Worked example. A submarket has 48 active listings. Over the past three months, 36 properties went under contract — an average of 12 per month.

Months of supply = 48 ÷ 12 = 4 months.

If the same submarket had 96 actives and 8 sales per month, months of supply would be 12 — a materially different market calling for a materially different pricing strategy, even if recent closed prices looked similar.

3.5 The stale-listing effect

A property that sits generates its own headwind. Buyers and their agents read long market time as a signal that something is wrong, and offers that eventually arrive are frequently below where the property would have sold had it been priced correctly at launch.

This is the substantive argument against overpricing, and it is more persuasive to sellers than "the comps don't support it," because it describes a mechanism rather than asserting a conclusion:

"If we launch above the market, the first three weeks — when buyer attention is highest and every agent with a matching buyer looks at it — go to a price the market won't meet. After that we are competing for attention with the next new listing, carrying the market time as a visible number. Sellers who reduce later frequently end up below where they would have started."

3.6 Concessions, and why the recorded price is not the price

A closed sale's recorded price is the number the analysis starts from. It is frequently not the number the parties agreed to in economic terms, and the difference is concessions.

What moves value away from the recorded price:

ConcessionEffect
Seller credit toward buyer's closing costsThe buyer paid a higher nominal price in exchange for cash back. The recorded price overstates the market price.
Rate buydown paid by the sellerSame mechanism, larger and less visible. A meaningful buydown can support a materially higher nominal price.
Personal property includedFurnishings, equipment, or a vehicle bundled into the price.
Repairs credited rather than performedThe price reflects a property that was never brought to the condition the price assumes.
Seller-carried financing on favorable termsThe price and the financing are one bargain. A price achieved with seller financing at below-market terms is not a cash-equivalent price.
Leaseback or delayed possession at no costValue transferred outside the price.

The analytical consequence. A grid built on recorded prices in a market where credits are common systematically overstates value, and the overstatement is invisible on the page. The licensee who does not pursue concession data is comparing nominal numbers in a market that trades in net ones.

Where the data comes from: the listing service's concession fields where they are populated, the closing information the licensee has for his own transactions, and — most reliably and least often used — asking the agent who closed the comparable. That call takes two minutes and is the single highest-value verification step in market analysis.

The same discipline applies to the subject. A seller told "the house down the street sold for 812" is being given a number that may have carried a substantial credit. Section 4.4 establishes that price interacts with terms; this section establishes that the interaction is already baked into the comparables, and a net sheet built on a gross comparable is wrong before the arithmetic starts.

State it in the analysis. Where concession data was available, say what was found. Where it was not, say that too. "Concession information not available for comparables 2 and 3" is a professional statement, and it tells the seller how much confidence the range deserves.

4. Seller net and pricing consequences

4.1 The seller's real question

Sellers ask about price. What they need in order to decide is proceeds. An estimated seller net sheet translates one into the other.

4.2 What a net sheet contains

LineNotes
Estimated sale priceThe variable being tested
Less: existing loan payoff(s)Balance plus accrued interest to the payoff date, plus any other amounts under the note — not the balance alone
Less: brokerage compensationPer the listing agreement
Less: escrow and title chargesAllocation varies by local custom and by agreement
Less: transfer taxes and recordingJurisdiction dependent
Less: county and association prorationsTaxes, assessments, dues to the ownership boundary
Less: agreed repairs or creditsWhere known
Less: buyer closing cost creditWhere agreed
Less: other liens, judgments, or assessmentsDiscovered at intake, not at closing
= Estimated net proceedsLabeled an estimate

4.3 A worked comparison

A seller is weighing a $920,000 offer with a $15,000 closing cost credit against an $895,000 offer with no credit.

Offer AOffer B
Price$920,000$895,000
Buyer credit−$15,000$0
Compensation (assume 5%)−$46,000−$44,750
Escrow, title, transfer, recording (assume)−$9,200−$9,000
Subtotal before payoff$849,800$841,250

Offer A nets about $8,550 more. That is the arithmetic — and it is not the whole answer. The licensee's job is to put the rest in front of the seller too:

  • Financing strength. A credit-heavy offer sometimes signals a buyer stretched on cash.
  • Appraisal exposure. The higher price carries more risk of an appraisal shortfall, and the credit does not reduce the price the appraisal must support.
  • Contingencies and timelines, which affect the probability the transaction closes at all.
  • Occupancy and possession terms, which may matter more to this seller than $8,550.

A net sheet that produces a number without that context invites the seller to decide on the number alone.

4.4 How price interacts with terms

Sellers frequently under-weight terms. Three worth explaining explicitly:

  1. A credit is not a price reduction for appraisal purposes. The property must still appraise at the contract price for the lender to lend against it.
  2. A shorter contingency period has real value because it shortens the window in which the transaction can fail, during which the property is off the market.
  3. A larger down payment reduces the risk of a financing failure, which is worth something even at an identical price.

4.5 Updating the net sheet

A net sheet prepared at listing is an estimate built on assumptions. It should be updated when the price changes materially, when an offer with different terms is under consideration, and when the payoff figure is actually obtained — which is frequently higher than the seller expects, because it includes accrued interest and other amounts under the note rather than the principal balance alone.

4.6 The seller who may not have enough equity

A net sheet sometimes produces a negative number, or a number too small to cover the seller's next step. This changes the conversation from pricing to something else, and the licensee's obligation is to recognize the change rather than to keep discussing price.

What the net sheet has revealed: that the transaction as contemplated does not work, which is a fact the seller needs before he makes commitments — a deposit on a replacement property, a moving date, a school enrollment.

The paths that exist, none of which the licensee decides:

  1. Bring funds to closing. Available to some sellers and not others, and its consequences are the seller's own financial question.
  2. Reduce the cost of sale. Real but usually small, and it does not close a large gap.
  3. Do not sell now. A legitimate outcome and often the right one. A licensee who cannot say this to a seller has an incentive problem, not a knowledge problem.
  4. A sale requiring lienholder approval. A transaction of a different kind, with its own timeline, its own documentation, its own disclosure requirements, and consequences to the seller — credit, tax, and potentially deficiency — that are legal and tax questions, not licensee questions.
  5. Alternatives outside a sale, which belong to the seller's lender, counsel, and tax advisor.

Three obligations attach the moment the number comes back short.

Route the questions. The tax consequences of debt forgiveness, whether a deficiency can be pursued, and what any of it does to the seller's credit are not questions a licensee answers. They are referred, and the referral is recorded.

Do not promise an outcome that depends on a third party. Whether a lienholder will approve anything is a lienholder's decision. "They'll approve it, they always do" is the assurance category from Lesson 02, and it is made to sellers under exactly this pressure.

Watch for the schemes that target this seller. A distressed seller is the target of arrangements involving up-front fees, title transfers with promised leasebacks, and rescue offers. A licensee who encounters one raises it with the broker immediately, and does not participate on the theory that the seller wanted to.

The net sheet's value here is that it produced the bad news early, while the seller still has options. Section 4.5's requirement to update it is what keeps the news current — a gap that was manageable at one price is not at another, and the seller should learn that from the licensee rather than at closing.

5. Appraisal versus comparative market analysis

5.1 The boundary

Comparative market analysisAppraisal
Prepared byA real estate licenseeA licensed or certified appraiser
PurposeTo advise a client on pricing or offer strategyAn independent opinion of value for a defined use, commonly lending
StandardsProfessional practice; not appraisal standardsProfessional appraisal standards
IndependenceAdvises a client the licensee representsIndependent of the parties
May be called an appraisalNoYes

The difference is not the data used. A licensee's analysis and an appraiser's report may rely on the same closed sales. The difference is who prepares it, under what standards, with what independence, and for what purpose.

5.2 What a licensee may and may not do
