A buyer who needs a third bedroom for an aging parent has different requirements — single story, bathroom access, proximity to care — from one who needs a home office. The stated criterion is a proxy; the purpose is the actual specification.

Constraints. Timeline, and what drives it. Whether a sale must close first. Lease expiry. School year. Employment start date. A constraint the licensee learns in week six was a constraint in week one.

Financial position. What the buyer can borrow, what cash is available, and what the buyer is willing to spend, which is frequently lower than what he can borrow. All three numbers matter and they are not the same.

Decision process. Who decides. A buyer whose spouse has not seen a property, or whose parent is providing funds, has a decision process that includes people not in the room. Establishing this early prevents the accepted-offer-then-withdrawn sequence.

Expectations about the process. What happens after an offer is accepted, roughly how long each stage takes, what the buyer will be asked to do and when. Buyers who understand the sequence make better decisions inside it.

1.3 Agency disclosure belongs here

Because agency can arise from conduct, the agency disclosure belongs at the consultation rather than at the point an offer is written. A licensee who advises a buyer for three weeks and then produces an agency disclosure with the offer has given the disclosure after the relationship it describes was already operating.

The practical sequence:

  1. Provide the agency disclosure and explain the relationship options.
  2. Discuss and, if agreed, execute the representation agreement.
  3. Then begin the search.

1.4 Questions that surface what matters

Instead ofAsk
"What's your budget?""What monthly payment are you comfortable with, and what have you been told you can borrow?"
"How many bedrooms?""Who will be living there, and how do you expect to use each room?"
"What areas do you like?""What does your day look like — where do you need to get to, and how?"
"When do you want to move?""What happens if you haven't moved by then?"
"Do you need to sell first?""What has to happen before you can close on a purchase?"

The left column produces a specification. The right column produces an understanding of the buyer's actual situation, which is what allows a licensee to recognize when a property meets the need despite failing a stated criterion — or fails the need despite meeting every one.

1.5 What the licensee must not do at consultation

Two boundaries appear immediately and recur throughout the relationship:

  • Do not characterize neighborhoods by who lives in them. Addressed fully in section 4, but it starts here, because the consultation is where a buyer most often asks.
  • Do not advise on loan program suitability. General education about how financing works is appropriate. A specific recommendation requires the buyer's full financial detail and belongs to a qualified lender.

1.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to take a buyer's stated criterion, identify the purpose behind it, and explain how knowing the purpose changes which properties you would show.

2. Representation agreement

2.1 What it does

A buyer representation agreement engages the brokerage to represent the buyer, and defines the terms of that engagement: its scope, its duration, and how the brokerage is compensated.

Like a listing agreement, it is a contract between the buyer and the brokerage, not the individual licensee. And like a listing agreement, it is negotiable.

2.2 The operative terms

TermWhat it doesWhat to explain to the buyer
PartiesBuyer and brokerageThe brokerage is the party, not the individual agent
TermBeginning and definite ending dateA definite end protects the buyer from an indefinite engagement
ScopeGeographic area, property type, price rangeA narrow scope leaves the buyer free elsewhere; a broad one does not
ExclusivityWhether the buyer may work with other brokeragesThe most consequential term, and the one buyers least expect
CompensationAmount, who pays, and when it is earnedNegotiable; explain the mechanics rather than assuming the buyer knows them
Protection periodCompensation where the buyer purchases a property the brokerage introduced, shortly after the termExplain its ordinary limits
DutiesWhat each party undertakesIncludes the buyer's obligations, which buyers often overlook
Dispute resolutionMediation and arbitration provisionsSubstantive rights; explain rather than initial past
Agency confirmationRecords the relationshipFollows the disclosure

2.3 Explaining exclusivity honestly

Exclusivity is where buyer representation agreements generate complaints, almost always because it was not explained.

The honest explanation:

"This agreement is exclusive for the term and the scope we've written. That means if you buy a home in this area and price range during this period, our brokerage is the one representing you — including if you find it yourself at an open house, and including if you work with another agent on it. In exchange, we commit to the work we've just described. If you'd rather not commit that way, we can shorten the term, narrow the scope, or discuss a different arrangement."

A buyer who hears that and signs has agreed. A buyer who signs after "it's just so I can show you properties" has not, and will say so later.

2.4 Compensation mechanics

Buyers routinely misunderstand how a buyer's agent is compensated, and licensees routinely explain it badly. Points to cover explicitly:

  1. Compensation is negotiable between the buyer and the brokerage. It is not set by any association, listing service, or custom.
  2. The source of payment does not determine representation. A buyer's agent compensated from the seller's side still represents the buyer. This must be explained, not assumed.
  3. What happens if the seller's side offers less than the agreed compensation, or offers none — the agreement should address the gap, and the buyer should understand it before he is looking at properties where it matters.
  4. When compensation is earned versus when it is payable.

2.5 The buyer's obligations

Agreements typically impose obligations on the buyer as well, and buyers are frequently surprised by them:

  • To work through the brokerage for properties within the scope.
  • To identify the brokerage when contacting listing agents or attending open houses.
  • To provide financial information necessary to make offers credible.
  • To disclose other relationships or prior contacts with properties.

That second one is worth stressing at signing. A buyer who visits an open house and gives a listing agent his name without mentioning his representation creates a procuring-cause dispute that lands on him as well as on the brokerages.

2.6 May a licensee show property without an agreement?

Whether to require a signed agreement before showing property is a business decision within the brokerage's policy, not a legal requirement in itself. What is not optional is the agency disclosure, which is required regardless of whether a representation agreement is signed.

A licensee who refuses to show anything without a signed agreement is stating a policy, and should say so as a policy. A licensee who tells a buyer it is legally required has misstated the law.

3. Financial readiness

3.1 Three different things

Buyers, sellers, and new licensees conflate these constantly. The distinctions are practical, not academic — they determine how much weight an offer carries and how likely it is to close.

What it isWhat it establishesWhat it does not
PrequalificationA lender's preliminary assessment, largely on stated informationA rough borrowing rangeVerification of anything
PreapprovalA lender's review of supporting informationA likely loan amount, subject to conditionsA commitment to lend
Loan commitmentA conditional or firm commitment after underwritingSubstantially more certaintyImmunity from changed circumstances

None of the three is a guarantee that a loan will fund. All three remain contingent on the property, the appraisal, and the borrower's circumstances holding steady through funding.

3.2 Reading a preapproval letter

A licensee should read the letter rather than filing it. Questions it should answer, and frequently does not:

  • What was actually verified — income, assets, employment, credit?
  • What conditions remain?
  • What loan program and terms does it assume?
  • What is the expiration date?
  • Is the amount stated a maximum, or the amount for this property?

A letter that verifies nothing and expires in a week is worth telling the buyer about, because the seller's agent will read it the same way.

3.3 The three numbers

What the buyer can borrow. The lender's number.

What cash is available. Down payment, closing costs, reserves, moving costs, and the immediate costs of occupying a home. Buyers routinely plan to the down payment and forget the rest.

What the buyer is willing to spend. Often lower than the first, and it is the number that should drive the search. A licensee who searches to the lender's maximum is showing properties the buyer will feel unable to afford.

Where these three conflict, the conflict is the conversation. A buyer whose comfortable payment supports less than his preapproval should know that, and should decide deliberately rather than discovering it at the offer stage.

3.4 Funds from family

Assistance from a relative is common and is not a problem — but how the lender treats it determines whether it supports a larger loan.

The correct handling when a buyer says a relative will help:

  1. Ask him to confirm with his lender how the assistance would be treated and documented.
  2. Do not build the search around the higher figure until that is confirmed.
  3. Do not contact the relative directly.

A gift and a loan are treated differently, because an undisclosed loan is a hidden obligation that undermines the lender's capacity analysis. This is the same principle that makes occupancy and liability misstatements serious, seen from the funds side.

3.5 What the licensee may and may not advise

May: explain in general terms how the common loan program types work; explain what a preapproval is; explain how the appraisal interacts with financing; recommend that the buyer speak with more than one lender.

May not: recommend a specific loan program as suitable for this buyer; predict what a lender will approve; advise on how to structure the buyer's finances to qualify; suggest anything be omitted or characterized differently on an application.

That last one deserves emphasis. A buyer who asks a licensee to help him present his finances more favorably is asking for participation in loan fraud, however casually the request is phrased.

3.6 When readiness changes mid-search

A buyer's financial position is established at consultation and then treated as settled. It frequently is not, and the changes that matter are the ones the buyer does not think to mention because they do not feel like loan events.

What the buyer doesWhy it matters
Opens a credit account — a store card at a furniture purchase, a financed applianceNew debt and a new inquiry, both visible to the lender, both potentially re-underwritten before closing
Changes jobs, including to a better oneEmployment history is part of the determination, and a change of structure — salaried to commission, employee to contractor — can be more consequential than the amount
Moves money between accountsCreates deposits the lender will ask about, and Lesson 14 of the mortgage course explains what the answer must and must not be
Receives a giftDocumentable, and the documentation is easier before the funds move than after
Pays off a debt using reserve fundsImproves one number and damages another
Co-signs for someoneAn obligation, whether or not the buyer thinks of it as his
Has a rate lock expireChanges the payment and therefore the qualifying amount, sometimes materially

The licensee's obligations here are narrow and important.

Tell the buyer at consultation that these categories exist and that the rule is simple: talk to the lender before doing any of them. That single sentence, said once at the beginning, prevents most of what appears on this list.

Ask periodically during a long search — "anything change with work, credit, or the money since we started?" — and record the answer. A search running four months is a search in which something has probably changed.

Route it to the lender, always. Section 3.5 sets the boundary and it holds here. The licensee does not evaluate whether a change matters, does not tell the buyer a job change is fine, and does not advise on how to handle a deposit. He tells the buyer to call the lender, and he confirms the buyer did.

Update the search when the answer comes back. A buyer whose qualifying amount has fallen is being shown properties he can no longer buy, which wastes his time and sets up a failure at the worst moment.

The failure this prevents. A buyer in contract, three weeks from closing, whose lender re-pulls credit and finds a financed furniture purchase made the week the offer was accepted — because nobody told him not to, and because buying furniture for the new house is the most natural thing a person in his position does.

3.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to read a preapproval letter and tell the buyer what it establishes, what it leaves open, and how a seller's agent will read it.

4. Property search and steering avoidance

4.1 The search must be driven by the buyer's criteria

The governing principle is short: the buyer sets the criteria; the licensee applies them.

Steering occurs when the licensee's own judgment about where a buyer belongs shapes which properties the buyer sees or how they are presented. It is defined by whose criteria drove the selection, not by the licensee's intent.

4.2 Steering in its obvious and subtle forms

FormExample
DirectDeclining to show properties in an area to a buyer
Redirection"You'd be more comfortable over here" — showing a different area than the one asked about
Curated balanceSelecting a "representative mix" of neighborhoods chosen by the licensee rather than by the buyer's criteria
CharacterizationDescribing residents rather than the property or the area's objective features
Differential enthusiasmShowing everything but presenting some properties warmly and others flatly
Differential effortPrompt follow-up on some inquiries, slower on others
Assumed suitabilityDeciding a two-story home "won't work" for a buyer with a disability and not showing it
Soliciting preferencesAsking which protected characteristics matter to the buyer so they can be "honored"

The last three are the ones committed with good intentions, and they are steering all the same.

4.3 The questions buyers ask, and how to answer

Buyers ask about neighborhoods. Declining well is a professional skill, and the components are consistent: decline to characterize, explain briefly why, and point to a source the buyer can use himself.

"What kind of people live in this neighborhood?" "I don't describe neighborhoods by who lives in them — that's something I'm not permitted to do, and honestly my impression wouldn't be worth much. What I can do is point you to census data and the city's own information so you can look at whatever matters to you."

"Is this a safe area?" "Safety isn't something I can characterize for you. Local law enforcement publishes incident data by area, and here's where to find it. I'd also suggest visiting at different times of day."

"Which schools are good?" "School quality is something you should evaluate against your own priorities. The district publishes performance data and here's the link. I'd avoid ranking them for you, because what matters in a school varies a lot by family."

"Would I fit in here?" "That's really a question only you can answer, and I'd be guessing. What I can tell you is what the area has — here's what's within walking distance, here's what the commute looks like, here's the housing stock."

Each answer does three things: declines the characterization, gives a reason that is honest rather than evasive, and leaves the buyer better equipped than before.

4.4 What not to do instead

  • Do not simply refuse and change the subject. The buyer has a real informational need and will get an answer somewhere.
  • Do not answer "in general terms." A general characterization is still a characterization.
  • Do not answer by showing rather than telling — redirecting the search toward what you would have said.

4.5 Documenting the search

Where a search is later questioned, what defends it is the record of the criteria and their application:

  • The buyer's stated criteria, recorded at consultation and updated as they change.
  • The search parameters used.
  • The properties presented and shown, with dates.
  • Where a property matching the criteria was not shown, why.

A list of properties shown, without the criteria that produced it, shows what happened without showing why. The criteria are what make it defensible.

4.6 Fair housing is not only about the buyer in front of you

The obligation runs to everyone. A licensee who accepts a seller's instruction to limit who sees a property has violated it as to buyers he never met. A licensee whose marketing reaches a narrow audience by design has done the same. The buyer relationship is one surface among several.

5. Investigation and inspections

5.1 The licensee's role

Three things, and only three:

  1. Advise the buyer to investigate, and be specific about what this property calls for.
  2. Disclose what the licensee knows and what the licensee's own visual inspection revealed.
  3. Recognize the limit of the license and refer beyond it.

What the licensee does not do: evaluate conditions, assess the adequacy of repairs, estimate repair costs, or advise whether a finding is serious enough to act on.

5.2 The visual inspection duty

In a residential transaction the licensee conducts a reasonably competent and diligent visual inspection of the accessible areas and discloses material facts it reveals.

Its boundaries matter in both directions:

  • It is bounded. Accessible areas, visual observation. The licensee does not move belongings, open walls, climb onto roofs, or operate systems.
  • It is independent. Reviewing the seller's disclosure is not performing it, and recommending a professional inspector does not discharge it.

What the licensee observes should be recorded — including observations that seem minor. Staining on a ceiling, a patched section of wall, a door that will not close: each is an observation, and each is worth noting whether or not the licensee knows what it means.

5.3 Recommending investigation

Investigation should be matched to the property rather than recited generically. A general home inspection is the baseline; beyond it, the property suggests what else:

Property featureInvestigation to recommend
Older constructionSystems, sewer line, and where applicable lead-based paint and asbestos evaluation
Slope, retaining walls, drainage evidenceGeotechnical or drainage evaluation
Visible foundation cracking or floor slopeStructural evaluation
Well or septicWater quality and quantity; septic inspection
Apparent additions or conversionsPermit records; verify legal status and square footage
Pool, spaEquipment and safety compliance
Fire, flood, or seismic mapped areaInsurance availability and cost, before the contingency expires
Common interest developmentGoverning documents, budget, reserves, minutes, litigation, assessments
Trees, shared fences, drivewaysBoundary and shared-facility questions

5.4 Reading an inspection report

The licensee's job when a report arrives is to make sure the buyer engages with it, not to interpret it.

Appropriate: confirming the buyer received and read it; asking whether he has questions for the inspector; helping him identify which findings warrant a specialist; tracking the contingency deadline; documenting what was recommended and what the buyer decided.

Not appropriate: characterizing a finding as minor or major; estimating repair costs; predicting whether the seller will agree to repairs; advising whether the buyer should proceed.

A buyer asks: "The inspector flagged the electrical panel. Is that a big deal?"

Correct: "That's outside what I'm qualified to assess, and it's exactly the kind of finding worth having an electrician look at before your contingency expires. Would you like me to arrange that? I'll note in the file that we discussed it."

Incorrect: "Those panels come up all the time, it's usually not serious." — an evaluation the licensee is not qualified to make, on which the buyer may rely.

5.5 New construction is not an exception

Buyers frequently assume new construction does not need inspection because it is new and carries a warranty. Both premises are weak: new construction has defects like anything else, and a warranty addresses remedies after a problem appears rather than identifying problems before closing.

The advice is the same — recommend investigation appropriate to the property — and the licensee's own observations remain a separate duty.

5.6 Waiving investigation

In a competitive market buyers propose waiving inspection contingencies. This is the buyer's decision, and the licensee's obligation is to make it an informed one:

  1. Explain in writing what protection each contingency provides and what waiving it exposes him to.
  2. Note that a buyer may still investigate without a contingency — he simply has no contractual path to act on what he finds.
  3. Follow the buyer's informed decision.

Both failure modes are wrong: refusing to write the offer overstates the licensee's role, and writing it without comment abandons the advisory duty.

5.7 A worked inspection response

The facts. A buyer's general inspection on a fifty-year-old house returns forty-one items. Among them: an active roof leak at a valley; two double-tapped breakers in the panel; a water heater with no seismic strapping; "evidence of past moisture" at the base of a bedroom wall with the note "further evaluation recommended"; a furnace at the end of its expected life but currently operating; original galvanized supply piping; and a retaining wall in the rear yard described as "leaning, recommend evaluation by a qualified professional."

What the licensee does with this, and what he does not.

He does not sort the list into important and unimportant items for the buyer. Section 5.4 governs: the report is the inspector's, its recommendations are the inspector's, and a licensee who tells a buyer which items to ignore has substituted his judgment for a professional's in a field he is not licensed in.

He does read it carefully, because two categories in this report require action rather than opinion.

The "further evaluation recommended" items are the report's most important content, and they are the ones buyers skim past. The moisture at the wall base and the retaining wall are both flagged for specialist evaluation, and a general inspection that recommends further evaluation has told the buyer that it did not answer the question. Section 5.3's obligation is to recommend the investigation, in writing, and to record that it was recommended.

The active leak is a present condition affecting a system, and it interacts with the moisture note. Whether they are the same problem is exactly what the specialist determines.

He helps the buyer understand the process without characterizing the findings: what the report says, that certain items were referred for further evaluation, what the contract's timelines are, what the options within them are, and what happens at each deadline. "What do I do about this?" is answered with the process, not with a recommendation about which items to request.

He puts the recommendation in writing. Every item flagged for further evaluation, listed, with the recommendation that the buyer obtain it before removing his investigation contingency. This is the single document that matters most in this scenario two years from now.

If the buyer declines the specialist evaluations, Section 5.6 governs: the declination is documented, in the buyer's words where possible, with the recommendation that preceded it. The licensee does not talk him out of the decision and does not pretend it was not made.

What the licensee must not say, in any form: that the furnace "has years left," that galvanized piping "is normal for the age," that the double-tapped breakers are "an easy fix," or that the retaining wall is "cosmetic." Each of those is an opinion about a building system, offered by a person without the qualification to hold it, and each is the sentence a claim quotes.

5.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to look at a property's features and produce a specific investigation recommendation, and respond correctly to a buyer asking you to evaluate an inspection finding.

6. Offer strategy and documentation

6.1 Terms are the buyer's; advice is the licensee's

The same division that governs listing price governs offer terms. The buyer decides price, contingencies, timelines, and concessions. The licensee supplies the analysis, the risks, and the options — and records that the decision was informed.

6.2 The components of an offer strategy

Price, informed by the same comparable analysis used in listing work, applied from the buyer's side.

Deposit, which signals commitment and is at risk under defined circumstances the buyer should understand before he offers it.

Financing terms, including the down payment and loan type, and how each reads to a seller.

Contingencies and their periods — the protections and the timeline for exercising them.

Closing date and possession, which are frequently worth more to a seller than a small price difference.

Concessions requested, and how they interact with the appraisal.

Personal property, named explicitly rather than assumed.

6.3 What sellers actually weigh

Buyers assume price decides. Sellers weigh probability of closing alongside price, and a licensee who can explain that helps a buyer compete without simply bidding more:

Seller's concernWhat addresses it
Will this close?Financing strength, verified funds, a credible preapproval
When will it close?A closing date matched to the seller's needs
What can go wrong?Shorter or fewer contingencies — with the buyer understanding the exposure
Will the price hold?Appraisal gap coverage, or a larger down payment
Where do I go?Post-closing occupancy, where the seller needs it

6.4 Concessions and the appraisal

A point buyers regularly get wrong: a closing cost credit is not a price reduction for appraisal purposes. The property must still appraise at the contract price for the lender to lend against it.

A buyer offers $920,000 with a $15,000 credit rather than $905,000 with none. The seller's net is similar. But the appraisal must now support $920,000 rather than $905,000, and if it comes in at $900,000, the buyer faces a gap he may not have anticipated.

6.5 Multiple offers

What the buyer's licensee can and cannot rely on:

  • The seller decides which offer to accept, and price is one factor.
  • There is no rule requiring the highest offer to be accepted, and no priority based on the order offers were received.
  • A seller may, but need not, disclose competing terms — that is the seller's decision, made with their agent.
  • Terms represented about competing offers should be treated with care. Inventing or misdescribing a competing offer is a misrepresentation, and a buyer's licensee who suspects it should raise it with their broker rather than act on it.

6.6 Escalation clauses

Where a buyer proposes an escalation clause — an automatic increase over a competing offer up to a cap — the licensee should ensure he understands the mechanics and the exposures: what evidence of the competing offer is required, how the cap works, whether the seller's side will accept the structure at all, and that the escalated price still must appraise.

Whether to use one is the buyer's decision. Whether the buyer understands it is the licensee's responsibility.

6.7 Documenting the decision

The file should show that the terms were the buyer's informed choice:

  1. The market analysis supporting the price advice.
  2. The written advice about contingencies, waivers, and their consequences.
  3. The buyer's decisions, and any instruction that departed from the advice.
  4. The offer as submitted, with the date and time of submission and delivery evidence.
  5. Communications about competing offers, and their sources.

6.8 After acceptance

The moment of acceptance starts the clock on everything that follows, and the buyer's licensee should immediately:

  • Confirm the fully executed contract and distribute it.
  • Build the deadline calendar from the contract's own terms and share it with the buyer in writing.
  • Confirm the deposit is delivered per the contract.
  • Open the investigation work early rather than at the end of the period.
  • Warn the buyer, in writing, about wire fraud before any funds move — and that wire instructions will never be changed by email.

6.9 When the offer is not accepted

Most offers do not become transactions, and the practices around an unaccepted offer are where buyer-side files are thinnest.

What happens to the offer. A rejected or expired offer is part of the buyer's file. It records what was offered, when, and on what advice — which is what answers a later question about whether the licensee's advice was sound. A file containing only the accepted offer shows a buyer who made one decision, when in fact he made eight.

Backup position. Where the buyer wants to remain in position behind an accepted offer, the arrangement is a contractual one with real consequences: what triggers it, what happens to the deposit, and what obligations run in the meantime. These are contract terms, and the buyer's questions about their legal effect go to counsel. The licensee's role is to make sure the buyer knows he is entering an agreement rather than expressing interest.

Feedback and information. A buyer's agent frequently learns why the offer failed — price, terms, timing, or another offer's structure. What may be repeated is limited by what the listing side was permitted to say. A buyer's agent who is told the winning number by a listing agent who should not have said it has received information whose disclosure was a breach; Lesson 03's second worked audit addresses what to do with it.

Re-engagement. Properties come back. A transaction that fails after inspection or appraisal returns to the market, and the buyer who offered before is the most informed buyer available. Track it, and note that the property's return is itself information — a buyer should know that a property came back and, where it is knowable, why.

What the file shows after an unaccepted offer:

  1. The offer as submitted, with its date and expiration.
  2. The advice given about strategy, and the buyer's decision where it differed.
  3. The response — rejection, counter, expiration, or silence — with its date.
  4. Any backup agreement, with its terms.
  5. What was learned, and from whom.

The habit that matters: the file is updated when the offer fails, not when a later one succeeds. An unaccepted offer reconstructed months afterward from an inbox is the same problem as every other reconstruction in this course — and unlike the others, this one is trivially avoidable, because the offer document already exists and only needs to be filed.

6.10 Checkpoint

You should be able to build an offer strategy that competes on terms rather than price alone, explain why a credit does not reduce the appraisal requirement, and list what the file must contain to show the terms were the buyer's informed decision.

Key terms

TermDefinition
Buyer consultationThe meeting establishing objectives, constraints, and financial position before any property is shown. Also sets the professional relationship before the emotional part of house-hunting begins.
Stated criterion versus purpose"Three bedrooms" is a proxy; the purpose behind it is the actual specification. Knowing the purpose changes which properties qualify.
Decision processWho actually decides. A buyer whose spouse has not seen the property, or whose parent supplies funds, has decision-makers not in the room.
Buyer representation agreementA contract between the buyer and the brokerage engaging it to represent the buyer, on negotiable terms.
ExclusivityThe term binding the buyer to the brokerage within the agreed scope and term — including for a property the buyer finds himself. The term buyers least expect and the one that generates complaints.
ScopeThe geographic area, property type, and price range the agreement covers. A narrow scope leaves the buyer free outside it.
Source of paymentWho pays the buyer's agent. Does not determine representation — a buyer's agent compensated from the seller's side still represents the buyer.
PrequalificationA lender's preliminary assessment on largely stated information. Establishes a rough range; verifies nothing.
PreapprovalA lender's review of supporting information. Establishes a likely amount, subject to conditions. Not a commitment to lend.
Loan commitmentA conditional or firm commitment after underwriting. Substantially more certainty, and still not immunity from changed circumstances.
The three numbersWhat the buyer can borrow, what cash is available, and what the buyer is willing to spend. The third should drive the search.
Gift versus loanFamily assistance treated differently by the lender. An undisclosed loan is a hidden obligation that undermines the capacity analysis.
SteeringLetting the licensee's judgment about where a buyer belongs shape which properties he sees or how they are presented. Defined by whose criteria drove the selection, not by intent.
Assumed suitabilityDeciding on a buyer's behalf that a property "won't work" for him and not showing it. Steering, committed with good intentions.
Declining to characterizeThe professional skill of answering a neighborhood question by refusing the characterization, giving an honest reason, and pointing to a source the buyer can use himself.
Visual inspection dutyThe licensee's own reasonably competent and diligent visual inspection of accessible areas, with disclosure of material facts it reveals. Bounded, and independent of the seller's disclosure and of any professional inspection.
Matched investigationInvestigation recommendations driven by this property's features rather than recited generically.
The limit of the licenseThe licensee advises investigation and discloses observations; the licensee does not evaluate conditions, assess repairs, estimate costs, or advise whether a finding is serious.
Contingency waiverThe buyer's decision to give up a contractual protection. The licensee's obligation is to make it informed in writing — neither refusing to write the offer nor writing it without comment.
ConcessionA credit or other term requested from the seller. Not a price reduction for appraisal purposes.
Escalation clauseAn automatic increase over a competing offer up to a cap. Whether to use one is the buyer's decision; whether he understands it is the licensee's responsibility.
Probability of closingWhat sellers weigh alongside price. The lever that lets a buyer compete on terms rather than only on money.

Practice problems

Problem 1

A buyer says he needs three bedrooms in a specific district, under $850,000, within sixty days. At week six the licensee learns that the third bedroom is for the buyer's mother, who uses a walker, and that the sixty days is driven by a lease that cannot be extended.

What did the consultation fail to establish, and what changes?

Solution. Both the purpose behind the criterion and the nature of the constraint.

"Three bedrooms" was a proxy. The actual specification is single-story or a ground-floor bedroom with accessible bathroom access, and proximity to care — which disqualifies properties that met the stated criterion and qualifies some that failed it.

The constraint is equally consequential: a lease that cannot be extended makes the sixty days hard rather than a preference, which changes the offer strategy and the contingency periods.

What transfers: a constraint the licensee learns in week six was a constraint in week one. The consultation is where "what's your budget?" is replaced by "what monthly payment are you comfortable with?" and "how many bedrooms?" by "who will be living there, and how will each room be used?"

Problem 2

A licensee tells a buyer that a representation agreement is "just so I can show you properties" and that state law requires one before showings. The buyer signs. Six weeks later he buys a home he found himself at an open house in the same area and price range, and objects to the compensation claim.

Identify both defects.

Solution. The law was misstated. A signed representation agreement before showing is a brokerage policy decision, not a legal requirement. What is not optional is the agency disclosure, which is required regardless of whether a representation agreement is signed. A licensee may state a policy as a policy; stating it as law is a misrepresentation.

Exclusivity was never explained. The honest version says plainly that within this area and price range and for this term the brokerage represents him — including a home he finds himself at an open house, and including if he works with another agent on it — and offers to shorten the term or narrow the scope if he would rather not commit that way.

What transfers: a buyer who hears that and signs has agreed. A buyer who signs after "it's just so I can show you properties" has not, and will say so later — as this one did.

Problem 3

A buyer presents a preapproval letter for $1.1 million. It states that no income or asset documentation was reviewed, lists six outstanding conditions, and expired four days ago. The buyer's comfortable monthly payment supports roughly $860,000, and a relative has offered $100,000 of help.

What should the licensee do with each fact?

Solution. Read the letter and tell the buyer what it establishes — which here is very little. Nothing was verified, six conditions remain, and it has expired. Say so plainly, because the seller's agent will read it exactly the same way, and an offer carrying it will be weighed accordingly.

Search to $860,000, not $1.1 million. What the buyer is willing to spend is usually below what he can borrow, and it is the number that should drive the search. Searching to the lender's maximum shows properties the buyer will feel unable to afford. Where the numbers conflict, the conflict is the conversation — held now, deliberately, rather than discovered at the offer stage.

The relative's $100,000: confirm with the lender first. Ask the buyer to confirm how the assistance would be treated and documented, do not build the search around the higher figure until that is confirmed, and do not contact the relative directly. A gift and a loan are treated differently, because an undisclosed loan is a hidden obligation that undermines the lender's capacity analysis.

Problem 4

A buyer asks his agent to describe "what kind of people live" in a neighborhood, whether it is safe, and which schools are good. The agent replies that he cannot discuss those topics and suggests they look at another area he thinks would suit the buyer better.

What is wrong, and what should each answer have been?

Solution. Two failures. The refusal was a dead end — the buyer has a real informational need and will get an answer somewhere, probably a worse one. And the redirection is steering: answering by showing rather than telling, which conveys the characterization the agent declined to state.

Each answer should do three things — decline the characterization, give an honest reason, and leave the buyer better equipped:

Who lives there: "I don't describe neighborhoods by who lives in them, and honestly my impression wouldn't be worth much. Here's census data and the city's own information so you can look at whatever matters to you."

Safety: "That isn't something I can characterize for you. Local law enforcement publishes incident data by area — here's where. I'd also visit at different times of day."

Schools: "Evaluate them against your own priorities. The district publishes performance data — here's the link. I'd avoid ranking them, because what matters in a school varies a lot by family."

What transfers: answering "in general terms" is not a middle path. A general characterization is still a characterization.

Problem 5

A buyer with a mobility impairment asks about a two-story listing. His agent, believing the stairs will be a problem, does not schedule it and shows three single-story homes instead. He also follows up on some buyer inquiries within an hour and others in two days, and presents certain listings warmly and others flatly.

Classify each behavior.

Solution. All three are steering, and the first is the one committed with the best intentions.

Assumed suitability. The buyer decides what works for him; the licensee provides accurate information about the property — here, that access is by stairs with no elevator. Deciding on his behalf and omitting the property is steering however kindly meant.

Differential effort. Prompt follow-up on some inquiries and slower on others allocates access unequally, and the distinction cannot be defended afterward in neutral terms.

Differential enthusiasm. Showing everything but presenting some properties warmly and others flatly shapes the selection just as effectively as declining to show them.

The defense is the record: the buyer's stated criteria, the search parameters used, the properties presented with dates, and the reason where a matching property was not shown. A list of properties shown without the criteria that produced it shows what happened without showing why.

Problem 6

A 1958 house sits on a downslope lot with a retaining wall, a converted garage, a septic system, and a pool. The buyer says a general home inspection is enough.

What should be recommended, and how should the licensee handle "the inspector flagged the electrical panel — is that a big deal?"

Solution. A general inspection is the baseline; the property calls for more. Systems and sewer or septic inspection, and given the age, lead-based paint and asbestos evaluation where applicable. Geotechnical or drainage evaluation for the slope and retaining wall. Permit records for the converted garage, which also bears on legal square footage. Pool equipment and safety compliance. Insurance availability and cost if the property is in a mapped fire, flood, or seismic area — obtained before the contingency expires, not after.

On the panel: "That's outside what I'm qualified to assess, and it's exactly the kind of finding worth having an electrician look at before your contingency expires. Would you like me to arrange that? I'll note in the file that we discussed it."

What must not be said is "those panels come up all the time, it's usually not serious" — an evaluation the licensee is not qualified to make, on which the buyer may rely.

What transfers: the licensee's three jobs are advise investigation, disclose what he knows and what his own visual inspection revealed, and refer beyond the limit of the license. Reviewing the seller's disclosure is not performing the visual inspection, and recommending an inspector does not discharge it.

Problem 7

In competition, a buyer wants to waive the inspection contingency. One licensee refuses to write the offer. Another writes it without comment.

Both are wrong. What is correct?

Solution. Refusing overstates the licensee's role — the terms are the buyer's decision. Writing it without comment abandons the advisory duty. The correct path is between them:

Explain in writing what protection the contingency provides and what waiving it exposes him to. Note that he may still investigate without the contingency — he simply has no contractual path to act on what he finds. Then follow his informed decision, and record it.

What transfers: the same division governs offer terms as governs listing price. The buyer decides; the licensee supplies the analysis, the risks, and the options, and the file shows the decision was informed.

Problem 8

A buyer is choosing between offering $920,000 with a $15,000 closing cost credit and $905,000 with none. He assumes the seller sees them as equivalent and that the credit reduces his exposure. He also assumes the highest offer must be accepted.

Correct each assumption.

Solution. The credit is not a price reduction for appraisal purposes. The property must still appraise at $920,000 for the lender to lend against it — against $905,000 on the other structure. If it comes in at $900,000, the gap on the first is $20,000 rather than $5,000, and it is the buyer's to cover.

The seller does not see them as equivalent, and price is not the only factor. There is no rule requiring the highest offer to be accepted and no priority by order of receipt. Sellers weigh probability of closing alongside price — financing strength and verified funds, a closing date matched to their needs, fewer or shorter contingencies, appraisal gap coverage, and post-closing occupancy where they need it. That is how a buyer competes on terms instead of only on money.

One more thing worth saying at acceptance: build the deadline calendar from the contract, confirm the deposit is delivered per its terms, open the investigation work early, and warn the buyer in writing about wire fraud before any funds move — including that wire instructions will never be changed by email.

Chapter summary

The consultation comes before any showing. Objectives, constraints, financial position, decision process, and expectations about the sequence. The stated criterion is a proxy; the purpose behind it is the specification.

Agency disclosure belongs at the consultation, because agency can arise from conduct. Disclose, then discuss representation, then search.

The representation agreement is the brokerage's contract and is negotiable. Exclusivity is the term buyers least expect, and explaining it honestly is what makes it enforceable in practice. The source of compensation does not determine who is represented.

Prequalification, preapproval, and commitment are three different things, and none guarantees funding. Read the letter rather than filing it.

Three numbers, and the search follows the third: what he can borrow, what cash exists, and what he is willing to spend.

The buyer sets the criteria; the licensee applies them. Steering is defined by whose criteria drove the selection — which is why assumed suitability, differential effort, and differential enthusiasm are steering despite good intentions.

Decline characterizations well: refuse, give an honest reason, and point to a source the buyer can use. Refusing flatly and answering "in general terms" both fail.

The visual inspection duty is the licensee's own — bounded to accessible areas and visual observation, and independent of both the seller's disclosure and any professional inspection.

Match investigation to the property. Age, slope, conversions, well and septic, pool, mapped hazard areas, and common interest developments each call for something specific.

Do not evaluate findings. Confirm the buyer engaged with the report, help him identify which findings warrant a specialist, track the deadline, and document what was recommended and decided.

Terms are the buyer's; advice is the licensee's — including on waivers, escalation clauses, and concessions. A credit does not reduce what the appraisal must support, and sellers weigh probability of closing alongside price.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. Identify the buyer's stated criteria and the purpose behind each, the financial facts that require confirmation and from whom, the questions you would decline to answer and how you would respond instead, the investigation you would recommend for this property, the offer terms you would advise and why, and the evidence you would retain. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Practice - Lesson Assignment 08

Purchase agreements, counteroffers, and contingencies

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Identify the structural components of a California residential purchase agreement and explain what each governs.
  • Apply offer, acceptance, and counteroffer rules to determine whether a contract exists and on whose terms.
  • Explain what a contingency does inside a formed contract, and how the standard California form removes one.
  • Build a deadline calendar from a contract and apply the notice mechanism the contract requires.
  • Distinguish a permissible refusal to disclose competing offer terms from a misrepresentation about them.
  • Document amendments, cancellations, and notices so that a later dispute about timing can be answered.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: agreement architecture and offer formation55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: multiple offers and contingency mechanics50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: deadlines, notices, and dispute prevention45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. California purchase agreement architecture

1.1 What the agreement is

The residential purchase agreement is the operative contract of the transaction. Everything that follows — escrow instructions, lender documents, disclosures, notices — implements it or reports on it. Where escrow's instructions and the purchase agreement conflict, the parties' agreement governs, and the discrepancy is reconciled by the parties in writing rather than by escrow deciding.

Licensees who treat the agreement as a form to be filled rather than a contract to be understood cannot answer their client's questions about their own transaction, and cannot recognize when a proposed change matters.

1.2 The functional components

Rather than memorizing a form's paragraph numbers, learn what any residential purchase agreement must address:

ComponentWhat it governs
Parties and propertyWho is bound, and what is being conveyed, including personal property
Price and depositThe consideration, and what the deposit secures
Financing termsThe loan the buyer will obtain, and the down payment
Escrow and titleWho holds, who insures, and the form of title policy
Allocation of costsWhich party pays which closing costs
ContingenciesThe conditions qualifying the buyer's obligation, and their periods
Disclosure obligationsWhat the seller must deliver, and when
Investigation rightsThe buyer's right to inspect, and the seller's obligation to provide access
Repairs and creditsWhether any are agreed, and how they are performed
Closing and possessionWhen title transfers, and when the buyer takes possession
Notices and deliveryHow communications that carry legal effect must be made
Default and remediesWhat happens if a party fails to perform, including liquidated damages
Dispute resolutionMediation and arbitration provisions
Agency confirmationThe relationships elected

1.3 The deposit

The deposit — earnest money — performs two functions: it signals commitment, and it is the fund from which a seller's remedy is typically drawn if the buyer defaults.

Points the buyer must understand before he offers it:

  • Where it goes. To escrow, per the contract's terms, within the time the contract specifies.
  • When it is at risk. Not on any change of mind — a buyer acting within a contingency generally has a path to recover it. Once contingencies are removed, the exposure changes materially.
  • Liquidated damages. Where the parties agree in advance that a specified amount will be the seller's recovery on default, that provision must be properly agreed, and its enforceability is constrained by law. It is not a penalty the seller may simply impose.

1.4 What conveys

Fixtures generally convey; personal property generally does not. Because the fixture tests are difficult to apply after the fact, the professional practice is to make them unnecessary by naming the disputed items.

Items worth naming explicitly in most transactions: appliances, window coverings, mounted televisions and their brackets, chandeliers and light fixtures, above-ground spas, storage sheds and outbuildings, security and smart-home equipment, water treatment systems, and anything the seller has said they intend to take.

1.5 Time is a term

Nearly every obligation in the agreement carries a date or a period, and those dates are terms of the contract rather than administrative targets. A licensee who treats a closing date as a scheduling preference will eventually explain to a client why the other party was entitled to act.

1.6 What the agreement does not do

A purchase agreement is comprehensive about the things it addresses and silent about a great many things the parties assume it covers. Most transaction disputes live in that silence, and a licensee who knows where it falls can close it before it becomes an argument.

The parties assumeThe agreement's actual position
"The seller will fix everything on the inspection."It says nothing of the kind unless a repair term was negotiated and written. An inspection contingency is a right to investigate and respond, not a promise of repair.
"Obviously the appliances stay."What conveys is what the agreement says conveys, plus what the law treats as part of the real property. A washer and dryer are personal property until an agreement makes them otherwise.
"We agreed on the closing date in the email."The operative date is the one in the agreement, amended in writing. Section 5.5 governs.
"The seller said he'd leave the patio furniture."An oral side agreement about personal property, unrecorded anywhere, discovered at walkthrough.
"Possession is at closing."Only if the agreement says so. Delayed possession and rent-back are terms, and each carries insurance, liability, and condition questions.
"The warranty is included."A negotiated term, and one that is frequently discussed and not written.
"They're taking the solar with them."Depends on ownership, lease, or financing arrangement — a question with a documentary answer that must be obtained rather than assumed.

Three of these produce the most walkthrough failures: what conveys, possession, and anything the parties handled in conversation.

The licensee's practice is a habit rather than a rule. At the point the offer is written, run the list of assumptions out loud with the client: what stays, when possession happens, what the repair expectation is, and whether anything was discussed that is not on the page. Whatever comes out of that conversation goes into the offer or into a written amendment, and whatever does not go in does not exist.

The sentence that prevents most of them: "If it matters to you and it isn't written down, it isn't part of the deal." It is not a legal opinion, it is accurate, and clients understand it immediately.

2. Offer presentation and formation

2.1 The formation rules

Three rules resolve most questions:

  1. An offer is a proposal that, if accepted without change, forms a contract.
  2. An acceptance must match the offer. Any change to a material term is not an acceptance.
  3. Acceptance must be communicated to the offeror in the manner the offer or the law contemplates. A signed acceptance sitting in a drawer has not been communicated.

2.2 The counteroffer

When a seller changes the price and the closing date and signs, the effect is precise and is worth stating in full:

The counteroffer rejects the original offer and substitutes a new one. The original offer can no longer be accepted unless it is renewed. The buyer may accept, reject, or counter again.

Two consequences licensees regularly get wrong:

  • It is not an "acceptance with modifications" that forms a contract on the seller's terms.
  • It does not suspend the original offer, leaving it available if the counteroffer is rejected. The original is gone.

The same applies in the other direction. A buyer who signs a counteroffer but writes a change on it before returning it has made a further counteroffer, however small the change. Signing does not convert a modification into an acceptance.

2.3 Revocation and expiration

An offer may generally be revoked before acceptance, even where it states a deadline — unless it is supported by consideration as an option. A stated expiration sets an outer limit; it does not make the offer irrevocable in the meantime.

Practical consequence for a buyer's licensee: an offer stated to be open until Friday can be withdrawn Wednesday, and a buyer who is relying on the deadline should know that. Practical consequence for a seller's licensee: a seller intending to accept should not assume the offer will still be there.

2.4 Presenting offers

The seller's licensee presents offers; the seller decides. Within that:

  • All offers are presented unless the seller has lawfully instructed otherwise, and the licensee should understand and document any such instruction.
  • The presentation is accurate. Terms are described as they are, including terms unfavorable to a quick decision.
  • The licensee advises, including on the terms that make one offer more likely to close than another, rather than presenting price alone.
  • The seller's decision is the seller's, including a decision the licensee considers unwise.

2.5 Electronic signature and delivery

Electronic signature is ordinary in California residential practice. Two points matter:

  1. Delivery still has to happen and still has to be evidenced. An executed document in a signing platform that no one delivered has not been delivered, and the platform's audit trail is frequently the best evidence of when it was.
  2. The contract's own notice provisions govern what constitutes delivery for a notice that carries legal effect. Delivery by a method the contract does not authorize may not count.

2.6 A worked formation problem

The sequence.

  • Monday 9:00 a.m. Buyer signs an offer with an expiration of Wednesday at 5:00 p.m. It is delivered to the listing agent at 9:20.
  • Tuesday 11:00 a.m. The seller signs a counteroffer changing price and closing date, with its own expiration Thursday at 5:00 p.m. The listing agent puts it in her outbox and does not send it.
  • Tuesday 3:00 p.m. A second buyer's offer arrives, better on price.
  • Tuesday 4:00 p.m. The seller tells the listing agent he wants to withdraw the counteroffer and work with the second buyer.
  • Tuesday 4:30 p.m. The listing agent sends the first counteroffer by mistake — a scheduled send she forgot to cancel.
  • Tuesday 4:45 p.m. The first buyer signs the counteroffer and returns it immediately.

The questions this raises, and they are contract-law questions on which the parties will need counsel — the licensee's job is to recognize them, preserve the record, and escalate, not to declare who has a deal.

1. Was the counteroffer effective before it was sent? A counteroffer signed and sitting in an outbox has not been communicated. Section 2.1's formation rules turn on communication rather than on signature, which is why the 4:30 transmission matters more than the 11:00 signature.

2. Did the seller's 4:00 instruction revoke it? Revocation generally must be communicated to be effective, and at 4:00 the counteroffer had not been communicated to anyone. Whether an instruction to withdraw something that was never sent has any operative effect is precisely the kind of question that goes to counsel.

3. What did the 4:30 send do? The listing agent transmitted a document her principal had told her thirty minutes earlier not to send. That is a serious problem for the listing agent independent of the contract question — Lesson 03's obedience duty is engaged, and this is a broker matter immediately.

4. What is the first buyer's position? He received a counteroffer, within its stated period, and accepted it. From his side the sequence looks ordinary. Nothing about the listing brokerage's internal failure is visible to him, which is why the situation is dangerous rather than merely awkward.

What both licensees do now.

Preserve everything immediately — the send record with its timestamp, the 4:00 instruction and how it was given, the scheduling configuration that produced the send, and every message in both directions. Nothing is deleted, unsent, or recalled. An attempted recall is itself an act on the record.

Notify the brokers. Both of them, now.

Say nothing to either buyer about whether a contract exists. "I don't know, and my broker and counsel are addressing it" is the accurate answer, and the buyer is better served by it than by a confident wrong one.

Do not present the second offer to the seller as though the first sequence had not happened, and do not accept it, until the brokerage knows where it stands.

The preventable failure is not the mistake. It is the automation. A scheduled transmission of a document whose authorization can change is a system that acts without a person, and the first thing the broker should change is that documents go out when someone sends them.

2.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to take a sequence of signed documents and say at which point, if any, a contract was formed and on whose terms.

3. Counteroffers and multiple offers

3.1 How multiple offers actually work

Three rules that dispose of most buyer and seller misconceptions:

  1. The seller chooses. There is no obligation to accept the highest offer.
  2. There is no priority by order of receipt. An earlier offer has no claim over a later one.
  3. The seller decides whether competing terms are disclosed. Disclosure is the seller's call, made with their agent, not a right the competing buyers hold.

3.2 Countering multiple offers

A seller may counter more than one offer at once. Doing so creates a real hazard the licensee must manage: if two counteroffers are accepted, the seller may be exposed on both.

The customary protection is a provision making the seller's obligation contingent on the seller's subsequent written acceptance of the buyer's acceptance — so the seller retains a final election. A licensee who counters multiple offers without that protection has exposed the client.

3.3 The line between confidentiality and misrepresentation

This is the most consequential distinction in the section.

ConductAssessment
Declining to disclose competing offer termsPermissible. The seller decides.
Saying "there are multiple offers" when there arePermissible and accurate
Saying "there are multiple offers" when there are notMisrepresentation
Describing a competing offer's terms accurately, with the seller's authorizationPermissible
Inventing a competing offer's terms to raise the priceMisrepresentation
Describing an anticipated offer as an existing oneMisrepresentation
Disclosing terms without the seller's authorizationBreach of duty to the seller

Note that two of these fail in opposite directions: inventing competition breaches the duty of honesty owed to the buyer; disclosing without authority breaches the duty of confidentiality owed to the seller. A licensee must satisfy both at once.

A client's instruction cannot authorize a misrepresentation. A seller who directs the agent to say there are three offers when there are none has asked for something the agent must refuse.

3.4 Backup offers

A backup offer is a contract that becomes primary if the first transaction fails, in the position and on the terms agreed. It must be documented as such — a buyer who believes he is "next in line" without a written backup agreement has nothing.

The seller's licensee should ensure the primary buyer's rights are not compromised and that the backup buyer understands his position, including whether and when his deposit is at risk.

3.5 Advising a buyer in competition

Useful advice competes on terms rather than only on price — financing strength, timeline, contingency periods, occupancy — and is honest about the exposures each concession creates. Advice that is not useful: encouraging a buyer to waive protections without explaining what he is giving up, or presenting an escalation clause without ensuring he understands its mechanics and that the escalated price must still appraise.

4. Contingency mechanics

4.1 What a contingency is

A contingency conditions a party's obligation on a defined event or approval, inside a contract that has already been formed. Three things it is not:

  • It does not delay formation. The contract exists.
  • It is not a general right to cancel for any reason.
  • It does not transfer the underlying responsibility to the other party.

4.2 The common residential contingencies

ContingencyWhat it protectsTypical resolution
Investigation / inspectionThe buyer's ability to evaluate conditionRemoved after investigation, or used to request repairs or cancel
LoanThe buyer's ability to obtain the financing describedRemoved on loan approval, or used to cancel if denied
AppraisalThe buyer's exposure if value does not support the priceRemoved, or used to renegotiate or cancel
Sale of buyer's propertyThe buyer's dependence on another closingRemoved on that closing, subject to the agreed terms
Review of documentsTime to review disclosures, reports, HOA documentsRemoved after review
Title reviewThe buyer's ability to evaluate title exceptionsRemoved after review of the preliminary report

4.3 How removal actually works

This is the single most misunderstood mechanic in California residential practice, and getting it wrong produces confident, wrong advice on both sides.

Under the standard California residential purchase agreement form, contingencies remain in effect until the buyer removes them in writing. They do not expire automatically when the date passes.

Consequences:

  • A buyer who lets the date pass without delivering a removal has not removed the contingency.
  • A seller cannot treat the transaction as firm merely because the date has passed.
  • The seller's route is to deliver the notice the contract requires, giving the buyer a defined period to act, after which the seller may cancel per the contract.

The habit to build is more durable than the rule: read the contract's own removal mechanism rather than assuming deadlines are self-executing. Different forms and negotiated terms can operate differently, and the contract in front of you governs.

4.4 A worked sequence

The buyer's investigation contingency period ends on the 17th. Nothing is delivered.

On the 18th, what is true? The contingency remains in effect. The buyer has not removed it. The seller is not free to sell to someone else.

What may the seller do? Deliver the contractual notice requiring the buyer to act, which starts the period the contract specifies.

What if the buyer removes the contingency during that period? The notice is satisfied and the transaction continues.

What if the buyer does not? The seller may cancel per the contract's procedure.

What is not true at any point? That the contract became void, that the buyer automatically became committed, or that the seller could cancel on the 18th without notice.

4.5 Cancelling under a contingency

A contingency provides a path, and the path has requirements. A buyer whose loan is denied while the loan contingency is in effect must follow the contract's cancellation procedure — the written notice it requires, within its terms, with any supporting documentation.

What forfeits the protection: notifying the other agent verbally, instructing escrow unilaterally to return the deposit, or simply ceasing to perform and letting the seller cancel for non-performance.

4.6 Waiver and its consequences

A buyer may waive a contingency, and in competitive markets many do. Two points the buyer must understand:

  1. He may still investigate. Waiving the contingency removes the contractual path to act on what he finds; it does not prevent him from looking.
  2. The exposure is concrete. Where the appraisal contingency is waived and the appraisal comes in low, the buyer's options narrow to bringing funds, renegotiating without leverage, or defaulting — with his deposit exposed.

The licensee's obligation is to put that in writing before the buyer decides, then follow his decision.

4.7 Contingencies under competitive pressure

In a market where sellers receive several offers, buyers are advised — often by other buyers, sometimes by licensees — to shorten or remove contingencies to compete. This is a real strategy with real consequences, and the licensee's obligation is that the buyer understands the consequence before he chooses it.

What each shortening actually costs:

The moveWhat the buyer gives up
Shortening the investigation periodTime to obtain the specialist evaluations a general inspection recommends. The evaluations do not get faster because the period got shorter.
Waiving investigation entirelyThe right to respond to what he finds — and Section 4.6 establishes that the property's condition is unchanged by the waiver.
Waiving the appraisal contingencyThe obligation to cover a shortfall in cash, in an amount he cannot know in advance. This is the waiver whose cost is most often misunderstood, because it is stated as a risk and experienced as a bill.
Waiving the loan contingencyExposure of the deposit — and potentially more — if financing fails for a reason outside his control.
Shortening the closing periodA deadline his own lender may not be able to meet, converting his aggressiveness into his default.
Increasing the depositMore money at risk under every scenario above.

The licensee's role here has three parts and one prohibition.

Explain the mechanism with numbers. "Waiving the appraisal contingency means if it appraises at 40,000 below your price, you bring 40,000 more in cash or you are in breach." The number makes it real in a way the word "risk" does not.

Confirm the capacity is real. A buyer waiving an appraisal contingency should have the funds, and the licensee should know he has told his lender what he is doing. A waiver the buyer cannot perform is a default with a delay built in.

Document the decision. Section 6.4 and Lesson 03's file requirements govern. The advice given, the consequence explained, and the buyer's decision — in writing, before the offer goes out.

The prohibition: the licensee does not recommend a waiver. The strategy belongs to the buyer, and a licensee whose file shows he advised waiving an appraisal contingency for a buyer who then could not perform has advised on a matter with financial consequences he does not carry.

One further point, easily missed. The listing side has an interest in these waivers, and a listing agent who tells buyers' agents that "non-contingent offers only" will be considered is communicating the seller's preference — which is legitimate — while also creating pressure on buyers to waive protections without advice. Where a licensee sees a buyer waiving under that pressure with no apparent understanding, the professional response is to make sure the buyer's own agent has explained it, not to assume someone did.

5. Deadlines, notices, and removal

5.1 Build the calendar from the contract

The first act after acceptance is to build a deadline calendar from the contract's own terms, not from habit or from what is usual. Every date, every period, and what triggers each one.

Then share it with the client in writing. A buyer who knows his investigation period ends on the 17th behaves differently from one who is told on the 16th.

5.2 How periods are counted

Contracts define how time is counted, and the definitions vary: calendar days versus business days, whether the triggering day counts, when a period ending on a weekend or holiday rolls, and what time of day a deadline falls.

Read the definitions in the contract in front of you. A licensee who assumes and is wrong by one day has cost a client a right.

5.3 Notices carry legal effect

The contract specifies how notices must be delivered because rights depend on whether and when delivery occurred. Consequences:

  • Use the method the contract authorizes. A text message is not delivery unless the contract makes it so.
  • Preserve the evidence. Delivery confirmation, platform audit trail, signed receipt — whatever the method produces.
  • An undated notice is a notice with no proof of timing, and timing is what disputes are about.

5.4 The common notices

NoticeFunction
Contingency removalThe buyer removes a specified contingency in writing
Notice to buyer to performThe seller requires the buyer to act within the contract's period
Notice to seller to performThe buyer requires the seller to act
Request for repairThe buyer requests repairs or credits following investigation
CancellationA party cancels under a right the contract provides
Demand to closeA party requires the other to close

Each has a form and a timing requirement in the contract. Sending the wrong one, or the right one by the wrong method, is a common and expensive error.

5.5 Amendments

Any change to a term of the contract — the closing date, the price, what conveys, an agreed repair — is made by written amendment signed by both parties and delivered to escrow so the instructions match the agreement.

What does not change the contract: an email between the agents, a verbal agreement between the parties, an instruction to escrow alone, or an informal understanding that a short extension "won't matter."

The parties agree during escrow to move the closing by a week. The agents confirm by email. Escrow is told. No amendment is signed.

If the transaction later fails, the parties are litigating on a contract whose closing date is the original one, with an email chain between non-parties as the evidence of a change.

5.6 Tracking and confirming

Two habits prevent most deadline disputes:

  1. A written timeline with each contractual date, the notice each requires, and the evidence of what was delivered.
  2. Written status confirmation at each milestone — a short message confirming what has been delivered, what remains, and what the next date is.

Relying on escrow to track dates, on software reminders, or on verbal confirmation with the other agent may help the work happen, but none of them creates the record.

5.7 A worked calendar

The contract. Acceptance is communicated on a Friday. The agreement provides an investigation period of seventeen days, a loan contingency of twenty-one days, an appraisal contingency of seventeen days, delivery of association documents "within seven days," a seller disclosure delivery obligation with a response period running from delivery, and a closing thirty days from acceptance.

Building the calendar — the questions that must be answered before a single date is written.

1. What is day zero? Periods run from a defined starting event, and the agreement defines it. Assuming acceptance day is day one is the most common error, and it shifts every deadline in the file by one.

2. Are the periods in calendar days or business days? The agreement says which, and different periods within the same agreement can be counted differently.

3. What happens when a deadline lands on a weekend or holiday? The agreement addresses it, and the rule is not the same in every agreement.

4. Which periods run from delivery rather than from acceptance? The disclosure response period is the important one, and it cannot be calendared until delivery actually occurs. Section 5.2 governs, and this is why a file with an undated delivery has a deadline nobody can compute.

5. What is the time of day? A deadline at 5:00 p.m. and a deadline at 11:59 p.m. are different deadlines, and a removal delivered at 8:00 p.m. on the correct date may be late.

The calendar that results contains more entries than the contract has deadlines, because each deadline generates work that must happen before it:

  • Association documents ordered — the day after acceptance, not on the seventh day. The seven-day delivery obligation is the seller's; the buyer's review time is what remains after it.
  • Inspections scheduled — within the first three days, because Section 5.7 of Lesson 07 establishes that further-evaluation items need time inside the same period.
  • Specialist evaluations ordered — the moment the general report recommends them.
  • Appraisal ordered through the lender, with a follow-up date, because it is the item over which the buyer's side has the least control.
  • A check-in three days before each removal deadline, which is the entry that actually prevents the failure.
  • Delivery confirmations for every notice, recorded on the calendar as tasks rather than assumed.

The two failures this structure prevents. First, the deadline that arrives with the work not started — the association documents ordered on day six, the specialist called on day fifteen. Second, the removal delivered on time to the wrong place or with no proof, which Section 5.3 identifies as carrying legal effect and Section 5.6 requires be confirmed.

And the calendar is built the day acceptance is communicated, not later in the week. A calendar built on day four has already lost the three days it would have protected.

6. Amendment, cancellation, and dispute prevention

6.1 Cancellation is a procedure

A party cancels by exercising a right the contract provides, following the procedure the contract specifies. Cancellation is not accomplished by announcing an intention, by ceasing to perform, or by instructing escrow.

Where a cancellation is disputed, escrow's position is instructive: escrow may act only on mutual written instructions. Where the parties disagree, escrow stops. It does not decide between them, and it cannot release the deposit on one party's say-so.

6.2 The deposit in a disputed cancellation

Buyers and sellers both assume the deposit follows automatically from who was right. It does not. Escrow holds pending mutual instruction or a resolution through the contract's dispute resolution provisions or the courts.

The licensee's role is to explain the mechanism, not to predict the outcome or to advise on entitlement — that is a legal question.

6.3 Mediation and arbitration

The contract's dispute resolution provisions are substantive rights, and they are frequently initialed without explanation.
