A licensee should ensure the client understands, at minimum, that mediation is generally a required first step before other remedies, and that an arbitration provision, where agreed, affects how disputes will be resolved and what review is available.

Where a client asks what the provisions mean for their position, that is a question for counsel.

6.4 What actually prevents disputes

Most transaction disputes are not about substance. They are about whether something was delivered, to whom, and when — which is why the practices that prevent them are documentary rather than interpersonal:

  1. Build the deadline calendar from the contract and share it.
  2. Deliver notices by the authorized method and keep the evidence.
  3. Confirm materially important conversations in writing.
  4. Amend in writing, signed, and deliver to escrow.
  5. Correct errors immediately, in writing, to everyone who received the original.
  6. Keep superseded drafts, so the sequence of versions shows how terms changed.
  7. Audit the file at closing while memories are fresh.

6.5 A worked file audit

A closed file contains: an email agreeing to move the closing date, with no signed amendment; a contingency removal that is signed but undated; a transfer disclosure with two items left blank; and an agency confirmation naming a relationship different from the disclosure earlier in the file.

Assessment. Four defects, and they are not equivalent. The agency contradiction is the most serious, because it suggests the brokerage acted on both sides without documented consent. The undated removal is next: rights ran from it and the file cannot show when. The unsigned date change means the operative contract still says the original date. The blanks make the disclosure ambiguous about whether the items were addressed.

The correct response. Record each finding, escalate to the broker, and obtain what legitimately exists. What must not happen is dating the removal now, completing the blanks now, or producing an amendment now with a matching date. A documented gap is survivable. A backdated document is falsification, and it converts a compliance problem into conduct that ends a career.

6.6 A second audit — the transaction that did not close

The file. A purchase cancelled twenty-two days after acceptance. The buyer wants his deposit. The seller says the buyer defaulted. The file contains:

  • (a) The purchase agreement, fully executed, acceptance communicated by email on a Friday afternoon.
  • (b) An inspection report dated day twelve.
  • (c) A signed investigation contingency removal, undated, in the file with no transmittal.
  • (d) A text from the buyer's agent to the listing agent on day sixteen: "we're good on inspection."
  • (e) A request for repairs sent on day eighteen — after items (c) and (d).
  • (f) A cancellation signed by the buyer on day twenty-two, citing the loan contingency, with the loan contingency period stated in the agreement as twenty-one days.
  • (g) No delivery evidence for the seller's disclosures.

Ranked by consequence to the deposit:

1. (g) The missing delivery evidence. Rights run from delivery, and a file that cannot show when the disclosures reached the buyer cannot show that the buyer's response period ever started — which may mean it never closed. This item is first because it potentially changes the answer to everything below it, and it is the item nobody looks at because it is not about the cancellation.

2. (c) The undated removal. The single most consequential defect in the file's own terms. The contingency either was or was not removed before the events that followed, and the document cannot say. Section 5.3 and Lesson 03's first worked audit both rank an undated removal near the top for exactly this reason.

3. (f) The cancellation timing. Cited on day twenty-two under a contingency stated as twenty-one days. Whether the period had expired depends on the day-zero and counting questions in Section 5.7, which is why those questions are not clerical.

4. (e) The repair request after the removal. A request made after the contingency was apparently removed. It may have been a negotiation the seller was free to decline, or it may indicate the parties did not believe the contingency had been removed. It is evidence about what everyone understood, and it points in a different direction from item (c).

5. (d) The text. Informal, ambiguous, and now evidence. "We're good on inspection" is not a removal and was probably not intended as one, but it is dated, which is more than the removal is.

6. (b) The inspection at day twelve. Not a defect. It is here because it establishes the timeline the other items sit on.

What the licensees do now. Preserve everything including the texts. Escalate to both brokers immediately. Deposit disposition is a contractual and legal question with a defined procedure, and it is not resolved by the licensees' view of who was right. Section 6.2 governs and counsel decides.

What must not happen: dating the removal now, producing a delivery receipt now, or characterizing the text as a removal in a communication to the other side. Each is Lesson 03's manufactured document.

The lesson the brokerage should take. Four of these six items are the same failure: a document exists and its date does not. A transaction management process that refuses to accept an undated document — and that records delivery as an event rather than assuming it — would have prevented all four.

6.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to state how a term of the contract is changed, what escrow may do when the parties disagree, and why an undated notice is a defect even when everyone remembers what happened.

Key terms

TermDefinition
Purchase agreementThe operative contract of the transaction. Everything after it — escrow instructions, lender documents, disclosures, notices — implements it or reports on it.
Conflict with escrow instructionsResolved in favor of the parties' agreement, reconciled by the parties in writing. Escrow does not decide.
Deposit (earnest money)The fund signaling commitment and from which a seller's remedy is typically drawn on buyer default.
Liquidated damagesAn amount agreed in advance as the seller's recovery on default. Must be properly agreed, and its enforceability is constrained by law. Not a penalty the seller may simply impose.
Naming what conveysListing appliances, window coverings, mounted televisions, chandeliers, sheds, and smart-home equipment expressly, so the fixture tests never have to be applied after the fact.
OfferA proposal that, if accepted without change, forms a contract.
Mirror-image acceptanceAn acceptance must match the offer. Any change to a material term is not an acceptance.
Communication of acceptanceAcceptance must reach the offeror in the manner contemplated. A signed acceptance in a drawer has not been communicated.
CounterofferA rejection of the original offer and the substitution of a new one. The original is gone and cannot be accepted unless renewed.
RevocationWithdrawal of an offer before acceptance, generally permitted even where the offer states a deadline — unless supported by consideration as an option.
Seller's final electionThe provision making a seller's obligation contingent on subsequent written acceptance of the buyer's acceptance. The protection when countering multiple offers.
Backup offerA contract that becomes primary if the first transaction fails, in the position and on the terms agreed. Must be documented; "next in line" without one is nothing.
ContingencyA condition on a party's obligation inside a formed contract. Not a delay of formation, not a general right to cancel, not a transfer of responsibility.
Removal in writingUnder the standard California residential form, how a contingency ends. Contingencies do not expire automatically when the date passes.
Notice to buyer to performThe seller's route when a date passes without removal: a contractual notice starting a defined period, after which the seller may cancel per the contract.
WaiverGiving up a contingency. Removes the contractual path to act on what is found; does not prevent the buyer from investigating.
Counting of periodsHow the contract defines time — calendar or business days, whether the triggering day counts, weekend and holiday rolls, and the time of day a deadline falls.
Authorized delivery methodThe method the contract specifies for notices. A text message is not delivery unless the contract makes it so.
Undated noticeA notice with no proof of timing — and timing is what disputes are about.
AmendmentA change to a contract term, made by writing signed by both parties and delivered to escrow. Agent emails, verbal agreements, and escrow instructions alone do not amend.
CancellationExercising a right the contract provides, by the procedure it specifies. Not accomplished by announcing an intention, ceasing to perform, or instructing escrow.
Mutual instructionThe only basis on which escrow may act where parties disagree. Escrow stops; it does not decide, and it cannot release a deposit on one party's say-so.

Practice problems

Problem 1

A buyer offers $880,000 with a 21-day investigation period and a March 30 closing. The seller strikes the price and writes $895,000, changes closing to March 20, signs, and returns it. The buyer signs it, but crosses out March 20 and writes March 25 before sending it back. The seller then says he no longer wants to sell at $895,000 and would accept the original $880,000 offer instead.

Trace the formation. Is there a contract, and can the seller accept the original offer?

Solution. No contract, and no — the original offer is gone.

The seller's changes were a counteroffer, which rejected the $880,000 offer and substituted a new one. That is not an "acceptance with modifications" forming a contract on the seller's terms, and it does not suspend the original offer leaving it available later. The original cannot be accepted unless the buyer renews it.

The buyer's signature with March 25 written on it is a further counteroffer, however small the change. Signing does not convert a modification into an acceptance.

Where the parties stand: the buyer's counteroffer at $895,000 / March 25 is outstanding. The seller may accept it, reject it, or counter again — or ask the buyer to renew the original $880,000 terms, which the buyer is free to decline.

Problem 2

A buyer's offer states it is open for acceptance until 5:00 p.m. Friday. On Wednesday the buyer finds another property and withdraws it. On Thursday the seller signs the original offer and puts it in a file; his agent emails the signed copy to the buyer's agent on Friday morning.

Was a contract formed?

Solution. No, on two independent grounds.

The offer was revoked before acceptance. An offer may generally be revoked before acceptance even where it states a deadline, unless supported by consideration as an option. A stated expiration sets an outer limit; it does not make the offer irrevocable in the meantime.

And acceptance was not communicated on Thursday. A signed acceptance sitting in a file has not been communicated to the offeror — by Friday there was nothing left to accept.

What transfers, in both directions: a buyer relying on "open until Friday" should know it can be withdrawn Wednesday, and a seller intending to accept should not assume the offer will still be there.

Problem 3

A seller receives four offers. His agent counters three of them at $940,000 without any further provision. Two buyers accept.

What has happened, and what should have been done?

Solution. The seller may now be exposed on two contracts for one property, which is the hazard countering multiple offers creates.

The customary protection is a provision making the seller's obligation contingent on the seller's subsequent written acceptance of the buyer's acceptance — preserving a final election. A licensee who counters multiple offers without it has exposed the client.

Worth stating alongside it, because buyers assume otherwise: the seller chooses, there is no obligation to accept the highest offer, and there is no priority by order of receipt.

Problem 4

A seller instructs his agent to tell competing buyers there are three offers on the table. There is one. Separately, a buyer's agent asks the listing agent to reveal the competing offer's price, and the listing agent declines without asking the seller.

Assess both.

Solution. These fail in opposite directions, and the licensee must satisfy both duties at once.

The seller's instruction must be refused. Saying there are multiple offers when there are not is a misrepresentation, and a client's instruction cannot authorize one. Inventing competition breaches the duty of honesty owed to the buyer. The same is true of describing an anticipated offer as an existing one.

Declining to disclose the competing price is permissible — whether competing terms are disclosed is the seller's decision, not a right the competing buyers hold. But the agent should route it: the call belongs to the seller. Disclosing terms without the seller's authorization would breach confidentiality owed to the seller.

What transfers: permissible silence and affirmative falsehood are not near neighbors. One is the seller's prerogative; the other ends careers.

Problem 5

The buyer's investigation contingency period ends on the 17th. Nothing is delivered. On the 18th the seller's agent tells the seller the contingency expired, the buyer is now committed, and the seller may accept a better offer that arrived overnight.

Correct each statement.

Solution. All three are wrong, and they are the most confidently repeated errors in California residential practice.

The contingency did not expire. Under the standard form, contingencies remain in effect until the buyer removes them in writing. They are not self-executing on a date.

The buyer is not committed. He has not removed anything, so the protection is intact.

The seller is not free to sell elsewhere. The seller's route is to deliver the notice the contract requires, which starts the period the contract specifies. If the buyer removes during that period, the notice is satisfied and the transaction continues; if he does not, the seller may cancel per the contract's procedure.

What is not true at any point: that the contract became void, that the buyer automatically became committed, or that the seller could cancel on the 18th without notice.

The durable habit, more useful than the rule: read the contract's own removal mechanism. Different forms and negotiated terms operate differently, and the contract in front of you governs.

Problem 6

A buyer's loan is denied while the loan contingency is in effect. He tells the listing agent by phone, emails escrow asking for his deposit back, and stops responding to requests for documents.

What has he done wrong, and what does the deposit dispute look like?

Solution. He had a path and did not follow it. A contingency provides a route with requirements: the written notice the contract specifies, within its terms, with any supporting documentation.

Each of his three acts forfeits protection rather than exercising it. A verbal notification to the other agent is not the contractual notice. A unilateral instruction to escrow is not cancellation — escrow may act only on mutual written instructions, and where the parties disagree it stops. And simply ceasing to perform invites the seller to cancel for non-performance instead, which is a materially worse position.

On the deposit: it does not follow automatically from who was right. Escrow holds pending mutual instruction or resolution through the contract's dispute resolution provisions or the courts. The licensee explains that mechanism and does not predict the outcome or advise on entitlement, which is a legal question.

Problem 7

The parties agree during escrow to move closing back one week. The agents confirm by email, escrow is told, and everyone proceeds. The transaction later fails and the parties dispute who breached.

What is the operative closing date?

Solution. The original one. A change to a contract term is made by written amendment signed by both parties and delivered to escrow so the instructions match the agreement.

An email between the agents does not amend the contract — the agents are not the parties. Neither does a verbal agreement between the parties, an instruction to escrow alone, or a shared understanding that a short extension "won't matter."

The parties are now litigating on a contract whose closing date is the original one, with an email chain between non-parties as the evidence of a change.

What transfers: time is a term. A licensee who treats a closing date as a scheduling preference will eventually explain to a client why the other party was entitled to act.

Problem 8

A closed file contains: an email agreeing to move the closing date with no signed amendment; a contingency removal that is signed but undated; a transfer disclosure with two items blank; and an agency confirmation naming a relationship different from the disclosure earlier in the file.

Rank the defects and state the correct response.

Solution. The agency contradiction is the most serious, and different in kind: it suggests the brokerage acted on both sides without documented consent — a breach of duties actually owed rather than a paperwork gap.

The undated removal is next. Rights ran from it, and the file cannot show when. An undated notice is a notice with no proof of timing, and timing is what disputes are about — even when everyone remembers what happened.

The unsigned date change means the operative contract still says the original date. The blanks leave the disclosure ambiguous about whether those items were addressed at all.

The response: record each finding, escalate to the broker, and obtain what legitimately exists. What must not happen is dating the removal now, completing the blanks now, or producing an amendment now with a matching date. A documented gap is survivable; a backdated document is falsification, and it converts a compliance problem into conduct that ends a career.

Chapter summary

The purchase agreement is a contract to be understood, not a form to be filled. Where escrow's instructions conflict with it, the parties' agreement governs, reconciled by the parties in writing.

Learn the functional components — parties and property, price and deposit, financing, escrow and title, cost allocation, contingencies, disclosures, investigation rights, repairs, closing and possession, notices, default and remedies, dispute resolution, agency confirmation — rather than a form's paragraph numbers.

Name what conveys so the fixture tests never have to be applied after the fact.

Three formation rules dispose of most questions: an offer accepted without change forms a contract; an acceptance must match the offer; acceptance must be communicated.

A counteroffer rejects and replaces. The original offer is gone, not suspended, and a signature with a change written on it is a further counteroffer however small the change.

An offer may generally be revoked before acceptance, even one stating a deadline.

Counter multiple offers only with the seller's final election preserved, or the seller may end up bound twice.

Declining to disclose competing terms is the seller's prerogative; inventing them is a misrepresentation — and a client's instruction cannot authorize one. Disclosing without the seller's authority breaches the other duty. Both must be satisfied at once.

A contingency conditions an obligation inside a formed contract, and under the standard California form it ends only by written removal. Dates are not self-executing; the seller's route is the contractual notice to perform.

Waiver removes the path, not the ability to investigate, and the exposure should be put in writing before the buyer decides.

Build the deadline calendar from the contract, read its own definitions of how time is counted, deliver notices by the authorized method, and keep the evidence.

Amend in writing, signed, and deliver to escrow. Cancel by the contract's procedure. Escrow acts only on mutual instruction and never decides between the parties.

Most disputes are about whether something was delivered, to whom, and when — which is why the practices that prevent them are documentary rather than interpersonal.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. State whether a contract exists and on whose terms, identify each contingency in effect and what would be required to remove it, build the deadline calendar from the contract's terms, identify the notice each next step requires and its delivery method, and state what evidence you would retain. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Practice - Lesson Assignment 09

Disclosures and property-condition risk

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Explain the seller's transfer disclosure obligation and apply the materiality standard to specific conditions.
  • Perform and record the licensee's own visual inspection, and distinguish it from the seller's disclosure and from a professional inspection.
  • Identify when natural hazard, environmental, and age-triggered disclosures apply and what each obligates.
  • Apply the limits on disclosing a prior occupant's death or illness, and explain why those limits exist.
  • Distinguish a material fact from a stigma or opinion that must not be represented as fact.
  • Sequence disclosure delivery correctly and explain the consequence of late delivery.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: transfer disclosure and the visual inspection duty55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: hazard, environmental, and age-triggered disclosures50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: material facts, stigma limits, and delivery timing45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Transfer disclosure framework

1.1 The obligation

A seller of residential property must disclose known material facts affecting the value or desirability of the property. In California this is delivered principally through a statutory transfer disclosure statement completed by the seller, supplemented by other disclosures the property, its age, and its location require.

Two features define the obligation and resolve most questions about it:

  1. It is about what the seller knows. The seller is not required to investigate or to become an expert. The seller is required to disclose what they actually know.
  2. It is measured by materiality to a reasonable buyer, not by the seller's own view of importance.

1.2 The materiality standard

A fact is material if it would affect a reasonable buyer's decision to buy or the price they would pay.

Apply that standard and the recurring seller objections resolve themselves:

Seller's positionAnalysis
"It's fixed, so there's nothing to disclose."The repair's adequacy is exactly what a buyer wants to evaluate. Disclose the condition and the repair.
"It only happens in heavy rain."Frequency is not the test. A drainage problem that appears three times a year is material.
"The buyer could see it himself."Obviousness is not the test. Assuming the buyer noticed is how disputes begin.
"Nobody asked."Disclosure is not question-driven.
"It's not a problem for me."The standard is a reasonable buyer, not this seller.
"It happened before I owned it."If the seller knows it, the seller discloses it.

1.3 Completing the disclosure

Four rules, each of which prevents a specific failure:

  1. The seller completes the seller's disclosure. A licensee who fills it in on the seller's behalf misattributes the statement and may create a misrepresentation attributed to the seller.
  2. Every item is answered. A blank is ambiguous; "unknown" is an honest, available, and complete answer.
  3. Uncertain items are not answered "no." Marking an item "no" asserts knowledge the seller does not have.
  4. Amend when new information arrives. A disclosure is a statement of what the seller knows, and knowledge acquired during the transaction updates it.

1.4 Counseling the reluctant seller

The counseling argument that works is not moral — it is practical, and it is true:

"Undisclosed conditions are far more expensive than disclosed ones. Disclosed, this is a fact the buyer prices in and moves past. Undisclosed and discovered during inspection, it becomes a renegotiation with your credibility gone. Undisclosed and discovered after closing, it becomes a claim. The only version where this costs you least is the one where you disclose it now, with the repair records."

1.5 What the licensee does with the seller's disclosure

The licensee's obligations regarding the seller's disclosure are specific:

  • Ensure it is delivered to the buyer, and record when.
  • Do not alter it or complete it on the seller's behalf.
  • Do not suppress it, and do not follow an instruction to conceal a material condition.
  • Where the licensee knows something the disclosure omits, raise it with the seller and the broker. The licensee's own knowledge creates the licensee's own obligation.

That last point is the one that separates a compliant licensee from a complicit one. A licensee who knows of a material condition the seller has omitted, and delivers the disclosure without addressing it, has adopted the omission.

1.6 When the seller cannot complete the disclosure

The framework assumes a seller who has lived in the property and knows things about it. A significant share of transactions do not have one.

The sellerThe problem
A trustee or personal representativeHas authority to convey and may have never entered the property
A successor who inheritedKnows the house socially and not structurally, and may be confusing childhood memory with fact
An institutional owner after foreclosureHas no occupancy knowledge at all
An investor who bought, held, and never occupiedKnows what he did to it and nothing about what preceded him
An owner who has been away for yearsKnows the property as it was, not as it is
A seller in a dissolutionMay have knowledge that is genuinely disputed between two people who each have to sign

What does not change. The obligation is about known material facts, and a seller who genuinely does not know something answers accordingly. "Unknown" is an honest and available answer, as Section 1.3 establishes, and it is the correct one far more often in these transactions than in an ordinary owner-occupied sale.

What changes is everything around it.

The exemption question is a legal one. Certain transfers are treated differently under the disclosure framework, and which exemptions apply to a given transfer is a question for counsel and the broker, not a determination the licensee makes from the seller's description of how he acquired the property. A licensee who concludes "this is a trust sale so the disclosure isn't required" has answered a legal question.

An exemption from one requirement is not an exemption from everything. The most consequential error in this area is treating an exemption as a general release. The obligation not to conceal known material facts, the obligation of honesty, and the licensee's own independent obligations do not disappear because a particular form is not required of a particular seller.

The licensee's own duty is unaffected and becomes more important. Section 2.1 establishes it as independent. Where the seller's disclosure is thin because the seller genuinely knows little, the licensee's own observations are a larger share of what the buyer receives — and the buyer's need for independent investigation is correspondingly greater.

The counseling move is to say all of this to the buyer plainly and early: this seller has limited knowledge of the property, the disclosure will reflect that, and the investigation contingency is carrying more weight in this transaction than it usually does.

What must not happen: a disclosure completed by a licensee, a family member, or anyone other than the seller, filling in from what "everybody knows about the house." Section 1.3 forbids it and the reason is that the resulting document misattributes a statement — it tells the buyer the seller said something the seller did not say.

2. Agent visual inspection duties

2.1 An independent obligation

In a residential transaction the licensee conducts a reasonably competent and diligent visual inspection of the reasonably and normally accessible areas and discloses material facts it reveals.

This is the licensee's own duty. It is not satisfied by:

  • Reviewing the seller's disclosure.
  • Recommending that the buyer hire an inspector.
  • The buyer's own agent conducting one.
  • The presence of a professional inspection report.

2.2 What the duty includes and excludes

IncludedExcluded
Areas reasonably and normally accessibleAreas requiring moving belongings or furniture
Visual observationOpening walls, floors, or ceilings
Conditions apparent on inspectionOperating systems to test them
The interior and accessible exteriorRoofs, crawl spaces, or areas not reasonably accessible
What a competent licensee would noticeWhat only a specialist would recognize

The licensee is not an inspector. The licensee is a professional expected to notice what a diligent professional would notice, and to say so.

2.3 What to record

Record observations rather than conclusions, and record them whether or not their significance is clear:

  • Observation: "Brown staining approximately three feet across on the ceiling of the northwest bedroom; paint in that area appears newer than the surrounding ceiling."
  • Not a conclusion: "There was a roof leak that has been repaired."

The first is what the licensee observed and is qualified to state. The second is an inference the licensee is not qualified to make, and it may be wrong in a way the buyer relies on.

2.4 The worked case

Previewing a listing, the agent notices ceiling staining and fresh paint around it. The seller says a pipe leaked two years ago, was repaired, and "there's nothing to disclose because it's fixed." The draft transfer disclosure says nothing about it.

What is required:

  1. The past leak and the repair are disclosed by the seller — a repaired condition is still a fact a buyer would want, because the repair's adequacy is the question.
  2. The agent's visual observation is separately recorded and disclosed — the staining is what the agent saw, and that is the agent's own duty regardless of what the seller discloses.
  3. The buyer is advised to have the repair evaluated by an appropriate professional before his contingency expires.

What is not sufficient: disclosing the staining but not the leak, because the agent observed one and the seller knows the other and both obligations exist. Nor is leaving it to the buyer's inspector, which discharges neither duty.

2.5 Where the duty meets the competence limit

When the visual inspection reveals something the licensee does not understand well enough to evaluate, the correct sequence is:

  1. Disclose the observation.
  2. Recommend evaluation by the appropriate specialist.
  3. Document the recommendation and the buyer's response.

What is not appropriate: offering a general impression of how serious such conditions usually are, asking the listing agent whether the seller considers it significant, or waiting to see whether the inspector flags it.

2.6 A worked walkthrough

The property. A single-story house built in 1968, owner-occupied, listed by the licensee. He conducts his visual inspection on a Tuesday afternoon.

What he observes, and what each observation obligates.

A brown stain on the ceiling of the hall bathroom, roughly a foot across, dry to appearance. Recorded and disclosed. He does not determine whether it is active, does not conclude it is old because it looks dry, and does not describe it as "prior water intrusion, repaired" — which asserts two facts he does not know. He writes what he saw: a stain, its location, its approximate size, and that its cause and status were not determined.

A hairline crack running diagonally from the corner of a doorway. Recorded. He does not characterize it as settlement, cosmetic, or structural. Section 2.5's competence limit governs — the characterization is exactly the part he is not qualified to supply.

A door that does not latch and a window that sticks. Recorded. Individually trivial; noted because a pattern of doors and windows out of square is information, and the pattern is the sort of thing a professional evaluates.

A subpanel in the garage with a hand-lettered label. Recorded. Electrical work of unknown provenance in a house of this age is an observation, not a conclusion.

Fresh paint on one interior wall and nowhere else. Recorded. He does not accuse anyone of concealment. He writes what is observable, which is that one wall was recently painted, and a reviewer can make of it what a reviewer makes of it.

A gate in the rear fence that opens onto a neighbor's yard. Recorded, and it prompts a question to the seller about the boundary — which produces the answer that "the fence has always been about three feet inside the line." That answer is a material fact and it belongs on the seller's disclosure, and the licensee's note records that he asked and what he was told.

What he does not do.

He does not move furniture, lift carpet, enter the crawlspace, or climb onto the roof. Section 2.2's scope is a reasonably competent and diligent visual inspection of accessible areas, and exceeding it creates an expectation he cannot meet on every property.

He does not omit an item because the seller says it was fixed. A repaired condition is disclosed, and the licensee records both the observation and the seller's statement about it as two separate things.

He does not omit an item because it will be obvious to the buyer. Section 1.2 of Lesson 04 disposes of that.

He does not write "no issues observed" on a fifty-seven-year-old house. That sentence is a statement about the property that the inspection did not support, and it is the single most dangerous phrase in this document.

What the record looks like. Dated, specific, factual, in his own words, with locations. Photographs where the description is hard. And it is written the same day — Section 2.3's requirement — because the value of the note is that it is contemporaneous.

3. Natural hazard and environmental disclosure

3.1 Natural hazard disclosure

California requires disclosure of whether a property lies within certain mapped hazard zones — flood, fire, seismic, and related designations. The disclosure is commonly prepared by a third-party report provider.

What it does and does not do:

  • Does: tell the buyer that the property is within a mapped zone, so the buyer can investigate what that means for insurance, construction requirements, and risk.
  • Does not: certify the property is safe, predict whether an event will occur, or substitute for the buyer's own investigation.

It applies based on mapping, not on the property's event history. A property that has never flooded is disclosed as within a flood zone if it is mapped in one.

3.2 Why it matters more than buyers assume

Hazard zone status drives consequences buyers frequently discover late:

  • Insurance availability and cost. In some areas this is now the determining factor in whether a transaction closes at all, and it should be investigated early in the contingency period rather than at the end.
  • Construction and improvement requirements, which can materially affect a buyer's renovation plans.
  • Financing, where lenders require particular coverage.
  • Disclosure obligations the buyer will inherit as a future seller.

A licensee who ensures a buyer investigates insurance early has prevented a common and avoidable failure.

3.3 Environmental conditions

Depending on the property, environmental disclosures and investigations may include soil and groundwater contamination, proximity to industrial or agricultural use, radon, mold, methamphetamine contamination, and other conditions.

The licensee's role is consistent throughout: disclose what is known, recommend appropriate investigation, and do not evaluate. A licensee who tells a buyer that visible mold "is just surface mold, it'll clean off" has made an assessment they are not qualified to make.

3.4 Age-triggered disclosures

Certain obligations are triggered by the age of the housing rather than by any observed condition. For housing built before 1978, the federal lead-based paint requirements apply.

Those requirements are specific, and licensees routinely misstate them:

The obligation isThe obligation is not
Disclose known lead-based paint and hazardsTest the property
Provide available records and reportsCreate records that do not exist
Provide the required pamphletCertify the property is free of lead
Give the buyer the opportunity to conduct an assessmentRemediate before transfer

It is triggered by the age of the housing, not by whether children will occupy it.

3.5 Water, drought, and California-specific conditions

California transactions increasingly involve water-related requirements: conservation obligations, fixture requirements, well and septic conditions on rural property, and supply questions that affect what may be built.

These are investigation items and, where known, disclosure items. A licensee marketing rural property who has not asked about water source, adequacy, and rights has skipped a question that determines whether the property is usable as intended.

3.6 Common interest developments

Where the property is in a common interest development, the buyer's investigation extends to the association: governing documents and use restrictions, budget and reserves, assessments including any special assessment, meeting minutes, and pending or threatened litigation.

Construction-defect litigation affecting the building is a clear material fact, and the buyer should have the documents in time to review them within the contingency period — which means requesting them promptly rather than late.

3.7 Reading a hazard report properly

A natural hazard disclosure arrives as a report from a third-party provider, and it is treated by most participants as a form to be delivered. It is a document with content, and three habits separate a licensee who uses it from one who forwards it.

Read what it actually says. The report states whether the property is within mapped zones, and its statements are qualified in ways that matter: mapping is done at a scale, boundaries are approximate, and maps are revised. A property just outside a mapped boundary is not a property without the hazard, and a buyer who reads "not in a zone" as "not at risk" has misread it.

Understand what each determination does downstream. A flood zone determination drives lender-required insurance. A fire severity zone determination can drive insurance availability, cost, and — increasingly in California — whether coverage is obtainable at all. A seismic or landslide zone can drive disclosure and investigation obligations. Insurance availability is the item that most often surprises a buyer late, and it is knowable early.

Tell the buyer to price insurance before removing contingencies. This is the practical instruction that flows from the report, and it is the one most often given too late. A buyer who discovers at day nineteen that coverage will cost several times what he assumed, or that carriers will not write it, has lost the ability to respond — and Section 5.4 of this lesson explains why the timing of information determines whether it protects anyone.

Three things the licensee does not do:

  • Interpret the maps. The report is the disclosure; the licensee is not the source of the determination and does not offer an opinion about whether a boundary is "really" where it is drawn.
  • Reassure. "It's technically in the zone but nothing's ever flooded there" is a prediction and a characterization, and it is offered to buyers constantly.
  • Answer the insurance question himself. The carrier answers it. The licensee's contribution is telling the buyer to ask, early, and recording that he did.

What belongs in the file: the report, its delivery with a date, and the licensee's written recommendation that the buyer obtain insurance quotes and any further evaluation the report suggests, delivered early enough for the buyer to act inside his periods.

And where the seller knows something the report does not say — that the street floods, that the property was evacuated in a fire season, that a slope moved — that is a known material fact and the report does not cover it. Section 4.3 governs: the seller's knowledge and the licensee's knowledge are separate obligations, and neither is satisfied by a third-party map.

4. Lead-based paint, material facts, and stigma limits

4.1 The limits on death and illness disclosure

California imposes specific limits on the disclosure of a prior occupant's death and on disclosure relating to a prior occupant's illness. These limits exist for a reason worth teaching: without them, disclosure practice would operate as a channel for discrimination against people with particular illnesses, and would attach a permanent penalty to properties for reasons unrelated to their condition.

The practical guidance for a licensee:

  • Do not volunteer information about the manner of a prior occupant's death or about a prior occupant's illness.
  • Do not misrepresent. Where a direct question arises and the answer is constrained, the licensee should not lie.
  • Escalate. The precise boundary is a legal question with real consequences on both sides — disclose too much and there is one exposure, too little and another. This is a question for the broker and, where necessary, counsel, not one to resolve at a showing.

4.2 Material fact versus stigma

CategoryTreatment
Physical condition of the propertyMaterial; disclose if known
Legal condition — liens, boundary disputes, litigation, permit statusMaterial; disclose if known
Conditions affecting use — zoning, association restrictions, easementsMaterial; disclose if known
Neighborhood characteristics that are objective and verifiablePoint the buyer to the source rather than characterizing
Characteristics of neighbors or residentsDo not characterize — this is steering territory
Prior occupant's death or illnessSubject to specific legal limits; escalate
Rumor, reputation, and speculationDo not represent as fact

4.3 The licensee's own knowledge

A licensee's disclosure obligation attaches to what the licensee knows, from any source. Two situations recur:

A neighbor tells the agent the home flooded three years ago. The seller has said nothing about it.

The report is neither ignorable nor assertable as fact. The correct path runs through the seller and the broker: raise it, ask the seller, and ensure the disclosure addresses what is known and what remains unverified. The file should show the issue was surfaced and how it was resolved.

The agent sold the same property four years ago and knows of a condition disclosed then.

The agent knows it. The obligation is the agent's, regardless of the current seller's disclosure.

4.4 Unverified information

The professional treatment of unverified information is to attribute it and flag it, never to launder it into apparent fact:

  • Acceptable: "The seller reports the roof was replaced in approximately 2019; this has not been verified."
  • Not acceptable: "Roof replaced 2019."
  • Not acceptable: repeating a figure from a prior listing as though the licensee had verified it.

4.5 What must not be represented as fact

  • A prediction — that values will rise, that a permit will issue, that a variance will be granted.
  • A legal conclusion — that a use is permitted, that an encroachment is unimportant, that a neighbor has no claim.
  • A specialist's assessment — that a crack is cosmetic, that a repair was adequate, that mold is harmless.
  • Anything the licensee has not verified and cannot attribute.

4.6 The questions buyers ask about the surrounding area

Buyers ask about schools, crime, neighbors, demographics, "what kind of area is this," and whether a neighborhood is "good." These questions are the most reliable way for a licensee to create a fair housing violation while trying to be helpful, and the correct handling is a discipline rather than a script.

The principle: a licensee does not characterize the composition of a neighborhood or the desirability of an area in terms that sort people, whatever the question's phrasing and whatever the buyer's evident intent.

The questionThe correct handling
"How are the schools?"Direct the buyer to the district's own published information and to the district itself for attendance boundaries. Do not rate, rank, or compare. Attendance boundaries change, and a boundary stated from memory is also a reasonable-care problem.
"Is it safe?"Direct the buyer to law enforcement's published data and to his own investigation. Do not characterize. "Safe" and "bad area" are exactly the terms that carry composition.
"What kind of people live here?"Do not answer. Not carefully, not vaguely, not with a compliment. Explain that you do not describe the composition of neighborhoods and that public sources exist for demographic data.
"Would you buy here?"The buyer is inviting the characterization through a side door. The answer is about the property and his criteria, not about the area's desirability.
"Are there a lot of families?"Familial status. Do not answer, including favorably.
"Is it quiet?"An observable condition rather than a composition question, and it is answerable by direction to investigate — visit at different hours, check for a nearby flight path, arterial, rail line, or venue.
"Any problems with the neighbors?"Section 4.3 governs the licensee's own knowledge of material facts. A known material fact is disclosed; a characterization of people is not offered.

Two clarifications that licensees find genuinely confusing.

Refusing to characterize is not refusing to help. The buyer gets a better answer from the district, the police department, and his own visits than from the licensee's impression, and saying so is a service rather than an evasion. The professional version is: "I don't characterize neighborhoods — I'd be giving you my impression when you need facts. Here is where the actual data lives, and here is what I'd go look at myself."

The prohibition does not depend on the buyer's intent or on the answer being positive. A licensee who says "you'll love it, it's a great family area" to a buyer who asked innocently has still steered, and the violation does not require anyone to have been excluded on purpose.

The file consequence: Section 4.5 of Lesson 07 requires documenting the search on the buyer's stated criteria. A search record built on the buyer's criteria is the evidence that the search was not built on anything else, which is why the documentation habit and the fair housing discipline are the same habit.

5. Delivery, timing, and rescission

5.1 Delivery is where rights attach

Content is half the obligation. Delivery, and evidence of when it occurred, is the other half, because disclosure rules attach consequences to delivery — including periods during which a buyer may respond.

A file with a signed disclosure and no delivery date proves that the buyer received it, and proves nothing about when. Timing is what disputes are about.

5.2 What the file must show

For each disclosure:

  1. What was delivered — the specific document and version.
  2. To whom.
  3. When — the date, and where the rule is time-sensitive, the time.
  4. By what method, and that the method was one the contract or the law authorizes.
  5. Evidence — delivery receipt, signed acknowledgment with date, platform audit trail.

5.3 Early delivery is a professional advantage

Disclosures delivered early give the buyer time to review and investigate within the contingency period, which produces better decisions and fewer late renegotiations. Disclosures delivered late compress that review, create pressure, and generate the disputes that follow from pressure.

This is why the disclosure work belongs at the listing appointment rather than at the offer. A seller who resists — "let's wait until we have an offer" — is asking for the arrangement most likely to cost them.

5.4 Late delivery and its consequence

The seller delivers a required disclosure after the buyer has already removed his contingencies.

What is true: rights that run from delivery are not extinguished by the buyer having acted earlier without the information. The late delivery may revive or extend the buyer's ability to respond.

What the licensee must do: identify the issue immediately and raise it, rather than proceeding as though the transaction were firm.

What is not a resolution: treating it as cured because the buyer received it before closing, or extending the closing date, neither of which addresses the buyer's right to respond.

5.5 Amending a disclosure

Where information changes or new information arrives — a new leak, a notice from the association, a claim filed — the disclosure is amended and re-delivered, with a new delivery date. The buyer's rights run from the delivery of the new information.

An amendment does not erase the original. Both stay in the file, so the sequence shows what was known when.

5.6 The correction obligation

Where a licensee discovers that a disclosure or a statement was wrong, the obligation is to correct it immediately, in writing, to everyone who received the original — before the recipient acts on it if that is still possible.

Waiting to see whether the error matters is how an error becomes a misrepresentation. A correction that arrives after closing is worth almost nothing to the person who relied on it.

6. Disclosure risk management

6.1 The three-part test for any condition

When a licensee encounters something about a property and is unsure what to do, three questions resolve it:

  1. Do I know it? If yes, the licensee's own obligation is engaged, whatever the seller does.
  2. Would a reasonable buyer consider it important? If yes, it is material.
  3. Am I qualified to evaluate it? If no, disclose the observation, recommend the specialist, and do not characterize.

6.2 The failure modes, and which is worse

FailureConsequence
Not disclosing a known material factThe most serious. Concealment, and it survives closing as a claim
Disclosing but not evidencing deliveryThe file cannot show the buyer's rights were honored
Evaluating beyond competenceA statement the buyer relies on that the licensee could not support
Disclosing what the law limitsA different exposure, and potentially a discrimination issue
Over-disclosing rumor as factMisrepresentation, and potential harm to the seller

Note that the failures run in both directions. "Disclose everything" is not the rule; "disclose what is material and known, attribute what is unverified, decline what the law limits, and evaluate nothing" is.

6.3 Building the disclosure file

A defensible disclosure file contains:

  • The seller's disclosures, completed with no blanks, signed and dated.
  • The licensee's visual inspection disclosure, with observations recorded as observations.
  • Hazard and third-party reports.
  • Age-triggered disclosures with the required acknowledgments.
  • Association documents where applicable, with the date requested and the date delivered.
  • Delivery evidence for every item.
  • Amendments, with their own delivery evidence, and the originals retained.
  • A record of recommendations made to the buyer and the buyer's responses.

6.4 The audit question

The test to apply to any disclosure file: could a reviewer, reading only this file, determine what the buyer knew and when he knew it?

If the answer is no, the gap is not a paperwork problem. It is the specific gap that a dispute will be about.

6.5 A worked disclosure timeline

The transaction. Acceptance communicated on a Monday. The agreement gives the buyer an investigation period and a defined period to respond to disclosures, running from delivery.

What the file records:

  • Day 1 (Monday). Acceptance communicated.
  • Day 4. Seller's transfer disclosure signed by the seller. No delivery record.
  • Day 6. Natural hazard report ordered.
  • Day 9. Buyer's general inspection performed. Report recommends specialist evaluation of the subfloor.
  • Day 11. Natural hazard report received by the listing agent. Forwarded "to the buyer's agent" by email — no record of transmission to the buyer.
  • Day 14. Buyer signs a receipt acknowledging the transfer disclosure. The receipt is undated in the buyer's hand; the email transmitting the disclosure to him is dated Day 13.
  • Day 16. Seller amends the transfer disclosure to add a prior roof repair he had forgotten. The amendment is emailed to the buyer's agent.
  • Day 17. Buyer removes his investigation contingency. He has not obtained the subfloor evaluation.
  • Day 19. Buyer's agent forwards the Day 16 amendment to the buyer.

The findings, ranked.

1. The amendment reached the buyer after he removed his contingency. This is the defect with consequences, and Section 5.5 and Section 5.6 both govern. An amended disclosure delivered after the buyer's decision has been delivered too late to inform the decision it was about, and Section 5.4 establishes that rights running from delivery are not extinguished by the buyer having already acted without the information. The correct response is to raise it immediately, not to proceed as though the removal settled it.

2. Delivery of the transfer disclosure is documented only by inference. The seller signed on Day 4; the file's evidence of the buyer receiving it is a Day 13 email and an undated receipt. The response period cannot be computed from the file, which is Section 5.2's requirement.

3. The hazard report's delivery to the buyer is unevidenced. Forwarding to the agent is not, by itself, evidence the buyer received it, and the file has no record beyond the agent-to-agent email.

4. The nine-day gap between the seller's signature and transmission is not itself a violation, but Section 5.3 explains what it costs: the buyer's review time was consumed by the file rather than by the buyer.

5. The contingency removed without the recommended evaluation is a Lesson 07 issue rather than a disclosure issue, and it is recorded here because it interacts with finding 1 — the buyer made an uninformed decision twice on the same day.

What happens now. Both agents raise it with their brokers immediately. The buyer is told what the amendment says and when it reached him. Whether his rights are affected is a legal question, and the licensees do not resolve it between themselves.

What must not happen: dating the Day 14 receipt, re-sending the amendment now with a new date and treating that as delivery on Day 16, or persuading the buyer that the roof repair "doesn't change anything." The third is the most tempting and the most serious, because it substitutes the licensee's judgment for the buyer's on the exact question the disclosure existed to give him.

The process fix. Every disclosure and every amendment is transmitted directly to the party, with delivery evidenced, and logged the day it is sent. Four of the five findings above disappear under that one rule.

6.6 Checkpoint

You should be able to apply the three-part test to a condition, state which failure mode a given file defect represents, and explain why late delivery is not cured by delivery before closing.

Key terms

TermDefinition
Transfer disclosure statementThe statutory statement in which a California seller of residential property discloses known material facts affecting value or desirability.
What the seller knowsThe scope of the seller's obligation. The seller is not required to investigate or to become an expert — only to disclose what they actually know.
MaterialityWhether a fact would affect a reasonable buyer's decision to buy or the price they would pay. Measured against a reasonable buyer, not the seller's own view of importance.
"Unknown"The honest, available, complete answer to an item the seller cannot answer. Distinct from a blank, which is ambiguous.
MisattributionThe defect created when a licensee completes the seller's disclosure on their behalf — the statement is attributed to a person who did not make it.
Adopting the omissionWhat a licensee does by delivering a disclosure they know omits a material condition. The licensee's own knowledge creates the licensee's own obligation.
Visual inspection dutyThe licensee's reasonably competent and diligent visual inspection of reasonably and normally accessible areas, with disclosure of material facts it reveals.
Independence of the dutyThe visual inspection is not satisfied by reviewing the seller's disclosure, recommending an inspector, the other agent's inspection, or the existence of an inspection report.
Observation versus conclusion"Brown staining roughly three feet across on the northwest bedroom ceiling" is an observation the licensee may state. "There was a roof leak that has been repaired" is an inference the licensee is not qualified to make.
Natural hazard disclosureDisclosure of whether the property lies within certain mapped hazard zones. Applies on mapping, not on event history, and certifies nothing about safety.
Insurance availabilityThe consequence of hazard zone status that most often decides whether a transaction closes — and the reason it belongs early in the contingency period.
Age-triggered disclosureAn obligation arising from the age of the housing rather than any observed condition. For pre-1978 housing, the federal lead-based paint requirements.
Lead-based paint obligationDisclose what is known, provide available records and the required pamphlet, and give the buyer the opportunity to assess. Not: test, create records, certify, or remediate.
Death and illness limitsCalifornia's specific constraints on disclosing a prior occupant's death or illness. Do not volunteer, do not misrepresent, escalate.
StigmaRumor, reputation, and speculation. Never represented as fact.
AttributionThe professional treatment of unverified information: "the seller reports the roof was replaced in approximately 2019; this has not been verified."
LaunderingRepeating an unverified figure — from the seller, or from a prior listing — as though it had been verified.
Delivery evidenceWhat was delivered, to whom, when, by what authorized method, with proof. Rights attach to delivery, so a signed disclosure with no date proves the wrong thing.
AmendmentA re-delivered disclosure reflecting new information, with a new delivery date. The original stays in the file so the sequence shows what was known when.
Correction obligationCorrecting a wrong statement immediately, in writing, to everyone who received the original — before they act on it, if that is still possible.
Three-part testDo I know it? Would a reasonable buyer consider it important? Am I qualified to evaluate it?
The audit questionCould a reviewer, reading only this file, determine what the buyer knew and when he knew it?

Practice problems

Problem 1

At the listing appointment a seller says of a recurring drainage problem in the side yard: "It only happens in heavy rain, it's not a problem for me, the buyer could see the grading himself, and anyway it started before I bought the place. Nobody's asked about it."

Address each objection.

Solution. Every one of them measures the wrong thing. The standard is whether a reasonable buyer would consider the fact important to the decision or the price.

Frequency is not the test — a drainage problem appearing three times a year is material. The seller's own tolerance is not the test — the standard is a reasonable buyer, not this seller. Obviousness is not the test — assuming the buyer noticed is how disputes begin. Predating ownership is not the test — if the seller knows it, the seller discloses it. And disclosure is not question-driven — "nobody asked" is not a category of exemption.

The counseling argument that works is practical rather than moral: disclosed, this is a fact the buyer prices in and moves past. Undisclosed and found at inspection, it is a renegotiation with the seller's credibility gone. Undisclosed and found after closing, it is a claim.

Problem 2

A seller returns a draft disclosure with three items blank and one marked "no" about which he is genuinely unsure. The licensee, wanting a clean form, fills in the blanks himself based on what the seller told him verbally.

Identify each defect.

Solution. Three, and the last is the most serious.

The blanks are ambiguous — they invite a later dispute about whether the item was addressed at all. Every item is answered, and "unknown" is an honest and complete answer.

The uncertain item marked "no" asserts knowledge the seller does not have. That is not a conservative choice; it is an affirmative statement that may be false.

The licensee completing it is the worst of the three. The seller completes the seller's disclosure. Filling it in on his behalf misattributes the statement and may create a misrepresentation attributed to a person who did not make it.

Also worth stating: the disclosure is a statement of what the seller knows now. New information arriving during the transaction updates it by amendment.

Problem 3

Previewing a listing, the agent notices brown ceiling staining with newer paint around it. The seller says a pipe leaked two years ago, was repaired, and there is nothing to disclose "because it's fixed." The draft disclosure says nothing about it.

What is required, and what is not sufficient?

Solution. Three things, and two obligations that exist independently.

The seller discloses the past leak and the repair. A repaired condition is still a fact a buyer wants, because the adequacy of the repair is precisely the question — and here there are no records.

The agent separately records and discloses the visual observation. Recorded as an observation, not a conclusion: "brown staining approximately three feet across on the northwest bedroom ceiling; paint in that area appears newer than the surrounding ceiling." Not "there was a roof leak that has been repaired," which is an inference the agent is not qualified to make and may be wrong in a way the buyer relies on.

The buyer is advised to have the repair evaluated by an appropriate professional before his contingency expires, and the recommendation is documented.

Not sufficient: disclosing the staining but not the leak, since the agent observed one and the seller knows the other and both obligations exist. Nor leaving it to the buyer's inspector, which discharges neither duty.

Problem 4

A property has never flooded in the seller's twenty years of ownership but sits within a mapped flood zone. The buyer says the mapping is "clearly outdated" and asks whether the disclosure is really necessary. The licensee plans to order insurance quotes after the inspection contingency closes.

Correct both.

Solution. The disclosure applies on mapping, not on event history. A property that has never flooded is disclosed as within a flood zone if it is mapped in one, and the disclosure does not certify the property is safe, predict an event, or substitute for the buyer's investigation. It tells the buyer he is in a mapped zone so he can investigate what that means.

The insurance timing is the more expensive error. Hazard zone status drives insurance availability and cost — in some California areas now the determining factor in whether a transaction closes at all — along with construction requirements, financing conditions, and the disclosure obligations the buyer inherits as a future seller. Investigating it early in the contingency period rather than at the end is one of the clearest preventable failures in current practice.

Problem 5

A 1964 home is being sold to a buyer with no children. The listing agent tells the seller that the lead-based paint requirements do not apply, and separately assures a buyer that visible growth in the laundry room "is just surface mold, it'll clean off."

Assess both statements.

Solution. Both are wrong, and the second is a competence failure.

Lead-based paint requirements are triggered by the age of the housing, not by whether children will occupy it — pre-1978 housing is covered. And the obligation is frequently misstated in the other direction too: disclose what is known, provide available records and reports, provide the required pamphlet, and give the buyer the opportunity to conduct an assessment. It does not require testing the property, creating records that do not exist, certifying the property is free of lead, or remediating before transfer.

On the mold: the licensee has made an assessment they are not qualified to make, on which the buyer may rely. The correct sequence when the visual inspection reveals something the licensee cannot evaluate is disclose the observation, recommend evaluation by the appropriate specialist, document the recommendation and the response — not offer a general impression, not ask the listing agent whether the seller thinks it matters, and not wait to see whether the inspector flags it.

Problem 6

A neighbor tells the listing agent the house flooded three years ago. The seller has disclosed nothing about it. Separately, the same agent sold this property four years ago and recalls a foundation condition disclosed at that time.

How does each obligation run?

Solution. A licensee's disclosure obligation attaches to what the licensee knows, from any source — and these two run differently.

The neighbor's report is neither ignorable nor assertable as fact. The path runs through the seller and the broker: raise it, ask the seller, and ensure the disclosure addresses what is known and what remains unverified. The file should show the issue was surfaced and how it was resolved. Stating it as fact would be representing an unverified report as verified.

The prior-sale knowledge is different: the agent knows it. The obligation is the agent's, regardless of what the current seller discloses. That is the point separating a compliant licensee from a complicit one — delivering a disclosure the licensee knows omits a material condition adopts the omission.

Problem 7

The seller delivers a required disclosure two days after the buyer removed all contingencies. The listing agent proposes to say nothing, on the grounds that the buyer will have it well before closing, and to offer a one-week closing extension if the buyer complains.

Assess.

Solution. Neither proposal is a resolution, and the silence is itself the violation.

Rights that run from delivery are not extinguished because the buyer acted earlier without the information. Late delivery may revive or extend the buyer's ability to respond — which is exactly what the agent is proposing to conceal.

Receipt before closing does not cure it, and neither does a closing extension: neither addresses the buyer's right to respond, which is what the timing rule protects.

What is required: identify the issue immediately and raise it, rather than proceeding as though the transaction were firm.

What transfers: this is why the disclosure work belongs at the listing appointment. Early delivery gives the buyer time to review and investigate inside the contingency period; late delivery compresses the review, creates pressure, and generates the disputes that follow from pressure. A seller who says "let's wait until we have an offer" is asking for the arrangement most likely to cost him.

Problem 8

A file review finds a disclosure signed by the buyer with no date, an agent visual inspection reading "minor cosmetic settling cracks, nothing structural," a marketing sheet stating "roof replaced 2019" copied from the prior listing, and a note that the agent told a buyer the prior owner "died of an illness, but I probably shouldn't say more."

Classify each failure and state the rule that covers all four.

Solution. Four different failure modes, which is the point of the exercise.

The undated signature proves receipt and proves nothing about when — and timing is what disputes are about. Rights attach to delivery.

"Nothing structural" is evaluating beyond competence: a statement the buyer may rely on that the licensee could not support. The observation was permitted; the conclusion was not.

"Roof replaced 2019" launders an unverified figure from a prior listing into apparent fact. The professional treatment attributes and flags: "the seller reports approximately 2019; this has not been verified."

The death-and-illness comment is disclosing what the law limits — a different exposure, and potentially a discrimination issue. Do not volunteer, do not misrepresent, and escalate: the precise boundary is a legal question with real consequences on both sides, not one to resolve at a showing.

The rule covering all four: "disclose everything" is not it. Disclose what is material and known, attribute what is unverified, decline what the law limits, and evaluate nothing.

Chapter summary

The seller discloses what the seller knows, measured by materiality to a reasonable buyer. The recurring objections — it's fixed, it's rare, it's obvious, nobody asked, it doesn't bother me, it predates me — each substitute a different standard for that one.

The seller completes the seller's disclosure. Every item answered, "unknown" where that is true, uncertain items never marked "no," and amended when new information arrives.

The licensee's visual inspection is an independent duty — not discharged by the seller's disclosure, by recommending an inspector, or by an inspection report existing. Record observations, not conclusions, including observations whose significance is unclear.

A licensee who knows of a material condition the seller omitted, and delivers the disclosure anyway, has adopted the omission.

Natural hazard disclosure applies on mapping, not history, and its most consequential downstream effect is insurance availability — which belongs early in the contingency period, not at the end.

Age triggers the lead-based paint obligations, not occupancy by children. Disclose what is known, provide records and the pamphlet, allow assessment. Do not test, certify, or remediate.

Death and illness disclosure is legally constrained for reasons worth understanding: without the limits, disclosure practice would channel discrimination and attach permanent penalties unrelated to condition. Do not volunteer, do not misrepresent, escalate.

Attribute unverified information; never launder it. Never represent a prediction, a legal conclusion, or a specialist's assessment as fact.

Delivery is half the obligation. What, to whom, when, by what authorized method, with evidence. Amendments carry their own delivery date and the originals stay in the file.

Correct errors immediately, in writing, to everyone who received the original. Waiting to see whether an error matters is how an error becomes a misrepresentation.

Three questions resolve any condition: Do I know it? Would a reasonable buyer consider it important? Am I qualified to evaluate it?

The failures run in both directions, so the rule is not "disclose everything" but disclose what is material and known, attribute what is unverified, decline what the law limits, and evaluate nothing. The audit question is whether a reviewer reading only the file could determine what the buyer knew and when.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. Identify each condition and whether it is material, state whose disclosure obligation each engages, distinguish your own observations from the seller's knowledge and from unverified reports, identify what you would recommend the buyer investigate, state the delivery evidence you would create, and identify anything you would escalate rather than resolve. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Practice - Lesson Assignment 10

Financing the transaction

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Explain what preapproval establishes and how a seller's agent should evaluate one.
  • Describe the common loan program types at the level a licensee may properly discuss, and identify where the conversation must move to a lender.
  • Explain how the appraisal interacts with the loan amount, and advise a buyer facing a shortfall.
  • Apply seller credits and concessions correctly, including their interaction with program limits and the appraisal.
  • Recognize the events during escrow that jeopardize a conditional approval.
  • Respond to a financing failure through the contract's mechanism rather than improvising.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: preapproval and loan program orientation55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: disclosures, appraisal, and underwriting50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: credits, concessions, and financing failure45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Preapproval and loan terms

1.1 Why a licensee needs to understand financing

A real estate licensee does not originate loans, does not underwrite, and must not advise on loan program suitability. A licensee nonetheless needs a working understanding of financing for three reasons:

  1. To advise a buyer on the strength of his own position and on how his offer will read.
  2. To advise a seller on the probability that an offer will close — which is half of what a seller is actually choosing between.
  3. To recognize the events that jeopardize a transaction early enough to respond.

The boundary is consistent: explain how financing works generally; refer the specific recommendation to a qualified lender.

1.2 The three levels of lender assessment

BasisWhat it establishesWhat it does not
PrequalificationLargely stated informationA rough borrowing rangeVerification of anything
PreapprovalLender review of supporting informationA likely loan amount, subject to conditionsA commitment to lend
Loan commitmentUnderwriting completeSubstantially more certaintyImmunity from changed circumstances

None guarantees funding. All remain contingent on the property, the appraisal, and the borrower's circumstances holding through funding.

1.3 Reading a preapproval as a seller's agent

A seller's agent evaluating an offer should read the letter rather than note its existence. The questions:

  • What was verified? Income, assets, employment, credit — or nothing beyond the borrower's statements?
  • What conditions remain?
  • What program and terms does it assume? A letter assuming a program the buyer will not use is worth little.
  • Is the amount a maximum or the amount for this property?
  • When does it expire?
  • Who issued it? A lender the agent can contact, or an unnamed source?

A letter that verifies nothing and expires in ten days tells the seller something real about the offer's strength, and that is information the seller is entitled to when choosing.

1.4 Reading a preapproval as a buyer's agent

The same reading, for a different purpose: to know how the offer will be received, and to advise the buyer on strengthening it. A buyer who can obtain a fully underwritten preapproval before offering is materially more competitive than one with a two-line letter, and that is advice worth giving early.

1.5 What a licensee may say about financing

May: explain the general characteristics of common program types; explain what preapproval is; explain how the appraisal interacts with the loan; explain how a credit affects the transaction; recommend the buyer speak with more than one lender; explain what tends to make an offer read as strong.

May not: recommend a specific program as suitable for this buyer; predict what a lender will approve; advise on structuring the buyer's finances to qualify; suggest that anything be omitted or characterized differently on an application.

1.6 Cash offers and unconventional funding

An offer that says "cash" removes the financing contingency from the conversation and replaces it with a verification problem most licensees handle worse.

"Cash" means the buyer has the funds and is not obtaining a loan secured by the property. It does not mean the money is sitting in a checking account today, and the difference is where cash transactions fail.

The sourceWhat it actually requires
Funds on depositThe simplest case. Proof of funds identifies the institution, the account holder, and a balance as of a date.
Funds in securitiesA liquidation that takes time and produces a different amount than the statement shows.
Funds from a sale closing firstNot cash. It is a contingent transaction wearing a cash label, and the dependency belongs in the offer.
A loan against other property or assetsFinancing, secured elsewhere. It has its own approval and its own timeline.
Funds from a retirement or self-directed accountFrequently subject to procedural requirements and custodial timelines that are slower than the closing period.
Funds from another personRaises who the buyer actually is, and whether the person on the contract is the person acquiring.
Funds arriving from outside the countryTransfer timelines and institutional requirements that can exceed the closing period substantially.

What a listing agent does with a cash offer.
