Read the proof of funds as carefully as Section 1.3 reads a preapproval: whose name, which institution, what date, and does the amount cover the price plus costs. A statement in a name other than the buyer's, a screenshot with no institution identified, or a letter making an assertion without a source is not verification.

What a buyer's agent does. Confirm with the buyer where the money actually is and how long it takes to become available in the escrow, before proposing a short closing period. A fourteen-day close proposed by a buyer whose funds require a twenty-day custodial process is a default the buyer scheduled himself.

Two boundaries hold in every case.

The source of funds question belongs to escrow, title, and the institutions, which have their own obligations. A licensee who becomes aware of facts suggesting the funds are not what they are represented to be escalates to the broker rather than investigating, on the same reasoning Lesson 14 of the mortgage course applies to origination.

A cash buyer still gets an appraisal if he wants one. Removing the lender does not remove the value question, and a buyer who wants an independent valuation should have it as a contingency rather than an afterthought.

The seller's version of this conversation: cash is faster and more certain when the money is actually liquid. A cash offer contingent on a securities liquidation, an overseas transfer, or another closing is not more certain than a strong financed offer — it is less certain, with fewer disclosures.

2. Loan program orientation

2.1 Conventional financing

A conventional loan is one not insured or guaranteed by a government agency. Terms vary by the borrower's credit profile, the down payment, the property type, and occupancy. Where the down payment is below a program threshold, mortgage insurance is commonly required, adding to the monthly cost.

2.2 Government-backed programs

Government-backed programs share a common structure: a government agency insures or guarantees the lender against certain losses, which reduces the lender's risk and supports terms — notably lower down payments — that qualifying borrowers might not otherwise obtain.

Three points licensees regularly misstate:

  • The government is not the lender. Private lenders make the loans.
  • The loans bear interest on ordinary terms.
  • They are not restricted to new construction, and they are not restricted to first-time buyers as a general matter.

Government programs commonly carry property condition requirements, which matters to a seller evaluating an offer: a property with deferred maintenance may not satisfy them, and that is a closing-probability question rather than a preference.

2.3 Rate structures

StructureCharacteristicThe buyer's exposure
Fixed rateRate and payment fixed for the termNone to rate movement; pays for that certainty
Adjustable rateRate changes per an index plus a margin, within capsPayment changes over time
Interest-only periodScheduled payments cover interest only for a periodPrincipal is not retired during it
BalloonScheduled payments do not retire principal by maturityA large sum due at maturity

For adjustable-rate loans, what determines the rate at each adjustment is the index plus the lender's margin, subject to the caps in the note — not the lender's discretion, and not the borrower's credit at that time.

2.4 Occupancy matters, and misstating it is fraud

Occupancy — owner-occupied, second home, or investment — affects pricing, program eligibility, and which consumer protections apply. It is therefore a material fact the lender relies on.

A buyer who asks a licensee to record owner-occupancy on a property he intends to rent is asking for participation in loan fraud. The correct response is to decline plainly, explain why, and record the loan as it actually is. Whether the buyer can afford the payment either way is beside the point.

2.5 Where the conversation must move to the lender

The moment the question becomes "which is better for me," it belongs to the lender. Suitability depends on the borrower's full financial detail, plans, and tolerance for payment change — facts the licensee does not hold and is not qualified to weigh.

What a licensee can properly do is help the buyer ask better questions: what the payment would be under each option, what happens if rates move, what the total cost looks like over the period he expects to hold the property, and what the break-even is on paying points.

3. Financing disclosures

3.1 The disclosures a buyer receives

Federal law requires the buyer to receive an early estimate of loan terms and costs, and a final disclosure of the actual terms before consummation, with a period to review the final against the estimate.

The licensee's role is not to interpret these documents. It is to:

  1. Ensure the buyer knows they are coming and roughly when.
  2. Ensure the buyer reads them and compares the final against the estimate.
  3. Direct questions to the lender, which is the party that can answer them.
  4. Track how the review period interacts with the closing date, because a change requiring re-disclosure can move the closing.

3.2 Why the review period matters to the transaction

The waiting period before consummation exists for the buyer's benefit. It also has a practical consequence for scheduling: certain late changes can restart it. A licensee who understands this will not promise a seller a closing date that a late change makes impossible, and will raise the issue when a buyer proposes a late change to his loan.

3.3 What changes can move the closing

ChangeEffect
Buyer changes loan program or termProduct change; new disclosures, and the timeline moves
Rate lock expires and is re-lockedMay change disclosed figures
A fee increases beyond its toleranceRequires disclosure, and may require a revised final
Buyer changes lenders mid-escrowRestarts substantial parts of the process
Property or appraisal issue changes the loan amountTerms change; disclosure follows

A buyer who proposes any of these should be told plainly what it does to the closing date, before he decides.

3.4 What a licensee must not do

  • Do not tell a buyer that a fee is "standard" or that a figure is "about right." Those are lender questions.
  • Do not compare loan offers for a buyer as though selecting between them.
  • Do not suggest a buyer withhold information from the lender, or provide anything the licensee knows to be inaccurate.
  • Do not accept anything of value for referring a buyer to a settlement service provider. Referrals happen; payment for them does not.

That last point deserves emphasis: a licensee who accepts a fee from an escrow company, title company, lender, or home warranty provider for sending business has a serious federal problem, and disclosure does not cure it.

3.5 The buyer who wants to shop lenders mid-transaction

A buyer under contract calls: a different lender has quoted a better rate, and he wants to switch.

It is his decision. He may change lenders at any point, and a licensee who tells him he cannot has misstated his rights. What the licensee owes him is the consequence, stated clearly and before he acts.

What changing lenders costs, and none of it is obvious to a first-time buyer:

  1. The timeline restarts in substance. A new lender begins its own process — application, disclosures, underwriting, appraisal. The days already spent do not transfer.
  2. A new appraisal is usually required, because an appraisal is generally ordered for and delivered to a particular lender. The buyer may pay twice, and the second appraisal can come in differently.
  3. The loan contingency deadline does not move because he switched. It is a contract date, and Section 6.4 governs extensions — which require the seller's agreement.
  4. New disclosures and their timing apply, which can affect the closing date, as Section 3.3 describes.
  5. The rate that motivated the change may not survive underwriting, the appraisal, or the delay.

What the licensee does:

  • Lay out the five points above in writing, without recommending either lender.
  • Tell him to ask the new lender for a realistic closing date in writing, and to tell both lenders the contract deadlines.
  • Raise the extension question with the seller's side early if the timeline is genuinely at risk — Section 6.3's early-warning discipline, applied to a self-inflicted delay.
  • Record the conversation and the buyer's decision.

What the licensee does not do:

  • Compare the loan offers. Section 1.5 and Section 3.4 both draw this line. Rate, cost, and program comparison is the lender's work, and a licensee who says "that second offer is better" has advised on a matter he is not licensed for.
  • Discourage him from switching to protect the closing date. The buyer's economics are the buyer's, and a licensee whose advice is shaped by his own interest in a smooth closing has a loyalty problem.
  • Tell the current lender the buyer is shopping, or the new lender what the current one quoted.

The related situation, and it is more common: the buyer does not switch, but starts a second application "just to compare." That is a second credit inquiry and a second file, and the two lenders' processes can interfere. The instruction is the same one Section 3.6 of Lesson 07 gives about every financial move: talk to the lender before doing it, and tell the licensee it happened.

4. Appraisal and underwriting

4.1 What the appraisal does for the lender

The lender obtains an appraisal to measure its collateral risk: what could be recovered if the loan is not repaid. Lenders lend against the lower of the appraised value and the purchase price.

That single sentence explains everything that follows from a low appraisal.

4.2 The low appraisal, worked

Contract price $920,000. Buyer putting 20 percent down, so a $736,000 loan. The appraisal comes in at $900,000.

The lender will now lend against $900,000. At 80 percent that is $720,000 — $16,000 less than planned.

The buyer's options:

  1. Bring the additional $16,000, if he has it and chooses to.
  2. Renegotiate the price, in whole or part.
  3. Negotiate another resolution — a credit, a split, a term change.
  4. Cancel, if a contingency permits and its procedure is followed.

What does not happen automatically: the price does not adjust to the appraised value by operation of any loan program; the loan is not denied; and no one is entitled to a second appraisal as of right.

4.3 Where the appraisal contingency has been removed

The buyer's position is materially different, and this is where a licensee must be careful:

  • The practical options remain — bring funds, renegotiate, seek a negotiated resolution.
  • Whether he may cancel and what happens to his deposit is a legal question turning on the contract's terms and the circumstances.
  • The licensee should lay out the practical options, state plainly that the removed contingency limits his contractual rights, and recommend counsel before he decides to stop performing.

What a licensee must not do is tell the buyer he may cancel and recover his deposit because "the property isn't worth the price," or tell him he simply must perform. Both are legal conclusions, and both may be wrong.

4.4 Appraiser independence and the licensee

A licensee may provide genuinely relevant information through the proper channel: sales the licensee believes relevant, information about condition and improvements, a copy of the contract.

A licensee may not communicate a target. "The deal dies without another $25,000" is the request independence rules exist to prevent, and a value that moves after that conversation is unreliable for the purpose the appraisal serves — including when the request comes from the licensee's own client.

4.5 Underwriting conditions

An approval is commonly conditional. Conditions typically address verification — employment, funds, explanations for large deposits, documentation of gift funds — and property matters such as insurance and, where required, repairs.

Two implications for the licensee:

  1. Conditions are not formalities. A prior-to-funding condition that is not satisfied means the loan does not fund.
  2. The facts must hold through funding. An approval rests on the file as underwritten, and lenders commonly re-check credit and employment before funding.

4.6 What jeopardizes an approval during escrow

Advise the buyer of these at the start of escrow, in writing, because by the time they surface it is usually late:

Buyer actionEffect
Opening new credit, financing furniture or a carChanges the debt-to-income analysis the approval rested on
Changing or leaving a jobEmployment is a condition; a change may end the approval
Large unexplained depositsRequires sourcing; may look like undisclosed borrowed funds
Moving money between accountsComplicates asset verification
Paying off debt with funds needed for closingReduces verified cash to close
Missing a payment on any obligationAffects credit at the re-check

A short written note at the start of escrow — do not open credit, change jobs, or move large sums without telling your lender first — prevents more failed transactions than any other single communication a licensee sends.

4.7 A worked underwriting failure

Day 24 of a 35-day escrow. The loan contingency was removed on day 21. The buyer's agent receives a call: underwriting has issued a new condition requiring documentation of a 12,000 deposit made to the buyer's account eleven days ago, and the buyer has told the loan officer it was "money my dad gave me for the down payment."

What has actually happened. A gift, undocumented, deposited during the transaction. It is an ordinary and entirely fixable situation — and every way of making it go away quickly is a way of making it worse.

What the buyer's agent does.

Route it to the lender and stay routed. The condition is the lender's, its cure is the lender's to specify, and Section 3.4's prohibition governs. The licensee does not tell the buyer what to write, does not suggest characterizing the funds differently, and does not propose that the father "just take it back and re-send it later." Lesson 14 of the mortgage course names each of those.

Tell the buyer plainly what is at stake in time. The loan contingency is removed. The deposit is exposed if the loan does not fund, and the condition is now the thing standing between him and closing. This is information he needs today, not on day 33.

Get the actual timeline from the lender in writing: what documents, from whom, and how long after receipt underwriting needs. "We should be fine" is not a timeline and it is what a loan officer under pressure says.

Raise it with the listing side early. Section 6.3's early-warning discipline. A seller who learns on day 24 that there is a condition has options; a seller who learns on day 34 has a problem and a grievance. What is communicated is the fact and its timeline — not a characterization of the buyer's finances, which is his private information.

Prepare for the extension conversation rather than making it at the deadline. Section 6.4 governs, and an extension is the seller's to grant.

What the listing agent does. Takes the information to the seller, sets out the options — extend, hold, or address a failure to perform — and does not advise the seller that the buyer is "probably fine." The seller's decision is his, and the licensee's job is to make sure it is informed and documented.

The retrospective finding. The contingency was removed on day 21 while the file carried an undocumented deposit made on day 13. Nobody asked. Section 4.6 lists what jeopardizes an approval during escrow, and the discipline that would have prevented this is the one in Section 3.6 of Lesson 07: ask the buyer periodically whether anything changed with work, credit, or money, and route the answer to the lender before the removal deadline rather than after.

5. Closing costs and seller credits

5.1 What a seller credit is

A seller credit is an agreed amount the seller contributes toward the buyer's closing costs. It is used to reduce the buyer's cash requirement without reducing the price.

5.2 Two constraints licensees must know

Program limits. Loan programs limit how much a seller may contribute, and the limit varies by program, occupancy, and down payment. A credit exceeding the limit cannot simply be applied; the terms must be restructured, usually late in escrow when there is least time.

The appraisal. This is the point buyers most often get wrong:

A closing cost credit is not a price reduction for appraisal purposes. The property must still appraise at the contract price.

A buyer offers $920,000 with a $15,000 credit rather than $905,000 with none. The seller's net is similar. But the appraisal must now support $920,000 rather than $905,000 — and if it comes in at $900,000, the buyer faces a gap he did not anticipate.

5.3 Comparing offers on net rather than price

Sellers price-shop; licensees should net-compare. Worked:

Offer AOffer B
Price$920,000$895,000
Buyer credit−$15,000$0
Compensation (5%)−$46,000−$44,750
Escrow, title, transfer, recording (est.)−$9,200−$9,000
Net before loan payoff$849,800$841,250

Offer A nets about $8,550 more — and that is not the whole answer. The seller should also weigh: the higher appraisal requirement on A, the financing strength behind each, the contingency periods, and the closing and possession dates. A net comparison that produces a number without that context invites a decision on the number alone.

5.4 Repairs versus credits

Where investigation produces requests, the parties commonly choose between the seller performing repairs and the seller giving a credit.

Seller performsCredit
Control of qualitySeller's contractorBuyer's, after closing
Timing riskWork must finish before closingNone
Lender treatmentMay be required for some programsSubject to credit limits
Dispute riskWorkmanship disputes before closingBuyer bears the outcome

Neither is automatically better. What matters is that the buyer understands which risk he is accepting, and that whatever is agreed is documented in a signed amendment rather than in correspondence between agents.

5.5 Prorations and the closing statement

Continuing charges — property taxes, association dues, certain assessments — are allocated between seller and buyer at the ownership boundary. A seller who prepaid the year is reimbursed for the buyer's portion; a seller who has not paid is charged for theirs.

Licensees should be able to explain the concept and should not attempt to compute the final figures, which escrow prepares.

5.6 The payoff surprise

Sellers routinely expect the payoff to equal the principal balance. It does not: a payoff demand includes accrued interest to the payoff date and any other amounts owed under the note.

Explaining this at listing, on the net sheet, prevents an unhappy conversation at closing — and it is one of the small things that distinguishes a licensee who prepared the client from one who did not.

5.7 Reading an estimated closing statement

Clients receive an estimated statement and look at one number. The licensee's job is to read the rest of it, because errors in a closing statement are found by the people who know what the transaction was supposed to say, and escrow is not one of them.

What to check, in order:

  1. The parties and the property. Names spelled as they appear on title, the property described correctly. A misspelling here follows the file into the recorded instruments.
  2. The sale price and the deposit. Against the agreement and against the receipt.
  3. The loan amount and its terms, against the buyer's lender documents.
  4. Every credit. A seller credit negotiated in an amendment must appear, in the amount the amendment states. Section 5.1's credits are the single most frequently omitted item, because they were agreed late and the amendment reached escrow late or not at all.
  5. Prorations. Taxes, association dues, rent and deposits where the property is tenant-occupied, and any prepaid service. Check the date they are prorated to — a proration to the wrong date is a small error in a familiar place, which is why it survives.
  6. The payoff figures, against the demands, including whether the demand's good-through date covers the actual closing. Section 5.6 covers what happens when it does not.
  7. Fees, against what the parties were told they would pay and against who agreed to pay them.
  8. Commission and any referral or administrative charge, against the agreements — and, on the licensee's own side, against what was disclosed to the client.
  9. The bottom line, which should now be the arithmetic result of everything above rather than the first thing anyone looked at.

Two habits.

Compare against the last estimate, not only against the contract. Statements are revised, and the revision that matters is the one nobody read because it looked like the previous version.

Raise discrepancies immediately and in writing, to escrow, with the document that supports the correction attached. Escrow acts on instructions and documents; a phone call reporting that "the credit is wrong" without the amendment attached produces a delay rather than a correction.

What the licensee does not do: explain the tax consequences of any line, advise on how title should be taken, or tell a client that a charge is "standard." Section 2.4 of Lesson 11 draws the vesting line, and "standard" is a characterization of someone else's fee that the licensee has no basis to make.

6. Financing failure and contingency response

6.1 Respond through the contract

When financing fails, the response runs through the contract's mechanism. A buyer whose loan is denied while the loan contingency is in effect must follow the contract's cancellation procedure — the written notice it requires, within its terms, with any supporting documentation.

What forfeits the protection:

  • Notifying the other agent verbally, on the theory that the denial itself ends the contract.
  • Instructing escrow unilaterally to return the deposit.
  • Ceasing to perform and allowing the seller to cancel for non-performance.

6.2 The seller's side

Where a buyer's financing looks doubtful, the seller's licensee should:

  1. Confirm the contract's dates and what each permits.
  2. Communicate in writing and record the responses.
  3. Deliver the contractual notice where the contract provides one, rather than treating a passed date as self-executing.
  4. Advise the seller on the options — including backup offers and the risks of cancelling prematurely.

A seller who cancels without following the procedure may find the cancellation ineffective and their own position compromised.

6.3 Early warning signs

Financing failures rarely arrive without notice. Signals worth acting on:

  • The buyer changes lenders mid-escrow.
  • Conditions are outstanding well into the period with no movement.
  • The lender stops responding to status requests.
  • The appraisal is delayed or not ordered promptly.
  • The buyer becomes evasive about documentation requests.
  • The buyer mentions a job change, a new purchase, or moving funds.

A licensee who checks status weekly in writing, and records the answers, converts a surprise into a managed problem.

6.4 Extensions

Where the loan needs more time, an extension is a change to a term of the contract and is made by written amendment signed by both parties and delivered to escrow. An email between agents does not extend a contractual date.

The seller's decision whether to extend is the seller's, and it is a real decision: an extension keeps the property committed to a transaction that may still fail.

6.5 What the licensee must not do

  • Do not advise on whether the buyer is entitled to his deposit. That is a legal question.
  • Do not tell a seller she may keep the deposit, or a buyer that he will recover it.
  • Do not instruct escrow on behalf of a party, or attempt to resolve a dispute between the parties.
  • Do not let a contractual date pass while waiting for informal resolution.

6.6 Documenting the failure

Whatever the outcome, the file should show: the contract's financing terms and dates; the status communications and their dates; the notices delivered and the evidence; any amendments; the cancellation or resolution and its date; and the disposition of the deposit, or the fact that it remains unresolved.

A financing failure with a complete file is a business disappointment. The same failure with an incomplete file is a dispute in which the brokerage cannot show what it did.

6.7 After a financing failure

A transaction that fails on financing produces two subsequent problems, and licensees handle the second one badly.

The first problem is the deposit, and Section 6.1 governs. The disposition follows the contract's procedure. It is not decided by which side feels wronged, and it is a broker and counsel matter where it is disputed.

The second problem is what the seller says about the property when it goes back on the market.

What must be disclosed. The property's condition is what it was; the failure was the buyer's financing, not the house. But a transaction that failed after an appraisal came in low has produced information about value — and where the seller now knows the property appraised at a number, that knowledge exists and its treatment is a question for the broker, because whether and how it must be disclosed depends on facts and on law rather than on preference.

What must not be said. "It fell out for personal reasons" when it fell out on a low appraisal is a misrepresentation to the next buyer. The temptation here is strong and the licensee should recognize it as the same category as every other convenient characterization in this course.

What the listing service record should show. The status change, accurately and promptly. Days-on-market treatment is governed by the service's own rules, and manipulating a listing's history by cancelling and relisting to reset a counter is a misrepresentation to the market, whatever the service's mechanics permit.

What the next buyer's agent should ask, and what he is entitled to. Why the prior transaction failed is a fair question. The listing side answers it truthfully or declines to answer — it does not answer it falsely. A licensee whose seller instructs him to give a false reason is in Lesson 03's obedience situation.

The one item that is frequently missed: inspection reports and specialist evaluations obtained by the failed buyer. They belong to the buyer who paid for them, and they are not the seller's to distribute without permission. But where their contents revealed a material fact the seller now knows about his own property, the seller's disclosure obligation runs on his knowledge regardless of where the knowledge came from. The report and the fact are two different things, and only one of them is the buyer's property.

The professional handling in one paragraph: update the disclosures with anything the failed transaction taught the seller about the property, tell the truth about why it failed, change the listing status accurately, keep the failed file complete, and let the next buyer make an informed decision. A property that comes back to market with an updated disclosure package is a stronger listing than one that comes back pretending nothing happened — and the second one produces the claim.

6.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to advise a buyer facing a low appraisal with and without an appraisal contingency, name six events during escrow that jeopardize a conditional approval, and state how a closing date is extended.

Key terms

TermDefinition
The financing boundaryExplain how financing works generally; refer the specific recommendation to a qualified lender. The moment the question becomes "which is better for me," it belongs to the lender.
PrequalificationAn assessment on largely stated information. A rough range; verification of nothing.
PreapprovalA lender's review of supporting information. A likely amount subject to conditions — not a commitment to lend.
Loan commitmentUnderwriting complete. Substantially more certainty, and not immunity from changed circumstances.
Conventional loanA loan not insured or guaranteed by a government agency. Mortgage insurance is commonly required below a program's down payment threshold.
Government-backed programA loan where an agency insures or guarantees the lender against certain losses. The government is not the lender, the loans bear ordinary interest, and they are limited neither to new construction nor to first-time buyers as a general matter.
Property condition requirementsStandards common to government programs. A closing-probability question for a seller weighing an offer on a property with deferred maintenance — not a preference.
Index plus marginWhat determines an adjustable rate at each adjustment, subject to the note's caps. Not lender discretion and not the borrower's credit at that time.
Interest-only periodA period whose scheduled payments cover interest only; principal is not retired during it.
BalloonA structure whose scheduled payments do not retire principal by maturity, leaving a large sum due.
OccupancyOwner-occupied, second home, or investment. Affects pricing, eligibility, and which protections apply — and is therefore a material fact the lender relies on. Misstating it is loan fraud.
Loan estimate and final disclosureThe early estimate of loan terms and costs, and the final disclosure before consummation, with a review period. The licensee ensures the buyer knows they are coming, reads them, compares them, and asks the lender.
Re-disclosureThe consequence of certain late changes: new disclosures, and a timeline that can move the closing date.
Referral fee prohibitionA licensee may not accept anything of value for referring a buyer to a settlement service provider. Referrals happen; payment for them does not, and disclosure does not cure it.
Lower of value or priceThe rule that explains every consequence of a low appraisal: lenders lend against the lower of appraised value and purchase price.
Appraisal gapThe difference the buyer must resolve when the appraisal falls below the contract price. Resolved by funds, renegotiation, another negotiated outcome, or cancellation under a contingency — never automatically.
Underwriting conditionA requirement that must be satisfied for the loan to fund. Not a formality: an unsatisfied prior-to-funding condition means no funding.
The facts must holdAn approval rests on the file as underwritten, and lenders commonly re-check credit and employment before funding.
Seller creditAn agreed seller contribution toward the buyer's closing costs, reducing the buyer's cash requirement without reducing the price. Constrained by program limits and by the appraisal.
ProrationAllocation of continuing charges — taxes, dues, assessments — at the ownership boundary. Escrow computes the final figures.
Payoff demandWhat satisfies the loan: principal plus accrued interest to the payoff date and other amounts owed under the note.
Respond through the contractThe rule when financing fails: the written notice the contract requires, within its terms, with supporting documentation.

Practice problems

Problem 1

A seller receives two offers at the same price. Buyer A's letter is two lines from an unnamed source, verifies nothing, and expires in ten days. Buyer B's letter names the lender, states that income, assets, employment, and credit were reviewed, lists two remaining conditions, and states the amount for this property.

What should the seller's agent do with these, and what may she not say?

Solution. Read them and advise on closing probability — which is half of what the seller is actually choosing between. Noting that both offers "came with a preapproval" is not advice.

The questions each letter should answer: what was verified; what conditions remain; what program and terms it assumes; whether the amount is a maximum or the amount for this property; when it expires; and who issued it. Buyer A's letter answers almost none of them, and that is real information the seller is entitled to when choosing.

What she may not do is predict what either lender will approve, or recommend a program to either buyer. She explains what makes an offer read as strong; she does not underwrite it.

The buyer-side mirror: the same reading, for a different purpose. A buyer who can obtain a fully underwritten preapproval before offering is materially more competitive, and that is advice worth giving early rather than after he loses a property.

Problem 2

A buyer intends to rent out the property but asks the licensee to help him "put it down as owner-occupied since the rate is better — I can afford either payment anyway."

Respond, and explain why the affordability point does not matter.

Solution. Decline plainly, explain why, and record the loan as it actually is.

Occupancy affects pricing, program eligibility, and which consumer protections apply. It is therefore a material fact the lender relies on, and misstating it is loan fraud — participation in which is what the buyer is requesting, however casually phrased.

Affordability is beside the point because the misstatement is not about whether the buyer will pay. It is about obtaining terms on a false representation of the risk the lender is pricing.

What transfers: this is the same principle as undisclosed borrowed funds from a relative, seen from the occupancy side — a hidden fact that undermines the analysis the lender actually performed.

Problem 3

Three weeks before closing, a buyer tells his agent he has found a lender offering a slightly better rate and wants to switch, and separately asks whether the escrow fee on his estimate is "about right."

What are the consequences of the switch, and how should the fee question be handled?

Solution. Switching lenders mid-escrow restarts substantial parts of the process, and the agent's obligation is to tell him plainly what it does to the closing date before he decides — not after. Other changes with the same character: changing program or term, a re-lock after a rate lock expires, a fee increasing beyond its tolerance, and a loan amount change from a property or appraisal issue. Each can require re-disclosure, and certain late changes can restart the waiting period before consummation.

The practical corollary: do not promise a seller a closing date that a late change makes impossible.

On the fee: it is a lender question. Do not tell a buyer a fee is "standard" or a figure is "about right," and do not compare loan offers for him as though selecting between them. Direct it to the lender, which is the party that can answer it.

Problem 4

Contract price $920,000; the buyer is putting 20 percent down. The appraisal comes in at $900,000. The buyer's agent tells him the price will automatically adjust to the appraised value and that he can demand a second appraisal.

Work the numbers and correct the advice.

Solution. The lender lends against the lower of appraised value and price, so against $900,000. At 80 percent that is $720,000, against the $736,000 planned — a $16,000 shortfall in loan proceeds.

The buyer's options: bring the additional $16,000; renegotiate the price in whole or part; negotiate another resolution such as a credit, a split, or a term change; or cancel if a contingency permits and its procedure is followed.

Both statements to the buyer are wrong. The price does not adjust to appraised value by operation of any loan program, the loan is not denied, and no one is entitled to a second appraisal as of right.

Problem 5

Same facts, except the buyer removed the appraisal contingency to win in competition. He asks his agent whether he can now cancel and get his deposit back "since the property isn't worth the price."

What may the agent say?

Solution. The practical options are unchanged — bring funds, renegotiate, seek a negotiated resolution — and the agent should lay them out.

What the agent must say next: the removed contingency limits his contractual rights, and whether he may cancel and what happens to his deposit is a legal question turning on the contract's terms and the circumstances. Recommend counsel before he decides to stop performing.

Two answers the agent must not give, and they fail in opposite directions: that he may cancel and recover his deposit because the property "isn't worth the price," and that he simply must perform. Both are legal conclusions, and both may be wrong.

Problem 6

Two weeks into escrow, a buyer finances $9,000 of furniture, moves $22,000 from a relative's account into his checking account, and pays off a car loan using part of his down payment funds. His agent learns this the week before closing.

Assess each act, and state what should have happened at the start of escrow.

Solution. All three attack the file the approval rested on.

Financing furniture changes the debt-to-income analysis. The $22,000 deposit requires sourcing and may look like undisclosed borrowed funds — the gift-versus-loan distinction again. Paying off the car with closing funds reduces verified cash to close, solving one ratio by breaking another.

Underlying all three: an approval rests on the file as underwritten, and lenders commonly re-check credit and employment before funding. Conditions are not formalities — an unsatisfied prior-to-funding condition means the loan does not fund.

What should have happened: a short written note at the start of escrow — do not open credit, change jobs, or move large sums without telling your lender first. Also on that list: leaving a job, moving money between accounts, and missing a payment on any obligation. That single communication prevents more failed transactions than any other a licensee sends.

Problem 7

A buyer offers $920,000 with a $15,000 seller credit rather than $905,000 with none, and tells his agent the two are equivalent to the seller and that the credit protects him if value is soft. Separately, the investigation produces a repair request and the parties are choosing between the seller performing the work and giving a credit; the agents confirm the choice by email.

Correct the buyer, and assess the repair decision.

Solution. A credit is not a price reduction for appraisal purposes. The property must appraise at $920,000, not $905,000. If it comes in at $900,000 the gap is $20,000 rather than $5,000 — so the structure he thinks protects him against soft value increases his exposure to it. A second constraint he has not considered: program limits cap what a seller may contribute, and a credit exceeding the limit must be restructured, usually late in escrow when there is least time.

On repairs versus credit: neither is automatically better. Seller performs gives the seller's contractor control of quality, carries timing risk before closing, may be required by some programs, and creates workmanship disputes before closing. A credit shifts quality control and outcome to the buyer after closing, carries no timing risk, and is subject to credit limits. What matters is that the buyer understands which risk he is accepting.

The email is the defect. Whatever is agreed goes into a signed amendment, not correspondence between agents, who are not the parties.

Problem 8

A buyer's loan is denied. He tells the listing agent by phone, emails escrow to release his deposit, and stops responding. The listing agent tells the seller the contract is over, that she may keep the deposit, and that a two-week extension agreed by email would have saved it anyway.

Identify every error.

Solution. Errors on both sides of the transaction.

The buyer forfeited his protection rather than exercising it. A denial does not end the contract by itself. The loan contingency provides a path with requirements: the written notice the contract requires, within its terms, with supporting documentation. A verbal notification to the other agent, a unilateral instruction to escrow, and simply ceasing to perform each abandon it — the last invites cancellation for non-performance, a materially worse position.

The seller's agent gave three wrong answers. The contract is not "over" because a date passed or a loan was denied — the seller confirms the contract's dates, communicates in writing, and delivers the contractual notice rather than treating anything as self-executing; a seller who cancels without following the procedure may find the cancellation ineffective and her own position compromised. Whether she may keep the deposit is a legal question the licensee must not answer. And an email between agents does not extend a contractual date — an extension is a change to a term, made by signed amendment delivered to escrow, and whether to grant one is the seller's real decision, since it keeps the property committed to a transaction that may still fail.

What transfers: most of this was visible early. Changing lenders mid-escrow, conditions outstanding with no movement, a lender that stops responding, a delayed appraisal, evasiveness about documentation, a mentioned job change — a licensee who checks status weekly in writing converts a surprise into a managed problem. A financing failure with a complete file is a business disappointment; the same failure with an incomplete file is a dispute the brokerage cannot answer.

Chapter summary

A licensee explains how financing works and refers the recommendation to a lender. The understanding exists to advise a buyer on his position, advise a seller on closing probability, and recognize trouble early.

Prequalification, preapproval, and commitment are three levels, none of which guarantees funding. Read the letter rather than noting its existence.

Government-backed does not mean government-lent. Private lenders make the loans, the loans bear ordinary interest, and property condition requirements are a closing-probability fact a seller is entitled to weigh.

An adjustable rate is set by index plus margin within the note's caps — not by lender discretion or the borrower's later credit.

Occupancy is a material fact the lender relies on, and misstating it is fraud regardless of whether the buyer could afford the payment either way.

The licensee's role in loan disclosures is procedural, and late changes can move the closing — which is why a seller should never be promised a date a proposed change makes impossible.

Never accept anything of value for a settlement service referral. Disclosure does not cure it.

Lenders lend against the lower of appraised value and price. Everything about a low appraisal follows from that sentence, and nothing about the resolution is automatic.

Where the appraisal contingency was removed, the practical options remain but the contractual rights are limited — and entitlement to the deposit is a legal question for counsel, not for the licensee.

Supply an appraiser information, never a target — including when the request comes from your own client.

Conditions are not formalities and the facts must hold through funding. The written start-of-escrow warning about credit, employment, and moving funds prevents more failures than anything else a licensee sends.

A seller credit is constrained twice — by program limits and by the appraisal, which must still support the full contract price.

Compare offers on net, then on everything the net does not capture: appraisal requirement, financing strength, contingency periods, closing and possession.

Explain the payoff at listing. It includes accrued interest and other amounts under the note, not the principal balance alone.

When financing fails, respond through the contract — written notice, within its terms, with evidence. Extensions are signed amendments, not agent emails, and the deposit question belongs to the parties, their contract, and if necessary the courts.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. State the buyer's practical options and which of them the contract's terms permit, identify what you would recommend be referred to counsel and why, state the notices the next step requires and their delivery method, prepare the net comparison the seller needs, and identify the evidence you would retain. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Practice - Lesson Assignment 11

Escrow, title, closing, and possession

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Explain the escrow holder's neutrality and the limits it places on what escrow may do.
  • Read a preliminary report, distinguish an exception from a defect, and route each item correctly.
  • Explain why the manner of taking title is a legal and tax question outside the licensee's scope.
  • Coordinate the transaction's parallel tracks and identify what must be confirmed before closing.
  • Conduct a final verification of condition and respond correctly to what it reveals.
  • Distinguish recording, funding, disbursement, and possession, and document post-closing obligations.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: escrow's role and title review55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: vesting, coordination, and final verification50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: closing mechanics, possession, and post-closing audit45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Opening escrow

1.1 What escrow is

Escrow is a neutral third party holding funds and documents under the parties' conditional delivery, authorized to act only according to their mutual written instructions.

Two elements make it an escrow: a binding contract between the parties, and conditional delivery to a neutral holder. Without both, what exists is a stakeholder arrangement, not an escrow.

1.2 Neutrality, and what it forbids

Escrow's neutrality is not a courtesy. It is the structural feature that lets both parties rely on the same holder, and it produces limits licensees must be able to explain:

Escrow mayEscrow may not
Follow mutual written instructionsAct on one party's instruction alone
Hold funds and documentsAdvise either party
Prepare and circulate documents per the instructionsDecide a dispute between the parties
Record and disburse when conditions are metRelease funds because one party says they are entitled
Report status to both partiesTake a position on who is right

Escrow is sometimes described as a limited dual agent — owing duties to both parties simultaneously, bounded strictly by the instructions. That description is useful precisely because it explains why escrow stops rather than deciding when the parties disagree.

1.3 A worked test of neutrality

The day before recording, the buyer emails escrow: hold back $3,000 from the seller's proceeds for a repair the seller promised verbally. The seller knows nothing about it.

Escrow may not act on it. A change to the disbursement requires mutual written instructions. One party cannot redirect funds.

Nor may escrow simply disburse in full, which also acts on one side's position where a genuine dispute has been raised.

Nor does the buyer's agent confirming the request supply the missing consent. An agent is not the other party.

The correct outcome: escrow stops. The parties resolve it in writing, and the resolution reaches escrow as a mutual instruction. The agents' job is to get that done before the closing date, not to argue with escrow.

1.4 Who selects escrow

Selection is negotiable between the parties and is often addressed in the purchase agreement. Two points for the licensee:

  • A licensee must not accept anything of value for directing settlement business. Referrals are ordinary; payment for them is a serious federal problem, and disclosure does not cure it.
  • Where the brokerage has a financial interest in an escrow or title provider, that is an affiliated business arrangement, and it carries disclosure and other conditions.

1.5 Opening properly

What escrow needs at opening: the fully executed purchase agreement and all amendments; the parties' contact and identification details; the deposit per the contract; lender contact where financing is involved; and title order details.

The licensee's contribution is accuracy and completeness. An escrow opened on a partially executed contract, or without the amendment that changed a term, will produce instructions that do not match the agreement — which surfaces later, usually at the worst time.

1.6 Escrow instructions, and amending them

Escrow acts on instructions. That sentence explains most of what goes wrong.

The instructions are not the purchase agreement. They are a separate set of directions to the escrow holder, and where the two differ, escrow follows what it was instructed to do. A term negotiated in an amendment that never reached escrow does not exist as far as escrow is concerned, which is why Section 5.7 of Lesson 10 finds missing credits on closing statements.

What this means in practice:

  1. Every amendment goes to escrow when it is signed, not in a batch before closing. The licensee's transmittal is dated and recorded.
  2. Instructions are amended in writing by the parties, and the escrow holder will not act on an agent's phone call.
  3. Both parties must instruct. A change one party wants is not a change until the other has instructed it too, and a licensee who tells his client "it's handled" after a one-sided call has misinformed him.
  4. Conflicting instructions stop escrow rather than being resolved by it. Section 1.2's neutrality forbids the escrow holder from deciding between the parties, so a conflict produces a hold, not a ruling.
  5. The licensee does not draft instructions or interpret them for the client. Their legal effect is a legal question.

Two situations that recur.

The late verbal change. A seller agrees on the phone to a credit two days before closing. Unless it becomes a signed amendment and reaches escrow, the closing statement will not show it, and the discovery happens at signing with everyone in a room. The fix is the same day: reduce it to writing, get both signatures, send it to escrow, confirm receipt.

The instruction the client did not read. Clients sign escrow instructions the way they sign everything, and then are surprised by a provision. The licensee's contribution is to tell the client, before signing, that these are operative directions rather than formalities, and that questions about their effect go to counsel — not to answer the questions himself.

A useful mental model: the purchase agreement is what the parties owe each other. The escrow instructions are what a neutral third party has been told to do. Keeping them aligned is a task somebody has to perform on every file, and on most files that somebody is the licensee.

2. Title and vesting review

2.1 What a preliminary report is

A preliminary report reports the condition of title as shown in the records and states the terms on which the title insurer would be willing to issue a policy, including the exceptions it would take.

Three things it is not:

  • Not a guarantee that title is clear.
  • Not the policy. It is superseded by the policy, not replaced by it.
  • Not a boundary or condition survey. It addresses record title, not the physical property.

2.2 Exceptions, and what they mean

An exception is an item the policy will not insure against. That is the entire significance, and it is why exceptions must be read rather than skimmed:

If an item is excepted, the buyer bears that risk unless it is otherwise addressed.

Common exceptions include easements, covenants and restrictions, mineral rights reservations, assessments, and matters a survey would disclose.

2.3 Reading the report as a licensee

The licensee's role is to ensure the buyer reviews the underlying documents and understands their effect on his intended use — and to route legal questions to counsel.

ItemCorrect handling
Utility easement along the rear lineObtain and review the document: width, location, permitted use. A rear-line easement can prevent structures the buyer intends to build.
Recorded CC&RsObtain and review; use restrictions bind the buyer regardless of zoning
An unreleased deed of trust the seller says was paidA recorded reconveyance or other clearing document must be obtained; the seller's recollection does not clear the record
A mechanic's lienRoute to escrow and title; note that its priority may relate back to when work commenced
A judgment lien against a similarly named personRoute to title for resolution; do not assume it is the seller
An item the buyer does not understandRefer to counsel

Three responses that are wrong in every case: treating an item as routine because most properties have one, asking the title company to "remove" an exception, and predicting the practical effect of an easement on the buyer's plans.

2.4 Vesting is not the licensee's question

How the buyer takes title — sole ownership, joint tenancy, tenancy in common, community property in its forms, a trust, an entity — carries ownership, survivorship, tax, and estate consequences.

A licensee may explain that the choice matters and that it has those consequences. A licensee may not advise which to choose. The correct action is a referral to legal and tax counsel before escrow prepares the deed, because changing it later is neither free nor simple.

Two unmarried siblings buying together say they want the survivor to take the whole property automatically, and each to be able to leave her share to her own children.

Those two goals point in different directions. Resolving that tension is estate planning, not a form selection, and it belongs to counsel.

2.5 Title insurance

The owner's policy insures the buyer's interest; the lender's policy insures the lender's lien and its priority. They insure different interests, which is why both commonly exist.

What a policy does: indemnifies the insured against covered losses from title defects, within its terms and excluding what it excepts. What it does not do: guarantee perfect title, insure physical condition, or oblige the insurer to clear what it excepted.

2.6 A worked preliminary report review

The report on a suburban parcel lists, among the exceptions: general and special taxes for the current fiscal year; a supplemental assessment; a deed of trust in favor of a lender; an easement for public utilities recorded in 1962; covenants, conditions and restrictions recorded in 1958; an easement for ingress and egress in favor of the adjoining parcel recorded in 1979; a mechanic's lien recorded four months ago; a notice of a special assessment district; and an abstract of judgment against a person with the same surname as the seller.

How a licensee reads this — and Section 2.3's boundary applies throughout: he identifies items that require attention and routes the legal questions.

The mechanic's lien is the first item. It is recent, it is a monetary encumbrance, and it must be resolved or addressed before the buyer takes title. It also tells a story: work was done on the property recently and someone was not paid. That is a fact about the property's condition and history, and it belongs in the disclosure conversation as well as the title conversation.

The abstract of judgment against a similar name is the item most often mishandled. It may be the seller. It may be an unrelated person with the same surname. This is resolved by the title company through its own process, and the licensee's role is to flag it early — because if it is the seller, it must be dealt with, and if it is not, clearing the record takes time nobody scheduled.

The 1979 ingress and egress easement is a use question the buyer needs to understand. Someone has a recorded right to cross this property. Where it runs, how wide it is, and what it permits are answered by the recorded document, which the buyer should obtain and read. A buyer planning a fence, a structure, or a landscaping project across it will discover the problem later at greater cost.

The 1958 covenants and restrictions may constrain use — setbacks, permitted structures, architectural approval, or provisions that are no longer enforceable. Which provisions still operate is a legal question, and it is one buyers ask licensees constantly.

The utility easement from 1962 is ordinary and still worth locating, for the same reason as the ingress easement.

The special assessment district and the supplemental assessment are cost items the buyer's budget has probably not included, and the supplemental bill in particular surprises first-time buyers after closing. Section 5.5 of Lesson 10 and this exception are the same subject arriving from two directions.

The deed of trust is expected and will be paid at closing from the seller's proceeds.

What the licensee does. Deliver the report to the buyer promptly with a written recommendation to review it with the title officer and, for the recorded documents, with counsel; obtain the underlying documents for the easements and restrictions rather than relying on the one-line description; raise the lien and the judgment with escrow and the listing side immediately; and record the delivery and the recommendation.

What the licensee does not do. Tell the buyer the restrictions are "old and unenforceable," that the easement is "just for the utility company," that the judgment is "probably a different person," or that the lien is "the seller's problem." Four characterizations, none of which he has a basis for, each of which a buyer will rely on.

3. Transaction coordination

3.1 Parallel tracks

A residential transaction runs several tracks at once, each with its own deadlines and its own failure modes:

TrackOwnerTypical failure
Contract deadlinesThe agentsA notice missed or delivered by the wrong method
InvestigationBuyer, with the agent's coordinationStarted late, so findings arrive with no time to act
Disclosure deliverySeller and both agentsDelivered late; delivery not evidenced
FinancingBuyer and lenderConditions unsatisfied; a change restarts the timeline
AppraisalLenderOrdered late; value below price
TitleEscrow and titleAn exception discovered late that takes time to clear
EscrowEscrowInstructions that do not match the agreement
InsuranceBuyerAvailability or cost, especially in mapped hazard areas

Most transactions that fail do not fail because one track was impossible. They fail because a track started late.

3.2 What the licensee actually coordinates

  1. Build the deadline calendar from the contract and share it in writing.
  2. Start the slow items first — insurance in hazard areas, association documents, permit research, specialist inspections.
  3. Confirm status weekly in writing, and record the answers.
  4. Escalate early. A problem raised on day ten has options; the same problem on day twenty-eight has few.
  5. Reconcile escrow's instructions against the agreement, including every amendment.

3.3 The insurance track

Insurance deserves its own attention in California. In mapped fire and flood areas, availability and cost have become a common cause of failed transactions, and the problem is discovered late because buyers treat insurance as a formality.

The advice: obtain quotes early in the contingency period, not at the end. A buyer who learns in week one that coverage will cost far more than assumed can act; a buyer who learns it two days before closing generally cannot.

3.4 Communication that prevents disputes

A short written status note at each milestone — what has been delivered, what remains, what the next date is — does more to prevent disputes than any other practice, because most disputes are about whether something was delivered and when.

3.5 How coordination fails

Section 3.1 describes the parallel tracks. Coordination failures have a small number of recognizable shapes, and each has a control.

The failureWhat it looks likeThe control
The unowned taskEveryone assumed someone else ordered the association documentsA written task list with a name against each item, made at opening
The silent dependencyThe appraisal cannot be scheduled until access is arranged with a tenant nobody has contactedMap what each item waits on, not just when it is due
The stale assumptionWork continues on the belief that a condition cleared last weekConfirm status rather than assume continuity; verify at each check-in
The one-way messageAn email sent, no response, no follow-up, and the recipient never saw itFollow-up is scheduled, not remembered; no consequential item rests on an unacknowledged message
The out-of-band changeParties agree to something in a phone call and no document followsSame-day written confirmation of every agreement, without exception
The vacation gapA participant is out and their work stops without anyone knowingAsk who covers, at opening, for every party
The last-week compressionFour items all land in the final three daysFront-load: order everything orderable in week one

The two that cause the most damage are the unowned task and the silent dependency, because both are invisible until the deadline. Nothing looks wrong — no one is behind on anything, because no one has started.

The practical instrument is a single document, made the day escrow opens, listing every item the transaction needs, who owns it, what it depends on, and when it must be complete. It is reviewed at each check-in, and the review takes minutes.

What it replaces is a licensee holding the entire transaction in his head, which works until he has four transactions, and then fails on all four at once. Lesson 02's capacity analysis and this document are the same idea applied at two scales.

One communication rule sits above all of it. Section 3.4 explains why communication prevents disputes; the operational form is that the client hears from the licensee on a schedule, including when nothing has happened. A client who has to ask what is going on has already begun to lose confidence, and most cancellation requests — Section 6.3 of Lesson 04 — start there rather than with an actual failure.

4. Final verification and closing

4.1 What the final verification is

The buyer's final verification of condition — the walkthrough — confirms that the property is in the condition the contract requires: agreed repairs completed, included items present, and the property not materially changed since the buyer's investigation.

It is not an inspection, and it is not a new opportunity to renegotiate condition the buyer already accepted.

4.2 What to check

  • Agreed repairs, against the amendment that agreed them, with any documentation required.
  • Included personal property present — appliances, fixtures, window coverings, anything named in the contract.
  • Systems operating as they were.
  • No new damage, including damage from the seller's move-out.
  • Property vacant where the contract requires it, and personal property removed.

4.3 Responding to a problem

At the final verification the buyer finds an agreed repair was not completed.

The correct path: document the finding — photographs, dated notes — and address it through the contract before closing, because the ordinary leverage to resolve it disappears once the transaction closes.

What does not work: closing as scheduled and pursuing the seller afterward, which converts a solvable issue into a claim; instructing escrow to withhold funds, which escrow cannot do without mutual instruction; or treating the walkthrough as informational because it is not an inspection.

4.4 The closing sequence

Four events that licensees regularly conflate, in order:

  1. Signing. The parties execute the closing documents. The transaction is not closed.
  2. Funding. The lender releases loan proceeds to escrow, subject to its conditions.
  3. Recording. The deed and security instrument are recorded, establishing the interests of record and giving constructive notice. This is generally when the buyer becomes the owner of record.
  4. Disbursement. Escrow disburses once recording is confirmed.

Recording precedes disbursement deliberately: it prevents a gap in which money has moved but the interests are not of record.

4.5 Recording order matters

Where multiple instruments record, the order establishes lien priority. An out-of-sequence recording can place a new lender behind an interest it was to be ahead of. This is why closing is sequenced rather than simultaneous, and it is not a clerical preference.

4.6 Wire fraud at closing

The highest-risk moment in the transaction is when the buyer sends funds. The pattern is covered in Lesson 02; the closing-specific practices are:

  1. Warn the buyer in writing, early, that wire instructions will never change by email and that he must verify by voice before sending.
  2. Verify by calling escrow at an independently obtained number — never a number from the email carrying the instructions.
  3. Treat any change to instructions as presumptively fraudulent until verified through that independent channel.

A licensee who sends that warning at the start of escrow, rather than the day funds move, has done the single most valuable thing available to protect a client's money.

4.7 A worked final verification

The walkthrough, the afternoon before a scheduled closing. The buyer and his agent find:

  • (a) The refrigerator is gone. It appeared in the listing photographs and was not excluded in the agreement.
  • (b) A repair the seller agreed to make in a signed amendment — replacing a failed water heater — has been done, but the unit is not strapped and there is no permit record.
  • (c) Water staining on the garage ceiling that was not present at the inspection five weeks ago.
  • (d) The seller's belongings fill half the garage. He says the movers come "the day after close."
  • (e) A hole in a bedroom wall where a mounted television was removed.
  • (f) The rear slider does not lock; it did at the inspection.

Sorting these, because they are not the same kind of problem.

(c) is the serious one. New water intrusion since the inspection is a change in the property's condition, and its cause is unknown. It may be a roof failure, a plumbing failure, or the new water heater in item (b). The buyer is being asked to close tomorrow on a property with an active unexplained condition. This is escalated immediately — to both brokers — and it is a legitimate basis for the buyer to seek a delay while it is evaluated. It is not resolved by a credit negotiated in a hallway.

(b) is a performance question with a safety component. The amendment required a replacement; what was delivered may not meet the requirements applicable to the work. Whether the amendment was satisfied is a contract question, and the permit and strapping issues are matters for the appropriate authority and a qualified professional rather than for the licensees' judgment.

(d) is a possession problem and it is bigger than it looks. Belongings in the garage after closing means the seller occupies the property after title transfers, which Section 5.2 addresses. A day is not nothing — it raises insurance, liability, condition, and what happens if the movers do not come. It requires a written agreement, not a handshake at the walkthrough.

(a) and (e) are ordinary items — a fixture-versus-personal-property question and damage from removing one. Section 1.6 of Lesson 08 explains why the refrigerator question exists at all. They are negotiated or credited, and the resolution is written.

(f) is small and belongs on the list anyway, because a lock that no longer works is a security item on the property the buyer takes possession of tomorrow.

What the agents do, in order.

Document everything now — photographs, dated, with locations, before anyone moves anything or begins negotiating.

Notify both brokers and escrow the same day, because a closing scheduled for tomorrow may not happen and every participant's schedule depends on knowing.

Take the items to the parties as facts rather than as demands. The buyer decides what he wants; Section 4.3 governs the response and the options belong to him.

Reduce whatever is agreed to writing before closing, and get it to escrow — Section 1.6 of this lesson.

What must not happen: closing on a verbal promise to fix item (c) afterward, allowing the seller to remain in the garage without a written agreement, or a licensee reassuring his client that the staining is "probably from the old heater" — a characterization of a building condition by a person not qualified to make it, and one made under the pressure of a closing date.

5. Recording, disbursement, and possession

5.1 Possession is not ownership

Ownership transfers at recording. Possession transfers when the contract says it does, which is frequently the same day and frequently not.

Where they diverge, the arrangement must be documented.

5.2 Post-closing occupancy

Where the seller remains after closing, a written occupancy agreement should address:

TermWhy
Duration and end dateThe holdover risk is the whole problem
PaymentWhat the seller pays, if anything
Condition on deliveryThe standard the property must meet
InsuranceWho insures what, in whose name
Utilities and maintenanceResponsibility during the period
Deposit or holdbackSecurity for damage or holdover
Remedy for failure to vacateThe point at which this becomes expensive

A brief term does not reduce the need for terms. "They only need three days" is how a holdover with no agreed remedy begins, and the buyer now owns a property occupied by someone with no obligation he can enforce quickly.

5.3 Early possession before closing

Riskier still, and generally to be discouraged. A buyer occupying before closing who then fails to close leaves the seller with an occupant and a property that must be re-marketed. Where it happens at all, it should be documented at least as carefully as post-closing occupancy, with counsel involved.

5.4 Keys, access, and the transfer of control

Practical items that generate complaints when handled loosely: when keys transfer and from whom; garage and gate remotes, mailbox keys, association access devices; changing codes on smart locks and alarms; and confirming the seller's remote access to cameras and smart devices is removed.

That last one has become a real issue. A seller who retains app access to interior cameras after closing is a problem, and it should be addressed explicitly rather than assumed.

5.5 Utilities and services

Coordinate transfer dates so that services are not interrupted and neither party is billed for the other's period. This is administrative rather than legal, and it is a common source of avoidable client dissatisfaction.

5.6 Confirming closing to the client

When recording is confirmed, tell the client — in writing, with the date and what it means. Clients frequently do not know whether they own the property, and the licensee is the person they will ask.

5.7 Week one after closing

Most post-closing calls arrive in the first ten days, and they follow a short list. Knowing the list lets a licensee handle each correctly rather than defensively.

The callThe correct handling
"The [system] doesn't work."Sympathy, then process. What the contract provided, whether a warranty applies, and — where the buyer believes a condition was concealed — a matter for the broker rather than an argument between agents. The licensee does not diagnose and does not concede.
"There's a bill I wasn't expecting."Frequently a supplemental tax assessment or an association charge. Explain what it is; the amount and its calculation come from the assessing authority or the association.
"The seller left things behind."A cleanup and possession question. Where a post-closing occupancy agreement exists, it governs; where it does not, this is why Section 5.2 requires one.
"The keys don't work for the side gate."Section 5.4's transfer of control was incomplete. Fix it, and note for future files that "keys" means every point of access.
"Nobody told me about [X]."The most important call on this list. Determine what the file shows was delivered and when, before responding. Do not answer from memory — Section 5.2 of Lesson 09 exists for this moment.
"Can you send me everything for my taxes?"Provide copies of the transaction documents. The tax questions themselves go to the client's tax advisor, without exception and without a "generally speaking."
"My neighbor says the property line isn't where I think."A survey and legal question. Refer, and do not offer an opinion about the boundary.

Three disciplines apply to all of them.

Respond quickly, even when the answer is "I need to check." Most post-closing complaints escalate because of silence rather than because of substance.

Check the file before answering. A licensee who says "you were definitely given that" and turns out to be wrong has converted a service problem into a credibility problem.

Loop in the broker on anything that sounds like a claim. Not later, and not only if it develops. The first communication about a potential claim is the one that matters most, and it should not be improvised by the person whose work is being questioned.

And one thing not to do: offer to pay for something out of your own pocket to make a complaint go away. It feels generous, it is fast, and it is a decision with implications for the brokerage, for insurance, and for what the payment is later said to mean. That decision belongs to the broker.

6. Post-closing file audit

6.1 Audit while memories are fresh

The most useful review of a transaction file happens shortly after closing. Its purpose is to confirm the file can answer questions later, and to catch anything obtainable while the participants still respond to messages.

6.2 The audit questions

  1. Completeness. Every required document present — agency disclosures, agreement, offer and counteroffers, disclosures, notices, amendments, closing documents.
  2. Dates. Every document dated, and delivery evidenced where timing matters.
  3. Consistency. Names, dates, amounts, and property descriptions reconcile across documents.
  4. Chronology. The sequence makes sense against the contract's deadlines — the agency disclosure precedes the agreement, disclosures precede contingency removal, amendments precede the dates they change.
  5. Nothing improper present. No unexecuted draft mistaken for final, no superseded figure presented as current.

6.3 Keep the superseded versions

The instinct to keep only final documents should be resisted. The sequence of versions is frequently what shows when a term changed and who agreed to it. A file containing only executed finals can prove what was agreed but not how the parties arrived there — which is the question a dispute usually asks.

6.4 What to do with findings

  • Obtain what legitimately exists — a missing copy, an unrecorded acknowledgment.
  • Record what does not, plainly.
  • Escalate to the broker.
  • Address the process that produced the gap, not only the instance.

6.5 The line

Creating a document now and dating it to match the transaction, or removing surrounding records so an inconsistency does not show, is falsification.

A documented gap is survivable. A manufactured document is not, and it converts a compliance failure into conduct that ends a career.

The same principle governs corrections generally: they are made visibly, with the original preserved and the change attributable. A record that can be silently altered is not a record.

6.6 Retention

The file is retained for the required period, which runs from closing. Cancelled and failed transactions are retained too — and are disproportionately the ones that later produce disputes, which is exactly why they must not be discarded as uninteresting.

6.7 What the client asks for later

The relationship does not end at closing, and the requests that arrive months or years afterward have their own rules.

Copies of documents. Ordinary and easily satisfied — if the file is complete and findable. This is the least dramatic argument for the retention discipline in Section 6.6 and the one that actually comes up: a client refinancing, appealing an assessment, settling an estate, or preparing a return needs a document from a transaction the licensee handled, and either it is in the file or it is not.

Tax questions. Referred, every time. Holding period, basis, exclusions, exchanges, and the treatment of credits and improvements are the tax advisor's work. "I'm not a tax advisor, and here is why that question is worth asking one" is a complete answer, and the client is better served by it than by a confident summary.

A value opinion. Section 5.5 of Lesson 05 governs. Ask what it is for before preparing it, because the answer determines whether the licensee may prepare it at all.

Referrals to service providers. Section 5.6 of Lesson 03 applies unchanged. Time does not extinguish the disclosure obligation — a referral fee received on a contractor referral two years after closing is the same conflict it would have been during escrow.

Information about the other party. A former client asking for the buyer's contact information, or for something the licensee learned about him during the transaction. Confidentiality survives, and information about a party who is not the licensee's client was never his to distribute.

"Would you tell me what you'd do?" The former client is asking for advice — about a refinance, a rental, an addition, a family transfer. The categories that were outside the licensee's competence during the transaction are still outside it, and the pressure to answer is higher because the relationship is warmer.

A request to change something in the file. It arrives innocently — a name spelled differently, a date the client remembers otherwise. The file is a record. Corrections are made visibly, dated as of today, with the original preserved. Section 6.5's line governs and does not soften with time.

The habit that makes all of this easy: a complete, dated, retrievable file, and a standing willingness to say "that one goes to your attorney" or "that one goes to your tax advisor" without treating it as a failure to help. A licensee who has said it a hundred times says it comfortably, and clients read the comfort as competence rather than as evasion.

6.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to sequence signing, funding, recording, and disbursement; explain why escrow stops rather than deciding when the parties disagree; and state the correct response to a missing document found in a closed file.

Key terms

TermDefinition
