EscrowA neutral third party holding funds and documents under conditional delivery, authorized to act only on the parties' mutual written instructions.
The two elementsA binding contract between the parties, and conditional delivery to a neutral holder. Without both, what exists is a stakeholder arrangement, not an escrow.
NeutralityThe structural feature letting both parties rely on the same holder. It is why escrow stops rather than deciding when the parties disagree.
Limited dual agentThe description of escrow that explains its shape: duties to both parties simultaneously, bounded strictly by the instructions.
Mutual written instructionThe only basis on which escrow may change disbursement, release funds, or act on a disputed matter. An agent's confirmation is not the other party's consent.
Affiliated business arrangementWhere the brokerage has a financial interest in a settlement provider. Carries disclosure and other conditions. Accepting anything of value for directing settlement business is separately prohibited.
Preliminary reportA report of record title stating the terms on which the insurer would issue a policy, including the exceptions it would take. Not a guarantee, not the policy, not a survey.
ExceptionAn item the policy will not insure against. The buyer bears that risk unless it is otherwise addressed.
ReconveyanceThe recorded document clearing a paid deed of trust. The seller's recollection that a loan was paid does not clear the record.
Mechanic's lienA lien for work performed whose priority may relate back to when work commenced. Routed to escrow and title, not evaluated by the licensee.
VestingHow the buyer takes title. Carries ownership, survivorship, tax, and estate consequences — and is a question for legal and tax counsel before escrow prepares the deed.
Owner's versus lender's policyThe owner's policy insures the buyer's interest; the lender's insures the lien and its priority. Different interests, which is why both commonly exist.
Parallel tracksContract deadlines, investigation, disclosure, financing, appraisal, title, escrow, and insurance — each with its own deadlines and failure modes.
Started lateWhy most failed transactions fail. Not that a track was impossible.
Final verification of conditionThe walkthrough: confirming agreed repairs are done, included items are present, and the property is not materially changed. Not an inspection and not a renegotiation.
SigningExecution of the closing documents. The transaction is not closed.
FundingThe lender's release of loan proceeds to escrow, subject to its conditions.
RecordingRecording of the deed and security instrument, establishing the interests of record and giving constructive notice. Generally when the buyer becomes owner of record.
DisbursementEscrow's release of funds, after recording is confirmed — deliberately, to prevent a gap in which money has moved but interests are not of record.
Recording orderThe sequence establishing lien priority. Out-of-sequence recording can place a lender behind an interest it was to precede. Not a clerical preference.
PossessionTransfers when the contract says it does — frequently the same day as recording and frequently not. Ownership and possession are separate questions.
Post-closing occupancyThe seller remaining after closing, under a written agreement addressing duration, payment, condition, insurance, utilities, deposit, and the remedy for failure to vacate.
Superseded versionsEarlier drafts kept in the file because the sequence of versions is what shows when a term changed and who agreed to it.
FalsificationCreating a document now and dating it to match the transaction, or removing records so an inconsistency does not show. Distinct from a documented gap, which is survivable.

Practice problems

Problem 1

The day before recording, the buyer emails escrow asking it to hold back $3,000 from the seller's proceeds for a repair the seller promised verbally. The seller knows nothing about it. The buyer's agent emails escrow confirming the request is legitimate.

What may escrow do?

Solution. Escrow stops. That is the whole answer, and each alternative fails for its own reason.

It may not act on the holdback: a change to disbursement requires mutual written instructions, and one party cannot redirect funds.

It may not simply disburse in full either — that also acts on one side's position once a genuine dispute has been raised.

And the agent's confirmation supplies nothing. An agent is not the other party.

The correct outcome: the parties resolve it in writing and the resolution reaches escrow as a mutual instruction. The agents' job is to get that done before the closing date, not to argue with escrow about its neutrality.

Problem 2

A preliminary report shows a ten-foot utility easement along the rear property line, recorded CC&Rs, an unreleased deed of trust the seller says was paid in 2019, and a judgment lien against a person with a similar name. The buyer plans to build a workshop at the rear of the lot. The agent tells him easements are routine, asks title to remove the judgment, and predicts the workshop will be fine.

Route each item correctly and identify the three wrong responses.

Solution. The easement: obtain and review the document — width, location, permitted use. A rear-line easement can prevent exactly the structure this buyer intends, which makes it the most consequential item on the report for him. The CC&Rs: obtain and review; use restrictions bind him regardless of zoning. The unreleased deed of trust: a recorded reconveyance or other clearing document must be obtained — the seller's recollection does not clear the record. The judgment lien: route to title for resolution; do not assume it is or is not the seller.

The three wrong responses, and the agent made all three: treating an item as routine because most properties have one; asking title to "remove" an exception; and predicting the practical effect of an easement on the buyer's plans, which is a legal question for counsel.

What an exception means, stated once: the policy will not insure against it, so the buyer bears that risk unless it is otherwise addressed. That is why exceptions are read rather than skimmed.

Problem 3

Two unmarried siblings buying together tell the licensee they want the survivor to take the whole property automatically, and each to be able to leave her share to her own children. They ask which vesting to use, and escrow needs the deed instruction tomorrow.

How should the licensee respond?

Solution. By naming the tension and referring it — not by choosing.

Those two goals point in different directions. Automatic survivorship and the ability to devise a share to one's own children are not both satisfied by a single form, and resolving that is estate planning, not form selection.

The licensee may explain that the choice matters and carries ownership, survivorship, tax, and estate consequences. The licensee may not advise which to choose. The referral goes to legal and tax counsel before escrow prepares the deed, because changing it afterwards is neither free nor simple — and escrow's deadline is a scheduling pressure, not a reason to answer a question outside the license.

Problem 4

At the final verification the buyer finds an agreed repair was never completed and a built-in microwave named in the contract has been removed. The listing agent suggests closing on schedule and sorting it out afterward; the buyer's agent suggests instructing escrow to hold back funds.

Assess both suggestions.

Solution. Both fail, and the walkthrough's purpose explains why.

It confirms that the property is in the condition the contract requires — agreed repairs completed against the amendment that agreed them, included items present, systems operating, no new damage including from the move-out, and vacancy where required. It is not an inspection and not a new chance to renegotiate condition already accepted — but it is not informational either.

Closing and pursuing afterward converts a solvable issue into a claim: the ordinary leverage to resolve it disappears at closing. Instructing escrow to hold back cannot work — escrow acts only on mutual instruction.

Correct: document the finding with photographs and dated notes, and address it through the contract before closing.

Problem 5

A licensee tells his client the transaction "closed" the afternoon the parties signed. Funding occurs the next morning; recording that afternoon; disbursement the following day. The client had already given notice on her apartment based on the signing date.

Sequence the four events and explain why recording precedes disbursement.

Solution. Signing — the parties execute the closing documents, and the transaction is not closed. Funding — the lender releases proceeds to escrow subject to its conditions. Recording — the deed and security instrument are recorded, establishing the interests of record and giving constructive notice; this is generally when the buyer becomes owner of record. Disbursement — escrow disburses once recording is confirmed.

Recording precedes disbursement deliberately: it prevents a gap in which money has moved but the interests are not of record.

Related, and not clerical: where multiple instruments record, the order establishes lien priority, and an out-of-sequence recording can place a new lender behind an interest it was meant to precede. That is why closing is sequenced rather than simultaneous.

The client-facing lesson: when recording is confirmed, tell the client in writing, with the date and what it means. Clients frequently do not know whether they own the property, and the licensee is who they will ask.

Problem 6

Two days before funding, the buyer receives an email in the existing escrow thread with updated wire instructions. The reply-to address differs by one character. He forwards it to his agent asking whether it looks right.

What should the agent do, and what should have happened earlier?

Solution. Treat the change as presumptively fraudulent until verified, and verify by calling escrow at a number obtained independently — from the engagement documents or the company's published listing, never from the email carrying the instructions. Tell the buyer in writing not to send funds until that call is made.

What should have happened earlier is the more important half: the written warning at the start of escrow that wire instructions will never change by email and that he must verify by voice before sending. A licensee who sends that at the start of escrow rather than the day funds move has done the single most valuable thing available to protect a client's money.

This is the highest-risk moment in the transaction, and the moment a client is least able to recover from an error.

Problem 7

The seller needs three extra days in the house after closing. Both agents agree it is too short to need paperwork. The seller stays eleven days, leaves damage in the garage, and the buyer discovers the seller still has app access to the interior cameras.

What was missing, and what else belongs on the list?

Solution. A written occupancy agreement, whose absence is exactly what produced all three problems. "They only need three days" is how a holdover with no agreed remedy begins — and the buyer now owns a property occupied by someone with no obligation he can enforce quickly.

The terms it should have carried: duration and end date; payment; condition on delivery; insurance and in whose name; utilities and maintenance; a deposit or holdback as security for damage or holdover; and the remedy for failure to vacate, which is the point at which this becomes expensive. A brief term does not reduce the need for terms.

The camera access belongs on the transfer-of-control list, alongside when keys transfer and from whom, garage and gate remotes, mailbox keys, association access devices, and changing codes on smart locks and alarms. A seller retaining app access to interior cameras after closing is a real problem and must be addressed explicitly rather than assumed.

Worth noting: early possession before closing is riskier still and generally to be discouraged — a buyer who occupies and then fails to close leaves the seller with an occupant and a property to re-market.

Problem 8

A post-closing audit finds a missing signed copy of an amendment, an inconsistency between the closing statement and an earlier figure, and only executed final documents — the drafts were discarded as clutter. A colleague suggests recreating the amendment with a matching date and deleting the superseded closing statement so nothing conflicts.

State the correct response and the line.

Solution. Obtain what legitimately exists — the missing signed copy may still be retrievable, which is why the audit happens while participants still respond to messages. Record what does not, plainly. Escalate to the broker. And address the process that produced the gap, not only the instance.

The colleague's suggestion is falsification. Creating a document now and dating it to match the transaction, or removing surrounding records so an inconsistency does not show, converts a compliance failure into conduct that ends a career. A documented gap is survivable.

Discarding the drafts was itself a mistake. The sequence of versions is frequently what shows when a term changed and who agreed to it. A file of executed finals can prove what was agreed but not how the parties arrived there — which is the question a dispute usually asks.

The same principle governs corrections generally: made visibly, with the original preserved and the change attributable. A record that can be silently altered is not a record. And the file is retained from closing — including cancelled and failed transactions, which are disproportionately the ones that later produce disputes.

Chapter summary

Escrow is neutral and acts only on mutual written instructions. It holds, it does not decide; where the parties disagree it stops, and no agent's confirmation substitutes for the other party's consent.

Never accept anything of value for directing settlement business, and disclose affiliated business arrangements where the brokerage has an interest.

Open escrow on the complete, fully executed agreement including amendments, or the instructions will not match the contract — which surfaces later, at the worst time.

A preliminary report states the terms on which a policy would issue. An exception is a risk the policy will not cover, so exceptions are read, the underlying documents obtained, and legal questions routed to counsel. Never call an item routine, never ask title to "remove" one, never predict its effect on the buyer's plans.

Vesting is a legal and tax question, referred before escrow prepares the deed.

Transactions fail because a track started late. Build the calendar from the contract, start the slow items first — insurance in hazard areas, association documents, permit research, specialist inspections — confirm status weekly in writing, escalate early, and reconcile escrow's instructions against the agreement.

The final verification confirms contract compliance, not condition generally — and problems are resolved before closing, while leverage still exists.

Signing, funding, recording, disbursement are four events in that order. Recording is generally when ownership transfers; disbursement follows it deliberately; and recording order establishes lien priority.

Warn about wire fraud in writing at the start of escrow, and verify any change through a channel the attacker does not control.

Possession transfers when the contract says, and any divergence from recording is documented — post-closing occupancy in full, early possession rarely and with counsel.

Audit the file shortly after closing, keep superseded versions, obtain what exists, record what does not, and fix the process. A documented gap is survivable; a manufactured document is not.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. Separate the title issue from the contract issue and state which party and which process resolves each, identify what escrow may and may not do on the facts given, state the documentation the resolution requires, identify what you would refer to counsel, and list the evidence you would retain. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Practice - Lesson Assignment 12

Property management and leasing practice

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Identify the terms a management agreement must establish and explain why unsigned authority is a structural defect.
  • Apply uniform written screening criteria and recognize where discretion admits bias.
  • Handle rents, security deposits, and owner funds as trust funds, and explain why internal ledgers do not cure commingling.
  • Apply habitability obligations and lawful entry requirements to maintenance and access.
  • Distinguish the notice requirements that govern lease enforcement and termination.
  • Produce owner reporting that satisfies the accounting duty rather than summarizing it.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: management agreements and tenant selection55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: trust funds, habitability, and access50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: enforcement, termination, and owner reporting45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Management agreement

1.1 Property management is licensed activity

Managing real property for another for compensation is licensed activity. It is performed for and under the employing broker, the funds handled are trust funds, and the records are the brokerage's records. Everything in this lesson follows from those three facts.

Property management also differs from sales in a way that shapes the risk: it is continuous. A sale ends. A tenancy runs for years, generating funds monthly, decisions weekly, and exposure continuously. A defect in a sales file is one transaction. A defect in a management practice repeats every month across every unit.

1.2 What the agreement establishes

TermWhat it governs
PartiesThe owner and the brokerage
PropertyExactly which units are managed
Term and terminationDuration, notice to terminate, what happens on termination
Scope of authorityWhat the manager may do without asking, and what requires owner approval
Spending authorityThe threshold above which owner approval is required, and emergency authority below it
CompensationManagement fee, leasing fee, renewal fee, and any other charges
Trust fundsWhere funds are held and how they are accounted for
ReportingWhat the owner receives and how often
Owner obligationsFunding reserves, maintaining insurance, providing disclosures and documents
Insurance and indemnityCoverage each party maintains
Fair housingThe parties' obligations, and the manager's authority to refuse unlawful instructions

1.3 Unsigned authority is a structural defect

A manager acting without a signed agreement is acting without established authority. That is not a paperwork gap:

  • The scope of authority is undefined, so every decision is arguable.
  • The compensation is unenforceable.
  • The handling of funds is not authorized in writing.
  • The owner can dispute any action taken.

An audit that finds two owners with no signed agreement has found a defect of the same order as commingling, not a filing oversight.

1.4 Spending authority and emergencies

The most practically important term is the threshold above which the manager must obtain owner approval, paired with emergency authority below or outside it.

Emergencies do not wait for approval. A burst pipe, a failed heater in cold weather, a security failure — these require action, and the agreement should say so, define what counts, and require prompt notice to the owner afterward.

A manager without written emergency authority faces a genuine dilemma: act and be second-guessed, or wait and let the damage compound while a habitability obligation runs. Settling it in the agreement removes the dilemma.

1.5 Owner disclosures the manager needs

Before managing, the manager should obtain from the owner: known property conditions and repair history; permit status of improvements; existing leases and their terms; existing deposits held and their amounts; association documents where applicable; insurance details; and any pending disputes or claims.

Deposits held by the owner from an existing tenancy deserve particular attention. Where deposits transfer to the manager, they are trust funds from the moment received, and the amounts must reconcile to the leases.

1.6 The management agreement's hardest terms

Section 1.2 lists what the agreement establishes. Four of its terms produce nearly all management disputes, and each is worth negotiating deliberately rather than accepting from a template.

Scope of authority over money. Section 1.4 sets spending authority; the harder question is what the manager may do when the authority is insufficient and the owner cannot be reached. An emergency provision with no dollar figure and no definition of emergency is a provision that will be argued about, and it will be argued about after the manager has already spent the money.

Who decides on a tenant. The agreement should say whether the manager approves applicants against the written criteria or forwards them to the owner. The second arrangement is where fair housing exposure concentrates, because an owner reviewing applicants is an owner making decisions on information the criteria do not include. Where an owner insists on approving, the criteria still govern, the manager still applies them first, and the manager does not transmit an owner's improper reason to an applicant.

Term, termination, and what happens to the tenancies. A management agreement ending does not end the leases. The transfer of trust funds, deposits, records, and keys on termination is a term, and its absence is why manager transitions produce deposit disputes years later.

What the manager is not responsible for. Habitability obligations run to the owner, and an agreement cannot make the manager the party who does not perform them. What an agreement can do is state who does what, and clarity here is protective for both sides. An agreement silent on who obtains required inspections, who maintains required systems, and who holds the compliance obligations produces a gap that shows up during an enforcement matter.

A term worth adding that most agreements lack: what happens when the owner instructs the manager to do something the manager may not lawfully do. Naming it in advance — the manager declines, states why in writing, and the instruction does not proceed — converts a future confrontation into a term both parties already accepted.

And one practical requirement. The manager cannot perform an agreement he cannot find. A signed original, in the file, with every exhibit — the criteria, the fee schedule, the authority limits — is the baseline, and Section 1.3's point about unsigned authority is the reason.

2. Tenant selection and fair housing

2.1 Written criteria applied uniformly

The defensible practice, and the only one that survives review:

  1. Written criteria, established before applications are taken.
  2. Objective standards — income relative to rent, credit history, rental history, verifiable identity, lawful occupancy standards.
  3. Applied identically to every applicant.
  4. Documented — how each application was evaluated against each criterion.
  5. Disclosed to applicants before they apply, along with any application fee.

Uniformity is not merely fair; it is what makes a decision explainable afterward in neutral terms.

2.2 Where bias operates

PracticeProblem
"I get a feel for people at the showing"Impression-based selection is where bias operates and cannot be defended
Choosing whoever the current tenants would get along withCompatibility judgments reproduce the existing composition
Preferring applicants referred by existing tenantsSame effect, at scale
Applying criteria strictly to some applicants and flexibly to othersDifferential application is discrimination even with neutral criteria
Discouraging an application rather than denying itSteering, and harder to detect, which is why it is dangerous

That last one deserves attention. Telling an applicant "you probably wouldn't qualify" or "the owner is looking for someone long-term" — instead of taking the application and evaluating it — is discouragement, and it produces no record of a denial while achieving one.

2.3 Occupancy standards

Occupancy limits must be reasonable and applied uniformly. A limit set so restrictively that it excludes families with children functions as familial status discrimination however it is phrased.

2.4 Source of income

California protects source of income. An owner instruction to refuse applicants using housing assistance is an unlawful instruction, and the manager's obligations are those covered in Lesson 03: refuse it, explain why, escalate to the broker, and address whether the engagement can continue.

2.5 Assistance animals

For fair housing purposes, an assistance animal is not a pet. Three consequences:

  1. A no-pet policy does not by itself justify denial.
  2. No pet deposit or pet rent may be charged for an assistance animal.
  3. The request cannot be refused merely because the animal lacks "certification."

The provider may still evaluate reasonableness, and the resident remains responsible for damage and for the animal's behavior.

2.6 The worked case

An owner instructs: "No housing assistance, no service animals because of my no-pet rule, and I'd prefer a quiet single person over a family with kids. Also that unit has two steps at the entry so it won't work for the applicant in a wheelchair anyway."

Every clause is a problem. Source of income and familial status are protected in California. A service animal is not a pet. And deciding on the applicant's behalf that a unit will not work for him is not the manager's decision — accessibility is addressed through accommodation and modification, not exclusion.

The response: refuse the instructions, explain each, evaluate the applicant on the same written criteria as everyone else, treat the entry steps as a possible modification question, and escalate to the broker on whether the brokerage can act for this owner.

What does not work: applying only the "quiet tenant" preference by recasting familial status as a noise concern; documenting that the instructions came from the owner and following them anyway; or declining the engagement quietly without explaining the problem, which leaves the owner to repeat it with the next manager.

2.7 Advertising

Rental advertising is subject to the same rules covered in Lesson 06: describe the property, not the occupants. "Ideal for professionals, no students," "quiet building, adults preferred," and "perfect for a single person" all sort people. And the photographs must depict the actual unit — advertising a vacancy with images of a nicer unit in the building misrepresents what is offered, however similar the floor plans.

2.8 The application process, start to finish

Fair housing exposure in leasing is concentrated in a process that takes a week, and most of it is decided before any applicant applies.

Before the property is advertised:

  1. Written criteria exist — income standard, credit standard, rental history, and whatever else the owner lawfully requires — with each element defined so that two people applying it reach the same result.
  2. The criteria have been reviewed against what California prohibits, which Section 2.4 notes is broader than the federal baseline, and against how each element operates in practice rather than only in intent.
  3. The criteria are published or available to applicants, so an applicant knows what he is being measured against.

When inquiries arrive:

  1. Every inquiry gets the same information — availability, price, terms, criteria, and the application process. The most common violation in leasing is not a denial. It is different information given to different callers, and it leaves almost no trace.
  2. Every inquiry is logged — date, contact, property, what was said. This is the record that answers a testing complaint, and there is no substitute for it.

When applications arrive:

  1. Applications are processed in a defined order, and the order is documented. First-come-first-served is a rule; "whoever seems easiest" is not.
  2. The same verification steps run for every applicant. Additional scrutiny applied to some applicants is the pattern Lesson 14 of the mortgage course calls selective scrutiny, and it produces a violation even where every decision was defensible.
  3. Accommodation requests are handled on their own track — Section 2.5 and Section 4.8 of Lesson 06 — and never as a reason the application slows down or stops.

When a decision is made:

  1. The decision states the criterion it rests on, recorded at the time.
  2. Adverse action requirements are met where a consumer report contributed, including the disclosures the framework requires.
  3. The file is retained — approved and denied alike. Denied applications are the ones an examination is interested in, for the same reason denied loan files are.

The test of the whole process: hand the file to someone who was not there and ask them to explain why each applicant was approved or denied. If the answer requires knowing what the manager thought, the process failed — not necessarily unlawfully, but in the only way that can be proved either direction.

3. Rents, deposits, and accounting

3.1 What is a trust fund

Money received in the course of licensed activity that belongs to someone else is a trust fund. In property management that includes rents collected for the owner, security deposits, owner-provided reserves, and application fees held pending processing.

3.2 The three obligations

  1. Separation. Trust funds go into a trust account or neutral depository, kept separate from the brokerage's own funds.
  2. Records. A complete accounting of every receipt and disbursement, identifying whose funds they are.
  3. Timeliness. Funds are placed as required, without being held personally in the interim.

3.3 Why internal ledgers do not cure commingling

The most common defense a manager offers is that funds in the operating account are tracked separately in the accounting system. It does not work, for a structural reason worth teaching:

Funds in a brokerage operating account are exposed to the brokerage's creditors, to its operating losses, and to ordinary business risk. A ledger entry does not protect the money. Separation is what protects it, and the record is what proves the separation was maintained.

Commingling is complete when the funds are deposited in the wrong account, regardless of how carefully the total is tracked and regardless of whether anyone loses money.

3.4 Shortages and overages

FindingWhat it meansCorrect response
ShortageSomeone else's money is missingCure immediately from the broker's own funds; document the cause; reconcile
OverageThe records do not accurately reflect whose funds are heldInvestigate and document the source; the broker may not simply keep it

Neither is a rounding matter. There is no tolerance threshold, and neither may be deferred to the next reconciliation.

A manager discovers he deposited a tenant's rent into the operating account by mistake. The correct response is to correct it immediately, document the error and the correction, and notify the broker so the trust records reconcile and the cause is addressed. Correcting it quietly without a record is its own problem: trust accounting is judged by the record as much as by the balance.

3.5 Security deposits

Deposits are the tenant's funds held for defined purposes, and California regulates their handling and their return.

Practical obligations:

  • Hold them as trust funds, separate from the brokerage's funds. Forwarding them to the owner is governed by the agreement and the law, not by convenience.
  • Document condition at move-in, thoroughly, with dated photographs. A deposit dispute is decided on the difference between move-in and move-out condition, and a manager without move-in documentation has no case.
  • Distinguish damage from ordinary wear and tear. Wear and tear is not deductible.
  • Account in writing on termination, itemizing deductions with supporting documentation, within the required time.
  • Return the balance within the required period.

Deposit accounting is the single most common source of tenant complaints, and nearly all of it is prevented by move-in documentation and an itemized written accounting.

3.6 Reconciliation

The trust account is reconciled on a regular cycle, and the reconciliation compares three things: the bank balance, the book balance, and the total of the individual beneficiary ledgers. All three must agree.

A reconciliation that balances the bank against the book but never against the beneficiary ledgers will not detect the error that matters most — money in the account belonging to a different beneficiary than the records say.

3.7 A worked trust account month

A manager holds funds for nine properties in one trust account. During March:

  • Rent arrives for eight of nine properties; one tenant pays partially.
  • Two owners are due disbursements; one has asked that his be held for an upcoming repair.
  • A plumber is paid from property four's funds.
  • A tenant at property seven moves out; the deposit must be accounted for.
  • The manager's monthly fees are earned across all nine.
  • A tenant at property two pays with a check that is returned unpaid after the manager has already disbursed to that owner.

What each event requires.

The partial rent is recorded against that property's ledger for the amount actually received. It is not rounded, netted, or recorded as full with a note. Section 3.3 explains why the property-level ledger is what makes the account auditable.

The held disbursement stays in trust and stays on the owner's ledger. It is the owner's money being held on his instruction, which is a different thing from money the manager has not gotten around to sending, and the instruction should be in writing.

The plumber's payment comes from property four's funds and is recorded against property four. A payment for one property made from another property's funds is a use of one owner's money for another's benefit, and it is a violation regardless of whether the total balance was sufficient.

The move-out deposit is accounted for on the terms the deposit framework requires, within the required period, with the itemization the framework prescribes. Section 3.5 governs. The deposit was never the owner's money and it is not now the manager's, and a deduction is documented by what was actually spent or reasonably estimated on stated terms.

The manager's fees are earned when the agreement says they are earned, and they are removed from the trust account rather than left in it. Section 3.2's second obligation cuts both ways: broker funds do not stay in trust any more than trust funds go into the operating account.

The returned check is the month's real problem. The manager disbursed money against funds that did not exist. The account is now short, and Section 3.4 governs: the shortage is corrected by the broker, immediately, from broker funds, and documented — it is not carried until the tenant makes good, and it is not covered by the balances of the other eight properties, which is exactly what "the account still has enough in it" means and exactly why that reasoning is wrong.

The reconciliation at month end compares the bank balance to the sum of the individual ledgers, and Section 3.6 requires that both sides be examined. A bank-to-book reconciliation alone would show this account balancing, because the total is right; the property-level comparison is what reveals that property two's ledger is negative and property four paid property four's plumber with property four's money only because the total happened to cover it.

What the file shows afterward: every receipt, every disbursement, the corrected shortage with its date and source, the owner's written hold instruction, the deposit accounting with its itemization, and a three-way reconciliation signed by the person who performed it.

4. Maintenance, habitability, and access

4.1 The habitability obligation

A residential landlord must maintain the property in a habitable condition. The obligation covers the elements that make a dwelling fit to live in — weather protection, plumbing, hot and cold water, heating, electrical, sanitary facilities, and structural integrity, among others.

Two features shape how a manager must respond:

  1. It is not waivable by agreement. A lease clause purporting to relieve the landlord does not.
  2. It runs continuously, not only at move-in.

4.2 Triage

Not every repair request is equal, and a manager who treats them uniformly will respond too slowly to the ones that matter.

CategoryExamplesResponse
EmergencyNo water, no heat in cold weather, sewage backup, gas odor, electrical hazard, security failure, floodingImmediate; emergency authority applies
HabitabilityFailed plumbing, non-functioning heat, pest infestation, mold, roof leakPrompt, documented
OrdinaryAppliance malfunction, cosmetic damage, minor fixturesScheduled within a reasonable period
Cosmetic or improvementRepainting by preference, upgradesOwner decision

A loss of water is not routine maintenance and does not wait for the next scheduled visit. Requiring a written request before responding, or referring the tenant to the owner, may be part of the process but neither justifies delaying the response.

4.3 Documentation

Every request and every response is recorded: when the request was received, its nature, when the manager responded, what was done, by whom, and what it cost. In a later dispute — with the tenant, with the owner, or with an insurer — that record is the entire case.

4.4 Lawful entry

A tenant has a possessory right, and entry requires:

  1. A permitted purpose — necessary repairs, agreed services, showing the unit, inspection where permitted, or emergency.
  2. Proper notice, in the manner and within the time the law requires.
  3. A reasonable time of day.
  4. Documentation that notice was given.

What does not substitute for notice: the tenant being home, the owner's authorization, the manager holding a key, or the convenience of the contractor's schedule.

4.5 Showing an occupied unit

Where a unit is shown while occupied — for re-rental or for sale — the entry rules apply to each showing. The practical approach is to agree a protocol with the tenant in advance, honor it, and document the notices.

A tenant who is cooperated with is far easier to show around than one whose rights are treated as an obstacle, and a manager who treats notice as optional will eventually meet a tenant who does not.

4.6 Habitability and the tenant's remedies

Where a habitability obligation goes unmet, a tenant may have remedies. A manager's practical concern is that the exposure grows with delay, and that a documented prompt response is the defense.

Where a tenant asserts a remedy, the matter should be escalated to the broker and, where appropriate, to counsel. It is not a matter for the manager to resolve by argument with the tenant.

4.7 A worked maintenance week

Monday. A tenant reports no hot water. Triage places this high — Section 4.2 — because hot water is a habitability item rather than a convenience. The manager dispatches the same day, records the report time, the dispatch time, and the vendor.

Monday, later. A different tenant reports a dripping faucet. Logged, scheduled, and communicated with a date. The fact that it is minor does not mean it goes unanswered — an unanswered minor request is how a tenant learns that reports do not produce responses, which is how the next urgent report arrives late.

Tuesday. The hot water vendor reports the unit is failed and replacement exceeds the manager's spending authority. Section 1.4 governs. The manager reaches the owner, documents the contact and the authorization, and proceeds. Where the owner cannot be reached and the condition is a habitability item, the emergency provision is what the manager relies on — and this is why Section 1.6 argues for defining it in advance.

Wednesday. The owner instructs the manager to install a used unit a friend is providing rather than the quoted replacement. The manager confirms in writing what was instructed, states what the requirement is for the work and the permit, and routes the question rather than deciding it. An owner's cost preference does not override the standard the work must meet.

Thursday. A tenant at another property reports a broken window she says was broken by a neighbor's child. Repair first, allocate later. The security of the unit is a present condition; who pays is a separate question resolved afterward on the lease's terms and the facts.

Thursday, later. The manager needs to enter a unit to inspect a reported leak. Section 4.4 governs, and the notice requirements apply to an inspection just as they apply to a repair. A tenant's earlier report is not a standing invitation to enter, and entering without the required notice converts a maintenance visit into a violation.

Friday. A tenant who reported the hot water on Monday and has now had it fixed asks whether her rent will be reduced for the four days. The manager does not answer this. Whether any adjustment is owed is a legal question about remedies, and it goes to the broker and, where appropriate, counsel. What the manager does is give the owner the facts and the dates, which are exactly the facts the question will be decided on.

What the week produced in the file. Every report with its date and time. Every dispatch with its vendor. Every authorization with who gave it. Every entry with its notice. Every invoice against the property it belongs to. Section 4.3's documentation requirement is not a separate task performed at the end of the week — it is the week, written down as it happened.

The failure this prevents. Six months later a tenant alleges the hot water was out for three weeks and the manager entered her unit twice without notice. The log answers both allegations in thirty seconds, or it answers neither.

5. Lease enforcement and termination

5.1 Notice is where enforcement succeeds or fails

Termination and enforcement procedures are formal and unforgiving. A good reason delivered by a defective notice fails, and the landlord starts over — having lost weeks and given the tenant a stronger position.

The elements that must be right: the type of notice; its content; the period it gives; who is named; how it is served; and the proof of service. Each has requirements, and each is a common failure point.

5.2 What matters most, in order

  1. The correct notice type for the circumstance.
  2. Correct content, stating what the law requires it to state.
  3. Correct period, calculated correctly.
  4. Correct service method, with proof.
  5. Correct parties named.

A manager who documents the tenant's conduct impeccably and then serves the wrong notice has accomplished nothing. Documentation matters — it just does not cure a defective notice.

5.3 Retaliation and its appearance

Enforcement action taken close in time to a tenant's complaint, repair request, or assertion of a right can look retaliatory, and California protects tenants against retaliation.

The manager's protection is a documented, independent basis for the action that predates or is unconnected to the tenant's protected activity. This is another reason the maintenance log matters: it establishes what happened and when.

5.4 Self-help is prohibited

Changing locks, removing doors, shutting off utilities, removing a tenant's belongings, or otherwise forcing a tenant out without process is unlawful and carries serious consequences.

There is no version of this that becomes acceptable because the tenant is badly behaved, has not paid, or has abandoned the unit in the manager's judgment. Where a unit appears abandoned, there is a legal process for that too.

5.5 Escalation

Termination and eviction are legal proceedings. The manager's role is to maintain the record, follow the process, and involve counsel — not to advise the owner on the strength of the case or predict the outcome.

An owner asking "can we just get them out by the first?" is asking a legal question, and the honest answer describes the process and its timeline rather than promising a date.

5.6 Move-out

At the end of a tenancy:

  • Document condition as thoroughly as at move-in, dated, with photographs.
  • Compare against move-in documentation, which is why move-in documentation exists.
  • Distinguish damage from ordinary wear and tear.
  • Itemize deductions in writing with supporting documentation.
  • Return the balance within the required period.
  • Retain the record, including the photographs.

6. Owner reporting and risk controls

6.1 Reporting discharges the accounting duty

The manager holds and handles the owner's money. Reporting is how the accounting duty is discharged, which is why it must be regular and itemized rather than summary.

An accounting that cannot be verified is not an accounting. Owner preference for more or less detail does not change the obligation.

6.2 What the owner receives

ItemFrequency
Rent collected, by unit, with datesMonthly
Delinquencies and their statusMonthly
Disbursements, itemized, with supporting documentationMonthly
Management and leasing fees chargedMonthly
Reserve balance and trust funds heldMonthly
Security deposits held, by tenantMonthly, and on any change
Maintenance activity, including requests and responsesMonthly
Vacancy and leasing activityMonthly
Annual summary and tax reporting informationAnnually

6.3 An owner who has not been reported to for four months

That is not an administrative lapse. The owner has been unable to verify the handling of their own funds for a third of a year, the trust records have gone that long without external scrutiny, and if there is an error in them nobody has been in a position to find it.

An audit finding of that kind sits alongside commingling and unsigned agreements as a structural failure, not a filing backlog.

6.4 Risk controls that pay for themselves

  1. Move-in and move-out documentation, dated, photographic, complete. Prevents most deposit disputes outright.
  2. A maintenance log capturing request, response, action, and cost. Answers habitability and retaliation questions.
  3. Written screening criteria applied uniformly, with documented evaluations. Answers fair housing questions.
  4. Monthly trust reconciliation against beneficiary ledgers, not only bank against book.
  5. Written notices with proof of service, retained.
  6. Insurance verification — the owner's, and where required the tenant's.
  7. Vendor management — licensing, insurance, and workmanship standards for contractors sent to the property.

6.5 The audit view

A review of a management practice finds: security deposits in the operating account; one owner unreported for four months; two management agreements never signed; and a salesperson signing leases in her own name. The broker says nothing has gone wrong and no one has complained.

Each is an independent defect and none depends on harm having occurred. Deposits are trust funds that may not be commingled. Owners are owed an accounting. Authority must rest on a signed agreement. And a salesperson may not contract in her own name for licensed activity — the lease is the brokerage's, executed for the broker.

The absence of complaints is not evidence of compliance. It usually means the problems have not surfaced yet, and in a management practice — which repeats monthly across every unit — they will.

6.6 The owner who wants something the manager cannot do

Managers receive improper instructions more often than sales licensees do, because owners think of the property as theirs and of the manager as staff. The instructions are usually not malicious. They are the owner solving a problem the only way he has thought of.

The instructionWhy it cannot be followed
"Don't rent to [category]."Unlawful, whatever the phrasing, including where the owner offers a business reason. Lesson 03's obedience limit governs and the answer is refusal plus escalation.
"Just change the locks, he hasn't paid in two months."Section 5.4. Self-help is prohibited, and the manager who performs it has done the unlawful act himself.
"Shut off the water until she pays."The same category, and it compounds a habitability failure onto an unlawful eviction attempt.
"Don't give the deposit back, he was a bad tenant."The deposit is governed by a framework with a procedure and a timeline. "Bad tenant" is not a deduction category.
"Use this month's rent from the other building to cover my repair."One owner's funds for another's benefit. Section 3.7's plumber, in a more explicit form.
"Skip the inspection this year."Where an inspection is required, it is required. The manager does not certify or record that something happened when it did not.
"Tell the tenant the landlord is a management company" when the owner wants to conceal his identityDisclosure obligations about who the owner is exist for a reason, and misstating it is a misrepresentation.
"Raise her rent, she keeps complaining."Section 5.3. A rent increase following complaints has the appearance of retaliation regardless of the owner's actual reason, and the appearance is itself the problem.

The response is the same in every row, and it has four parts:

  1. Decline clearly. Not "let me look into it" when the answer is already known.
  2. Say why, in terms of what the manager may lawfully do rather than in terms of the owner's motives. The owner is far more likely to accept a limit on the manager than an accusation about himself.
  3. Offer the lawful path where one exists — the notice procedure instead of the lockout, the deduction process instead of withholding the deposit, the documented business reason instead of the retaliatory-looking increase.
  4. Write it down the same day, and send the confirmation to the owner. The written record is what protects the manager if the owner does it himself, and it is what the broker needs.

And the fifth step, when the owner insists: this goes to the broker. A manager who continues managing a property for an owner who has instructed him to break the law, without escalating, has made the brokerage's problem into his own. Whether the engagement can continue is a brokerage decision, and Section 1.6's suggested term is what makes the conversation easier when it comes.

6.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to explain why an internal ledger does not cure commingling, name the three balances a trust reconciliation must agree, and state why a defective notice defeats a well-documented enforcement action.

Key terms

TermDefinition
Continuity of exposureWhat distinguishes management from sales: a defect in a sales file is one transaction; a defect in a management practice repeats every month across every unit.
Management agreementThe contract between the owner and the brokerage establishing property, term, scope, spending authority, compensation, trust fund handling, reporting, owner obligations, insurance, and fair housing obligations.
Unsigned authorityA structural defect, not a filing gap. Scope is undefined, compensation is unenforceable, fund handling is unauthorized, and every action is disputable.
Spending authorityThe threshold above which owner approval is required, paired with emergency authority below or outside it.
Emergency authorityWritten authority to act without approval where waiting compounds damage while a habitability obligation runs. Its absence creates a genuine dilemma; the agreement removes it.
Written screening criteriaObjective standards established before applications are taken, disclosed to applicants, applied identically, and documented against each criterion.
Differential applicationApplying neutral criteria strictly to some applicants and flexibly to others. Discrimination, even though the criteria are neutral.
DiscouragementTelling an applicant they probably would not qualify instead of taking and evaluating the application. Achieves a denial while producing no record of one.
Occupancy standardsLimits that must be reasonable and uniform. A limit restrictive enough to exclude families with children functions as familial status discrimination however phrased.
Source of incomeA characteristic California protects. An instruction to refuse applicants using housing assistance is an unlawful instruction.
Trust fundMoney received in licensed activity belonging to someone else — rents, security deposits, owner reserves, application fees held pending processing.
The three obligationsSeparation into a trust account or neutral depository; complete records identifying whose funds they are; and timely placement.
ComminglingDepositing trust funds into the wrong account. Complete on deposit, regardless of how carefully the total is tracked and regardless of whether anyone loses money.
Why a ledger does not cure itFunds in an operating account are exposed to the brokerage's creditors, losses, and business risk. A ledger entry does not protect money; separation does, and the record proves it was maintained.
ShortageSomeone else's money missing. Cured immediately from the broker's own funds, with the cause documented. No tolerance threshold.
OverageRecords that do not accurately reflect whose funds are held. Investigated and documented; the broker may not simply keep it.
Move-in documentationDated, photographic, complete condition record. A deposit dispute turns on the difference between move-in and move-out condition, so a manager without it has no case.
Ordinary wear and tearNot deductible from a security deposit. Distinguished from damage in the itemized written accounting.
Three-way reconciliationBank balance, book balance, and the total of individual beneficiary ledgers — all three must agree. Bank-to-book alone misses the error that matters most.
HabitabilityThe obligation to maintain the property fit to live in. Not waivable by agreement, and running continuously rather than only at move-in.
TriageSorting requests into emergency, habitability, ordinary, and cosmetic. A manager who treats them uniformly responds too slowly to the ones that matter.
Lawful entryA permitted purpose, proper notice in the required manner and time, a reasonable hour, and documentation. Not substituted for by the tenant being home, the owner's authorization, holding a key, or a contractor's schedule.
RetaliationEnforcement action close in time to a tenant's complaint or assertion of a right. The defense is a documented, independent basis predating or unconnected to the protected activity.
Self-helpChanging locks, removing doors, shutting off utilities, or removing belongings to force a tenant out. Unlawful, with no exception for a badly behaved or non-paying tenant.
Defective noticeA notice wrong in type, content, period, service, or parties. A good reason delivered by a defective notice fails, and documentation does not cure it.

Practice problems

Problem 1

An audit of a management practice finds two owners with no signed management agreement. The broker calls it a filing oversight — the properties have been managed for three years without complaint, fees have been collected, and the owners are happy.

Assess.

Solution. It is a defect of the same order as commingling, not a filing oversight.

Acting without a signed agreement means acting without established authority: the scope is undefined so every decision is arguable, the compensation is unenforceable, the handling of funds is not authorized in writing, and the owner can dispute any action taken over three years.

The absence of complaints is not evidence of compliance. In a management practice — which repeats monthly across every unit — it usually means the problems have not surfaced yet.

What transfers: the same reasoning applies to the neighboring findings in a typical audit — deposits in the operating account, an owner unreported for months, a salesperson signing leases in her own name. Each is an independent defect and none depends on harm having occurred.

Problem 2

An owner instructs: "No housing assistance, no service animals because of my no-pet rule, I'd prefer a quiet single person over a family with kids, and that unit has two steps at the entry so it won't work for the applicant in a wheelchair anyway."

Address each clause and identify three responses that do not work.

Solution. Every clause is a problem. Source of income and familial status are protected in California. A service animal is not a pet — a no-pet policy does not by itself justify denial, no pet deposit or pet rent may be charged, and certification may not be required. And deciding on the applicant's behalf that a unit will not work for him is not the manager's decision; accessibility is addressed through accommodation and modification, not exclusion — here the entry steps are a possible modification question.

The response: refuse the instructions, explain each, evaluate the applicant on the same written criteria as everyone else, treat the steps as a modification question, and escalate to the broker on whether the brokerage can act for this owner.

Three that do not work: applying only the "quiet tenant" preference by recasting familial status as a noise concern; documenting that the instructions came from the owner and following them anyway; and declining the engagement quietly without explaining the problem, which leaves the owner free to repeat it with the next manager.

Problem 3

A manager says she selects tenants by getting a feel for people at the showing, favors applicants referred by existing tenants, and tells applicants she doubts will qualify that the owner is "looking for someone long-term" rather than taking their applications.

Identify the problem in each, and state the defensible practice.

Solution. Impression-based selection is exactly where bias operates and cannot be explained afterwards in neutral terms. Referral preference reproduces the existing composition of the building at scale — the same effect as choosing whoever the current tenants would get along with. And the third is the most dangerous: discouraging an application rather than denying it achieves a denial while producing no record of one, which is why it is harder to detect.

The defensible practice: written criteria established before applications are taken; objective standards — income relative to rent, credit history, rental history, verifiable identity, lawful occupancy standards; applied identically to every applicant; documented as to how each application was evaluated against each criterion; and disclosed to applicants before they apply, along with any application fee.

Uniformity is not merely fair. It is what makes a decision explainable afterwards.

Problem 4

A manager deposits a month's rents and three security deposits into the brokerage operating account. He points out that his accounting software tracks each beneficiary separately to the cent, that the total is correct, and that no one has lost a dollar.

Why does this fail, and what is the correct response?

Solution. Commingling was complete on deposit. It does not depend on the total being wrong or on anyone losing money.

The structural reason the ledger does not cure it: funds in a brokerage operating account are exposed to the brokerage's creditors, operating losses, and ordinary business risk. A ledger entry does not protect the money. Separation protects it, and the record proves the separation was maintained.

The correct response to the mistake: correct it immediately, document the error and the correction, and notify the broker so the trust records reconcile and the cause is addressed. Correcting it quietly without a record is its own problem — trust accounting is judged by the record as much as by the balance.

Related: a shortage is cured immediately from the broker's own funds with the cause documented; an overage is investigated and documented and may not simply be kept. Neither is a rounding matter, neither has a tolerance threshold, and neither waits for the next reconciliation.

Problem 5

A monthly reconciliation shows the bank balance agreeing with the book balance to the cent. The manager reports the trust account as clean. Two months later a tenant's deposit turns out to have been credited to a different tenant's ledger.

What did the reconciliation miss, and why?

Solution. The third balance. A trust reconciliation compares the bank balance, the book balance, and the total of the individual beneficiary ledgers — and all three must agree.

A reconciliation that balances bank against book alone cannot detect the error that matters most: money in the account belonging to a different beneficiary than the records say. The total was right the whole time, which is exactly why it went unnoticed for two months.

What transfers: the same shape as the commingling defense in Problem 4. A correct total is not evidence that the right people's money is being held correctly.

Problem 6

A tenant reports on Friday morning that the unit has had no water since Thursday night. The manager tells him to submit a written request, that the plumber's next routine visit is Tuesday, and that he should contact the owner directly. Separately, the manager lets the plumber in on Tuesday without notice because the tenant is home.

Assess the response and the entry.

Solution. A loss of water is an emergency, not routine maintenance, and it sits at the top of the triage list alongside no heat in cold weather, sewage backup, gas odor, electrical hazard, security failure, and flooding. Emergency authority exists precisely so this does not wait for approval.

The habitability obligation is not waivable and runs continuously rather than only at move-in. Requiring a written request may be part of the process and referring to the owner may be part of the structure, but neither justifies delaying the response — and the exposure grows with the delay.

The entry is separately unlawful. Entry requires a permitted purpose, proper notice in the manner and time the law requires, a reasonable hour, and documentation that notice was given. The tenant being home does not substitute for notice — nor does the owner's authorization, the manager holding a key, or the convenience of the contractor's schedule.

The record is the case. Every request and response — when received, its nature, when answered, what was done, by whom, and at what cost — is what answers a later dispute with the tenant, the owner, or an insurer.

Problem 7

A tenant files a complaint with the city about a persistent roof leak. Eleven days later the manager serves a termination notice. The stated reason is a lease violation the manager has been documenting for four months. The notice names only one of the two tenants on the lease and is served by leaving it under the door. The owner then asks whether they can "just change the locks if she won't leave."

Assess the retaliation exposure, the notice, and the owner's question.

Solution. The timing creates a retaliation appearance, and California protects tenants against retaliation. The manager's protection here is real but must be shown: a documented, independent basis predating or unconnected to the protected activity — which is what four months of contemporaneous records provide. This is another reason the maintenance and enforcement logs matter.

The notice is defective on at least two elements — correct parties named, and correct service method with proof. Termination procedures are formal and unforgiving: type, content, period, parties, service, and proof of service each have requirements, and a good reason delivered by a defective notice fails. The landlord starts over, having lost weeks and given the tenant a stronger position. Impeccable documentation of the conduct does not cure it.

The owner's question is answered flatly: no. Changing locks, removing doors, shutting off utilities, or removing belongings is unlawful self-help, with no exception for a badly behaved or non-paying tenant — and where a unit appears abandoned, there is a legal process for that too.

"Can we get them out by the first?" is a legal question. The honest answer describes the process and its timeline rather than promising a date, and the matter goes to counsel. The manager maintains the record and follows the process; the manager does not assess the strength of the case.

Problem 8

A tenancy ends. The manager withholds the full deposit for carpet replacement and repainting, sending the tenant a one-line note four weeks later. There is no move-in condition record. Separately, one owner has received no report for four months.

Assess both.

Solution. The deposit handling fails on every element. A deposit dispute is decided on the difference between move-in and move-out condition, and a manager without move-in documentation has no case — which is the whole reason move-in documentation exists. Ordinary wear and tear is not deductible, and carpet wear and repainting after a tenancy are the standard examples. The termination accounting must be itemized in writing with supporting documentation and delivered within the required time, with the balance returned within the required period. A one-line note four weeks later is none of that.

Nearly all deposit complaints — the single most common source of tenant complaints — are prevented by two practices: dated photographic documentation at move-in and move-out, and an itemized written accounting.

The unreported owner is not an administrative lapse. The owner has been unable to verify the handling of their own money for a third of a year, the trust records have gone that long without external scrutiny, and if there is an error nobody has been positioned to find it. Reporting is how the accounting duty is discharged — regular and itemized, not summary — and an accounting that cannot be verified is not an accounting. Owner preference for less detail does not change the obligation.

Chapter summary

Management is licensed activity performed for the broker, with trust funds and brokerage records — and unlike a sale, it is continuous, so a defective practice repeats monthly across every unit.

The agreement is with the brokerage, and unsigned authority is a structural defect: undefined scope, unenforceable compensation, unauthorized fund handling, and disputable actions.

Settle spending and emergency authority in writing, so no manager has to choose between acting without authority and letting damage compound.

Written criteria, objective standards, uniform application, documented evaluation, disclosed in advance. Impression, compatibility, referral preference, differential strictness, and discouragement are each how bias enters — and the last leaves no record of the denial it accomplishes.

Source of income and familial status are protected in California; assistance animals are not pets; and accessibility is addressed by accommodation and modification, never by exclusion.

Trust funds require separation, records, and timeliness. A ledger does not cure commingling, because a ledger entry does not shield money from the brokerage's creditors and losses — separation does.

Reconcile three balances, not two. Bank against book alone misses money held for the wrong beneficiary.

Move-in documentation is what wins deposit disputes, wear and tear is not deductible, and the termination accounting is itemized, supported, and timely.

Habitability is not waivable and runs continuously. Triage requests, respond promptly to emergencies, and record request, response, action, and cost — that record is the entire case later.

Entry requires purpose, notice, a reasonable hour, and documentation. Nothing about the tenant being home, the owner's say-so, or a contractor's schedule substitutes.

A defective notice defeats a good reason. Type, content, period, parties, service, proof — each must be right, and documentation of the conduct does not cure a defect in the notice.

Self-help is prohibited without exception, and termination is a legal proceeding for counsel, not a date to promise an owner.

Reporting discharges the accounting duty. Regular, itemized, verifiable — and an owner who has not been reported to for months is a structural failure, not a backlog. The absence of complaints is not evidence of compliance.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a one-page file memorandum for the applied scenario presented with this assignment. Identify each instruction or practice that cannot be followed and why, state the correct handling of the funds involved, identify the fair housing analysis that applies to each applicant request, state the documentation you would create and retain, and identify what you would escalate to the broker or to counsel. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Practice - Lesson Assignment 13

Ethics, risk management, and dispute prevention

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Distinguish innocent error, negligent misrepresentation, and fraud by the standard each applies.
  • Correct a known error at the point where correction still has value to the person relying on it.
  • Recognize the fact patterns that indicate transaction fraud and respond through escalation rather than judgment.
  • Disclose conflicts of interest so that a client can evaluate the advice they affect.
  • Respond to a complaint or claim without committing the brokerage before the file has been reviewed.
  • Build the contemporaneous record that determines the outcome of a dispute years later.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: honesty, error, and misrepresentation55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: fraud patterns and conflicts50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: complaint response and documentation45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario and decision memorandum20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Honesty and fair dealing

1.1 The duty that runs to everyone

Fiduciary duties run to the principal. Honesty and fair dealing run to everyone — the other party, their agent, lenders, escrow, appraisers, and the public.

That is why "I don't represent them" answers the wrong question. It correctly identifies whose interests the licensee advances. It says nothing about whether the licensee may mislead them.

1.2 The three categories

StandardDistinguishing feature
Innocent errorA mistake made with reasonable careReasonable basis existed; care was used
Negligent misrepresentationA statement made without reasonable basis or reasonable careThe care taken, not the intent
FraudA knowing misstatement or concealment, intended to be relied onIntent to deceive

The distinction that matters daily is the first two. Most claims against licensees are negligence claims, and negligence does not require any dishonesty at all. A licensee who repeats a square footage figure from a prior listing without verifying or attributing it has not lied — and may still have made a negligent misrepresentation.

1.3 The three categories of statement

The practical discipline, introduced in Lesson 01 and applied throughout:

  1. Verified fact — stated with its source.
  2. Unverified report — attributed and flagged as unverified.
  3. Professional opinion — identified as opinion.

Nearly every misrepresentation in residential practice is a category collapse: an unverified report delivered as a fact, or an opinion delivered as a certainty.

1.4 Concealment is a form of misrepresentation

Saying nothing about a known material fact is not neutral. Where a duty to disclose exists, silence is concealment, and careful silence — avoiding an affirmative false statement while ensuring the fact is not discovered — is worse rather than better, because it demonstrates awareness.

1.5 Predictions

A prediction stated as a certainty is actionable:

StatementAssessment
"This will definitely appreciate over the next two years."A future outcome stated as fact, with no basis to guarantee it
"Property in this area has appreciated in recent years; here is the data. Past performance doesn't predict the future."Accurate, sourced, and appropriately qualified
"The city will approve your second unit."A prediction about a discretionary decision
"Here is the planning department's process and contact; they can tell you what applies."Correct

The defect is not optimism. It is stating as certain something the licensee cannot know, on which the client will rely.

1.6 The pressures that produce dishonesty

Almost nobody enters this business intending to misrepresent anything. The misrepresentations that occur are produced by a small number of recurring pressures, and a licensee who can name the pressure he is under is much better placed to resist it.

The pressureThe statement it produces
A transaction about to fail"It'll appraise." "The loan is fine." "They'll approve it."
A client who wants reassurance"Don't worry, that's normal for a house this age."
Not knowing the answer in front of a clientA confident guess, offered because "I'll find out" feels like weakness
A number needed to make a deal workA square footage, a tax figure, a fee "usually around" an amount
A colleague or manager applying pressureParticipation in something the licensee would not initiate
Money the licensee needs this monthEvery item above, with the volume turned up
An error already madeThe second statement, covering the first — and this is the one that ends careers

Two observations about this table.

The first six rows produce misstatements about the transaction. The last row produces misstatements about the licensee, and Section 6.6 explains why that difference is the whole difference. A licensee who told a buyer something inaccurate has a correctable problem. A licensee who then adjusts a record so the inaccuracy is not visible has a different kind of problem entirely.

The sixth row is the one Lesson 02's business plan addresses. A licensee with three months of reserves and a licensee with none face the same client conversation, and they do not face it the same way. Financial pressure is a compliance risk factor, which is an unusual sentence in an ethics chapter and a true one.

The defenses are not attitudes. They are sentences prepared in advance:

  • "I don't know — let me find out and get back to you today." Solves rows three and four permanently, and clients respond to it far better than licensees expect.
  • "That's a question for your inspector." And for the lender, the tax advisor, the attorney, the title officer. Section 1.3's category discipline, in its practical form.
  • "I can't predict that, and here's what I can tell you." Row one, answered without abandoning the client.
  • "I'm not comfortable with that — let's take it to the broker." Row five, and it does not require confrontation.
  • "I made a mistake. Here's what happened." Row seven, said early, when it is still a small sentence.

The pattern worth internalizing: every one of those is shorter than the misstatement it replaces. Honesty in this business is almost always the less effortful option in the moment and always the less effortful option afterward.

2. Errors, omissions, and supervision

2.1 The correction obligation

A licensee who discovers an error in something they said or produced must correct it immediately, in writing, to everyone who received the original — and, critically, before the recipient acts on it, if that is still possible.

The instinct to wait and see whether the error matters is the instinct that converts a mistake into a misrepresentation. An error you know about and leave uncorrected is no longer an error.

2.2 The worked case

A licensee realizes she transposed two digits reporting square footage. The buyer relied on it in setting his offer. Escrow closes in four days. Her manager suggests waiting: the buyer has an inspection report, the figure came from a public record, and raising it now may kill the transaction.

The correct action: correct the figure in writing to both sides immediately, notify her broker, and let the buyer decide what to do with accurate information while he still can.

Why each alternative fails:

  • Say nothing because it came from a public record. The source does not matter once she knows the figure she gave was wrong.
  • Say nothing because the buyer had his own opportunity to verify. The buyer's diligence does not discharge her duty.
  • Wait and offer a credit if he raises it. Arrives after the decision it should have informed.
  • Correct it verbally to the other agent. Not to the person relying on it, and no record.

The manager's advice is the problem, not the guidance. A licensee told to conceal a known error should escalate above the person who told her.

2.3 What supervision looks like from below

Licensees encounter supervision failures from the receiving end. Three situations and their correct responses:

SituationResponse
A manager tells you to conceal an errorEscalate to the responsible broker; the instruction does not protect you
Office practice deviates from the written policyRaise it; a policy nobody follows is not a defense for anyone
You are asked to handle work beyond your competence with no supportSay so in writing and ask for supervision or reassignment
