The practicum asks you to receive structured feedback. In practice you will more often be the person who has to give it — to a colleague whose comment, advertisement, or handling of a client was a problem. Most licensees say nothing, and the silence is what allows the conduct to continue.

Why it is hard, named honestly: the person is a peer or a superior, the comment was probably not malicious, the setting is social, and raising it makes the licensee the person who made things awkward. All four of those are real and none of them changes what is required.

What works:

  1. Address the conduct, not the person. "That phrasing in the listing describes who the neighborhood is for" lands. "That's discriminatory" makes the conversation about whether the person is a bigot, which is a conversation nobody wins and which the person will spend the rest of the exchange defending.
  2. Be specific and immediate. The general observation delivered a week later cannot be acted on. The specific one delivered now can.
  3. Assume it was not deliberate, out loud. Most of it is not, and the assumption gives the person somewhere to go other than defensiveness.
  4. Offer the replacement. "You can point them at the district's own data" is more useful than "you can't say that," and it removes the objection that the licensee is being unhelpful.
  5. Say it privately where the audience does not need to hear it — and publicly where the audience already heard the original, because a room that hears a comment and no response has learned something.
  6. Escalate what needs escalating. Section 3.4's scripts cover the unlawful instruction; a pattern in a colleague's conduct goes to the broker, and a single careless phrase in an advertisement usually does not.

What does not work: hinting, joking about it, telling someone else, sending an anonymous note, or resolving to bring it up if it happens again.

The reciprocal obligation, and it is the harder half: be the person who can receive this. A licensee who reacts badly the one time a colleague raises something has ensured that nobody raises anything again — and the next thing nobody raises will be worse than the thing they did not raise this time.

What the practicum is training is not the ability to identify bias in a written scenario, which is easy. It is the ability to say something in a room, which is the skill that actually interrupts anything, and which is why the exercise asks you to write the words rather than to describe what you would generally do.

6. Fair housing action plan and reflection

6.1 What makes a plan real

A plan changes outcomes only if it changes what is actually done and can be checked afterward. Statements of commitment, lists of protected classes, and training schedules are inputs — they leave day-to-day practice exactly as it was.

A real plan consists of specific, checkable practices.

6.2 The required elements

Your action plan must specify, in operational terms:

  1. Intake. The questions you ask every consumer, in the same way, and how you record the criteria they give you.
  2. Search. How you translate stated criteria into search parameters, and how you document the properties presented and any matching property not shown, with the reason.
  3. Questions you will decline. The specific questions, the words you will use, and the source you will point to for each.
  4. Showing and access protocol. Applied identically to every visitor, including whether you accompany, whether you ask for identification, and how you register visitors.
  5. Screening or referral criteria, where applicable, written and applied uniformly, with documented evaluations.
  6. Advertising review. What you check before anything is published, including the audience it will be delivered to.
  7. Outcome review. What you will look at, how often, and what would tell you something is wrong.
  8. Escalation. What you will do when a client or owner gives you an unlawful instruction — in the exact words you would use.

6.3 Testing your plan

Apply each element to this test: could someone else check whether you did it?

ElementCheckable?
"I will treat all clients equally."No — no observable behavior
"I will use the same eight intake questions with every buyer and record their stated criteria in the file."Yes
"I will be aware of my biases."No
"I will map the properties I showed each quarter and compare against the criteria on file."Yes
"I will not discriminate."No
"I will answer neighborhood-composition questions with the census and district scripts in my plan, and note the request in the file."Yes

6.4 The unlawful instruction script

Write this one out in full, in advance, because it is the hardest thing in this lesson to do in the moment — the client is in front of you, the listing is at stake, and the instruction is often delivered casually.

A workable structure:

"I can't do that, and I want to be straight with you about why. [Characteristic] is protected under California law, and screening or showing on that basis isn't something I'm able to do — it would put your listing and my license at risk. What I can do is [the lawful alternative that addresses the underlying concern]. I'm also going to let my broker know we discussed this, because that's the process."

Note the components: refuses plainly, explains without lecturing, offers a lawful alternative addressing the real concern, and escalates transparently rather than behind the client's back.

6.5 Written reflection

Submit a short reflection addressing:

  1. Which element of your plan will be hardest to sustain, and what would make you skip it?
  2. Where in your practice is discretion highest — where you decide case by case without a standard?
  3. What would you look at to find out whether your practice is producing unequal outcomes?
  4. What will you do differently next week, specifically?

The fourth question is the one that matters. A plan that changes nothing next week will not change anything later.

6.6 Sustaining the plan

A fair housing action plan written at the end of a course and never opened again is a document, not a plan. The difference is a review cycle and a small number of things that happen whether or not anyone remembers.

What runs on a schedule:

CadenceWhat happens
Every transactionThe search is documented against the buyer's stated criteria; neighborhood questions are answered from the scripts in Section 3.4; every inquiry is logged
MonthlyAutomated marketing and drip content reviewed for stale or sorting language; audience parameters on any paid advertising re-checked
QuarterlyThe Section 3.6 measurements run; exceptions reviewed; the vendor referral distribution examined
AnnuallyThe conflicts and vendor inventory; the advertising archive spot-checked; the criteria and scripts reviewed against what California currently protects, because the list is added to
On any incidentAn instruction refused, a comment made by a client, a pattern noticed — recorded, escalated, and added to what the next review looks at

What makes a plan fail:

  • It depends on remembering. Anything that requires the licensee to notice in the moment will fail on the day he is busy, which is the day it matters.
  • It has no owner. In a brokerage, a plan everyone supports and no one runs does not run.
  • It measures nothing, so it cannot tell whether it is working.
  • It has no place to put an uncomfortable finding, so findings stop being recorded.

What makes a plan durable: it is short, its items are things that happen rather than things people intend, and it produces a record even when nothing is wrong — because a review that only generates documents when there is a problem creates an incentive not to find one.

The last element, and the one most often left out: what the licensee does when the plan's own review finds something. Decide that in advance — who is told, what gets examined, and what changes — because deciding it in the moment, about your own practice, is the hardest version of the decision.

And the reflection this component asks for is not a summary of the course. It is a specific answer to a specific question: which of your own current practices did this lesson identify as unstructured, and what will replace it? A reflection that names no practice of your own has answered a different question.

6.7 What the practicum is for

This lesson has asked you to take a consumer's position, take a licensee's position, receive structured feedback, revise, and write a plan. The reason it is built that way rather than as a set of rules is worth stating plainly at the end.

The rules are easy and they are not the difficulty. Almost no licensee, asked in a classroom whether he may steer a buyer on a protected basis, gets the answer wrong. The violations that occur are not produced by ignorance of the rule.

They are produced by three things this course can only address through practice:

Speed. Section 1.2's point about implicit bias is that it operates fastest under time pressure and cognitive load — which describes a working licensee's ordinary day. A structural control operates at that speed; a good intention does not.

Social difficulty. The comment in the room, the client's remark in the car, the manager's instruction. Section 3.4's scripts exist because the gap between knowing what to say and having said it is the entire distance, and it is closed by having said it before rather than by understanding why it matters.

Invisibility. Section 1.5's stage-by-stage map shows that most of what produces unequal service is distribution — information, effort, follow-up — rather than refusal. A person cannot detect his own distribution from inside it. Section 3.6's measurements exist because the only way to see it is to count.

What you should be able to do, having finished this component:

  1. Name the stage of a transaction where a given risk lives, rather than treating fair housing as a topic that applies generally.
  2. Say the sentence — to a client, a colleague, an owner, or a manager — without rehearsing it first.
  3. Answer a neighborhood question from the scripts rather than from instinct, including when the question is friendly and the answer would have been complimentary.
  4. Run a measurement on your own practice and read the result without explaining it away.
  5. Point to a structural control you installed, not an intention you formed.

The single most useful thing to carry out of this lesson. Every control in it works whether or not you are thinking about bias on a given day — and the day you are not thinking about it is the day it operates. That is not a statement about anyone's character. It is the reason the profession's answer is structure rather than sincerity, and it is why the action plan in Section 6.2 asks for practices rather than commitments.

6.8 Checkpoint

You should be able to state why structure outperforms effort, produce the words you would use to decline a neighborhood-composition question, and write a plan element that someone else could verify you performed.

Key terms

TermDefinition
Explicit biasA consciously held attitude the person is aware of. The conduct fair housing law was originally written against.
Implicit biasAn association operating automatically, outside conscious awareness. Produces the same effects in people who sincerely reject the attitude.
Systemic biasPatterns built into practices, policies, and structures, producing unequal outcomes with no individual acting with bias at all.
Sincerity is not protectiveA licensee holding genuinely egalitarian views can act on associations they are unaware of. No contradiction — the association is not the belief.
Self-report cannot detect it"I know I don't have any biases" is not evidence, because the thing denied is by definition unavailable to introspection.
DiscretionWhere implicit bias operates: any decision made case by case, without a standard, on incomplete information, under time pressure.
Racially restrictive covenantA recorded restriction prohibiting sale or occupancy by members of specified groups. Unenforceable now; the language still appears in title chains and the settlement patterns persist.
RedliningFederally influenced appraisal and lending practice grading neighborhoods for risk, with grades correlated to racial composition. Poorly graded areas received less mortgage capital for decades.
Exclusionary land useZoning and subdivision requirements — minimum lot sizes, multifamily prohibitions, parking and setback rules — that raise cost and restrict who can live in an area, with effects independent of stated intent.
Unequal termsContract-for-deed and similar arrangements transferring occupancy without equity, so payments built no ownership.
CompoundingWhy exclusion does not end when the practice ends: housing wealth is inherited and becomes the next generation's down payment. Equal treatment starting now does not by itself produce equal outcomes.
Corrective steeringSelecting properties for a buyer based on a protected characteristic with a corrective intent. Still steering.
Structure beats effortThe central finding: conscious effort to be fair does not reliably reduce implicit bias, because effort depends on noticing. Structure works by removing the discretion.
Interruption practiceA specific structural control — written criteria, uniform scripts, consumer-driven search, objective sources, uniform showing protocols, documented evaluations, slowing under time pressure, outcome review.
Outcome reviewPeriodically examining your own patterns rather than your intentions — properties shown mapped, response times by source, who was encouraged or discouraged, whom your marketing reached.
Declining wellRefusing the characterization, giving an honest reason, and pointing to a usable source. Distinct from refusing flatly, answering "in general terms," or answering by redirecting the search.
Steering by omissionRedirecting the search or downplaying a property instead of answering the question asked.
Participatory componentThe consumer and licensee role-plays, written submissions, structured feedback, and revision. Cannot be satisfied by reading, and cannot be waived.
The revisionThe part of the practicum that matters most — the gap is not between knowing the rule and not knowing it, but between knowing it and producing compliant words while a client you like waits for an answer.
CheckabilityThe test of an action plan element: could someone else verify whether you did it?
Unlawful instruction scriptWords written in advance: refuse plainly, explain without lecturing, offer a lawful alternative addressing the real concern, escalate transparently.

Practice problems

Problem 1

A licensee says: "I understand implicit bias, but honestly it doesn't apply to me — I genuinely believe in equal treatment, I've never had a complaint in fifteen years, and I'd know if I were doing something wrong."

Address each of the three claims.

Solution. Each claim relies on an intuition that implicit bias specifically defeats.

"I believe in equal treatment." Sincerity is not protective. The association operates automatically and outside awareness, and it is not the belief — so a licensee who genuinely holds egalitarian views can act on associations they do not know they have. There is no contradiction to resolve.

"No complaints in fifteen years." The consumer in this position usually cannot name what happened — most describe feeling vaguely managed, or assume the agent knew something they did not. That is exactly why the conduct is hard to detect and hard to complain about, and why the obligation sits on the licensee rather than on the consumer to object.

"I'd know." Self-report is the one instrument implicit bias reliably evades. That is why the practicum targets practices and outcomes, which are observable, rather than self-assessment.

What transfers: the productive response is not to argue about whether he has biases. It is that a licensee who applies uniform practices produces fair outcomes whatever he holds internally.

Problem 2

Three brokerages produce unequal outcomes. Brokerage A has an owner who instructs managers to prefer certain applicants. Brokerage B has an agent who sincerely believes in equal treatment and consistently shows buyers "where they'd be more comfortable." Brokerage C applies one screening criterion identically to every applicant, builds its business entirely on client referrals, and spends its marketing budget where response rates are highest.

Classify each, and explain why the third is the hardest to see.

Solution. A is explicit bias — a consciously held attitude, acted on deliberately. B is implicit bias — the same effects produced by someone who rejects the attitude, and experiences his own conduct as helpfulness.

C is systemic, and nobody in it is acting with bias at all. A criterion applied identically can still produce unequal outcomes where it correlates with a history of unequal access. A referral-based model reproduces the composition of the existing client base. And a marketing budget spent where response rates are highest concentrates opportunity where it already exists.

It is hardest to see because every individual decision is defensible and none of them is about a protected characteristic. Recognizing the category is what allows a licensee to ask whether an inherited practice is producing an outcome they would not choose.

Problem 3

A licensee lists the decisions she makes in a typical week: which properties to show and in what order, how quickly to return an inquiry, how warmly to describe a property, whether to accompany a visitor, whether to ask about preapproval, whether to encourage an application, and how firmly to apply a screening criterion. She observes that none of these is about a protected characteristic.

Why is that observation the point rather than the reassurance?

Solution. Because implicit bias operates in discretion, not in decisions people recognize as being about protected characteristics. Wherever a licensee decides case by case, without a standard, on incomplete information, under time pressure, there is room for it to work — and every item on her list has exactly that shape.

The corresponding structural controls map onto them one for one: written criteria applied uniformly constrain the decision itself; uniform service scripts constrain differential treatment at first contact; the consumer's criteria driving the search constrain whose judgment selects properties; objective sources constrain characterization; uniform showing protocols constrain selective supervision; documented evaluations make the basis reviewable, which itself changes behavior; and slowing decisions matters because bias operates most strongly when fast.

What transfers: the reason this lesson sits in a practice course rather than an ethics seminar. Effort fails because it depends on noticing, and the thing to be noticed operates outside awareness.

Problem 4

A buyer asks to see two homes on Alder Street. The licensee, warm and helpful throughout, says he has "a couple of places you'll like better," shows three homes elsewhere, tells her she would "be more comfortable over here," and mentions the schools are "a better fit for a family like yours."

From the consumer's position: what did she receive, what does she now know about the Alder Street homes, and did his friendliness change the effect?

Solution. She received three properties she did not ask for and none of the two she did. She now knows nothing about the Alder Street homes — not their condition, not their price against the market, not whether they met her criteria. She cannot evaluate them, and she cannot even tell that she was prevented from evaluating them.

The friendliness changed nothing about the effect and made the conduct harder to name. The licensee is warm, well-meaning, and trying to be useful; the consumer still did not see the homes she asked about. Effect is independent of intent — which is exactly what the consumer-side role-play exists to make concrete, because being told this is different from experiencing the gap from that side.

"A better fit for a family like yours" is the sentence that carries the most and looks the most innocuous. It sorts people, and it was delivered as helpfulness.

Problem 5

A client you like says during a showing: "Can you tell us honestly what the neighbors are like? We just want to know if we'd fit in. And which schools would be right for our kids? We trust your judgment more than some website."

Give the words you would say — then handle "come on, off the record."

Solution. Something close to: "I can't answer the question about the neighbors, and I want to be straight with you rather than just dodging — I don't describe neighborhoods by who lives in them, and honestly my impression wouldn't be worth much to you anyway. What I can do is point you to the census data and the city's own information, and the district publishes its own performance data so you can weigh what actually matters to your family. And here's what I can tell you: the property itself, and what's within walking distance."

On the follow-up: "There isn't really an off the record for this one, and my honest answer is the same — I'd be guessing about people, and you'd be making a real decision on my guess." Held without hostility, and without lecturing.

Against the criteria: it declines the characterization, gives an honest reason, redirects to a usable source, preserves the relationship, handles the follow-up, distinguishes property from people, and does not steer by omission.

Problem 6

Evaluate four responses to the same question: (a) "It's a great neighborhood, you'll love it." (b) "I'm not allowed to discuss that" — then a change of subject. (c) "Let me just say the families around here are a lot like you." (d) "The schools here are excellent."

Rank them by how badly they fail.

Solution. (c) is the worst — direct steering, delivered in the warmest possible form, and the warmth is what makes it effective rather than what excuses it.

(d) is next: a ranking the licensee cannot support, and one of the most common proxies in residential practice. School quality is a proxy question far more often than it is a school question.

(a) is a characterization, and a favorable one is still a characterization. Licensees say it without noticing that they have answered the question they meant to decline.

(b) declines correctly and still fails — it leaves the client with nothing, reads as evasive, and sends him to a less careful source. The consumer has a real informational need, and refusing without redirecting does not make it go away.

Two related failures worth naming: answering "in general terms," since a general characterization is still a characterization; and answering by showing rather than telling — redirecting the search toward what you would have said.

Problem 7

Two action plans. Plan 1: "I will treat all clients equally, be aware of my biases, and not discriminate." Plan 2: "I will use the same eight intake questions with every buyer and record their stated criteria in the file; map the properties I showed each quarter against those criteria; and answer neighborhood-composition questions with the census and district scripts in my plan, noting the request in the file."

Apply the test, and identify what Plan 2 still needs.

Solution. The test is: could someone else check whether you did it? Plan 1 fails every element — commitment, awareness, and non-discrimination are not observable behaviors, and a plan of that kind leaves day-to-day practice exactly as it was. Plan 2's three elements each pass.

Plan 2 is still incomplete. The eight required elements are intake, search, questions you will decline, showing and access protocol, screening or referral criteria, advertising review — including the audience it will be delivered to — outcome review, and escalation.

Plan 2 covers intake, part of search, the declining scripts, and part of outcome review. It is missing the uniform showing and access protocol, screening criteria with documented evaluations, advertising review including audience targeting, and the escalation script.

On outcome review specifically: it is the practice most likely to detect a problem and the least used. Mapped showings, response times grouped by source, who was encouraged or discouraged, and whom the marketing reached. Individual decisions rarely reveal anything; patterns do — and a licensee who reviews their own patterns finds problems while they are still correctable.

Problem 8

An owner tells you casually, while signing, that he does not want the unit shown to a particular category of applicant. The listing is worth several months of income. Write what you would say.

Then identify the four components your answer must contain.

Solution. Something close to: "I can't do that, and I want to be straight with you about why. That's protected under California law, and screening or showing on that basis isn't something I'm able to do — it would put your listing and my license at risk. What I can do is apply the same written criteria to every applicant, which is what actually protects you if a decision is ever questioned. I'm also going to let my broker know we discussed this, because that's the process."

The four components: it refuses plainly; it explains without lecturing; it offers a lawful alternative that addresses the underlying concern; and it escalates transparently rather than behind the client's back.

Why this is written in advance: it is the hardest thing in the lesson to do in the moment. The client is in front of you, the listing is at stake, and the instruction is usually delivered casually — which is exactly the condition under which people improvise something softer and end up complying informally.

Three failures to name: following it while documenting that it came from the owner, which does not transfer responsibility; complying informally by scheduling around those applicants, which achieves the same exclusion; and withdrawing quietly without explanation, which leaves the owner free to repeat the instruction with the next licensee.

Chapter summary

Three kinds of bias, and this lesson is about the second and third. Explicit bias is the one licensees already know they must not act on. Implicit bias produces the same effects in people who reject the attitude. Systemic bias produces unequal outcomes with no individual acting with bias at all.

Sincerity, self-report, and good intentions each fail as protections, because the association operates outside awareness and the most consequential steering is committed by licensees who experience their conduct as helpfulness.

Bias operates in discretion — case-by-case decisions, without a standard, on incomplete information, under time pressure. None of them looks like a decision about a protected characteristic, and that is the point.

The history is in a practice course because the patterns are still on the ground. Covenants, redlining, steering, exclusionary land use, and unequal terms produced settlement patterns a licensee who does not know their origin will read as natural facts and pass on as market insight.

Exclusion compounds, because housing wealth is inherited and becomes the next generation's down payment. That explains why equal treatment starting now does not by itself produce equal outcomes — and it does not license corrective steering, which is still steering.

Structure beats effort. Effort depends on noticing; structure removes the discretion. Written criteria, uniform scripts, consumer-driven search, objective sources, uniform protocols, documented evaluations, slowing under pressure, and outcome review.

Review outcomes, not intentions. Patterns reveal what individual decisions never do.

Have the declining scripts ready, because licensees steer partly because declining is hard in the moment. Decline the characterization, give an honest reason, point to a usable source — and never refuse flatly, answer "in general terms," or answer by redirecting the search.

The participatory components cannot be satisfied by reading. The consumer role makes the independence of effect from intent concrete; the licensee role and its revision close the gap between knowing the rule and producing compliant words under real social pressure.

A plan is real when someone else can check it. Eight elements, in operational terms, each verifiable — and the question that matters is what you will do differently next week.

Write the unlawful-instruction script in advance: refuse, explain, offer the lawful alternative, escalate transparently.

Required applied assignment

Submit your written fair housing action plan covering all eight required elements in operational terms, together with your written reflection. Each element must be specific enough that a third party could verify whether you performed it. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric, including the checkability standard applied to each element.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, both participatory practicum submissions and the revision following feedback, the written action plan and reflection, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. The participatory components cannot be waived or satisfied by reading. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.

Real Estate Practice - Lesson Assignment 15

Integrated California residential transaction

Course version: 1.0 submission draft Assignment time: 180 instructional minutes Regulatory status: Not approved; no California credit is available.

Learning objectives

  • Sequence a complete California residential transaction and explain why each step precedes the next.
  • Carry a single transaction from intake through closing, identifying the obligation engaged at each stage.
  • Recognize, at each stage, the failure that most commonly occurs there and the control that prevents it.
  • Audit a completed file and rank its defects by seriousness rather than listing them.
  • Distinguish a documented gap from a falsified record, and state the correct response to each.
  • Apply the course's recurring principles — whose decision, whose duty, what evidence — to an unfamiliar situation.

Timed sequence

ActivityMinutesEvidence
Guided reading: transaction chronology and the listing side55Sections 1-2 viewed and notes saved
Guided reading: offer through investigation50Sections 3-4 viewed
Guided reading: closing and complete file audit45Sections 5-6 viewed
Applied scenario: full transaction audit20Written response submitted
Lesson assessment10Five scored items submitted
Total180One of 15 separate lesson assignments

1. Intake and agency

1.1 The chronology, and why it is a chronology

A residential transaction is a sequence in which each step supplies the information the next step requires. That is the organizing principle of this lesson, and it is the test to apply when you are unsure whether something is in the right order:

Does this decision have, in front of it, the information it was meant to be made with?

Every common sequencing error is a decision made before its information arrived.

#StepSupplies
1Agency disclosureThe consumer's understanding of the relationship
2Listing or representation agreementAuthority to act, and its terms
3Pricing analysis and adviceThe basis for the client's price decision
4Seller disclosure preparationThe material facts, before pressure exists
5MarketingExposure, on compliant copy
6Offer and negotiationThe terms
7AcceptanceThe contract, and the deadline clock
8Disclosure deliveryWhat the buyer needs before committing
9InvestigationWhat the buyer needs before removing contingencies
10Contingency removalThe buyer's commitment, informed
11Loan and appraisalThe financing
12Final verificationCondition confirmed while leverage exists
13Signing, funding, recording, disbursementThe transfer
14PossessionControl
15Audit and retentionThe record

1.2 The intake questions that determine everything after

Authority. Who can convey? Confirmed against title and the governing instrument — not assumed from occupancy or the tax roll. Trust means the trustee within the trust's authority; entity means the person the governing documents authorize; probate means what the appointment permits; co-owners means all of them.

Subject matter. The legal description, the boundaries, and what conveys — with disputable items named in the agreement so the fixture tests never need to be applied.

Constraints. Loans and liens, association obligations, leases and occupants, easements and encroachments, known conditions and repair history, permit status, and any pending matter affecting the ability to convey.

1.3 The agency sequence

Disclose, elect, confirm — at three different times:

RoleDisclosure timing
Seller's agentBefore the listing agreement is signed
Buyer's agentAs soon as practicable before the buyer signs an offer
Agent presenting an offer to the sellerBefore or at the time the offer is presented

Each is the same principle: the disclosure precedes the decision it informs.

Where the brokerage will act on both sides, informed written consent from both parties is required, and it must precede the brokerage acting on both sides — not follow it.

1.4 The failure that occurs here

The most common intake failure is unverified authority, and it is expensive because it surfaces in escrow. A listing signed by one of two co-owners, by a beneficiary rather than the trustee, or by a person whose power of attorney does not authorize real property conveyance cannot deliver what it promises.

The control: verify against a source at intake, and stop and escalate where authority is unclear rather than postponing the question.

1.5 The buyer-side version of the same stage

Sections 1.2 and 1.3 walk the seller-side intake. The buyer side runs the same chronology with different content, and it fails more often because nothing forces the paperwork.

What the buyer consultation must establish, in the order it matters:

  1. Capacity and agency, disclosed at the outset of the working relationship — Lesson 03, Section 2.5, Transaction B. Not at the first showing that produces an offer, by which point weeks of advice have been given.
  2. What the buyer is actually trying to do. The property is the instrument; the objective is a household, a timeline, a monthly cost, or an investment return. A search built on a property description and not on an objective produces the buyer who sees forty houses and likes none of them.
  3. Financial readiness, in the three separate senses — what a lender will lend, what the buyer can carry, and what cash exists at closing. Lesson 07 separates them; the intake is where the separation is made and recorded.
  4. The criteria, written down. Lesson 07, Section 4.5 requires the search be documented against them, and the record only works if the criteria exist first.
  5. The constraints the buyer has not mentioned — a lease expiration, a school year, a co-buyer in another city, a gift that arrives on a date, a job that has not started.
  6. What the licensee will and will not do, including the categories that route to counsel, a tax advisor, and the lender.

Two intake failures produce most buyer-side problems.

No agency disclosure until the offer. Lesson 03's second worked audit is built on it, and the defect is invisible until a complaint arrives, because the file contains a signed disclosure.

Readiness assumed from a preapproval letter. Lesson 07, Section 3.2 explains how to read one. A buyer whose readiness was assumed is a buyer whose transaction will fail during escrow rather than before it, and Lesson 10's worked underwriting failure is what that looks like.

The buyer-side intake note is the same two-minute document as the seller-side one in Section 1.4 of Lesson 02: what he said he wanted, what he was told about capacity and compensation, and what he was told the licensee would not do. It is the only evidence that will exist about the conversation that framed the rest.

2. Listing and marketing

2.1 The agreement

The listing is the brokerage's contract with the seller. The licensee should be able to explain every operative provision, and three deserve particular care:

  • Compensation is negotiable between the seller and the brokerage. Not standard, not set by any association or listing service.
  • The definite termination date protects the seller from an indefinite engagement.
  • The protection clause applies on its terms, ordinarily to buyers identified to the seller within a defined period after the term.

2.2 Pricing

The analysis and the advice are the licensee's obligation; the price is the seller's decision. Both halves.

The file should show the comparables used with their sources as of the date, the adjustment grid with the direction and basis of each adjustment, the reconciliation and its weighting, the comparables rejected and why, the market conditions evidence, the recommendation as a range with a most-probable point, and the written advice with the seller's decision.

Where the seller lists above the supported range, the file must show that the decision was informed — and must not adopt the seller's reasoning as the brokerage's opinion of value.

2.3 Disclosures start now, not at offer

The disclosure work belongs at the listing appointment. A seller who wants to defer it until an offer arrives is asking for the arrangement most likely to cost them: compressed buyer review, pressure at the worst moment, and the disputes that follow from pressure.

Separate the categories openly: price, showing schedule, and cancellation terms are negotiable; disclosure obligations are not.

2.4 Marketing

Truthful against the property's current condition. Every factual claim traceable to a source, with the source stated where it is the seller. Licensee and brokerage identification present and legible, including on each social post rather than only in the profile. Copy that describes the property rather than the people who should live in it — and audience targeting examined as part of the advertisement.

Approval before publication, and an archive of what ran, where, when, and with whose approval.

2.5 The failure that occurs here

Late disclosure preparation, and advertising that runs without approval or without identification. Both are invisible until they are not.

The control: disclosure work scheduled at listing, and an approval process fast enough that licensees do not route around it.

2.6 When the listing is not straightforward

The listing stage in Sections 2.1 through 2.4 assumes an owner who can convey, a property that is what it appears to be, and a market with comparables. Where any of those fails, the stage takes longer and skipping the extra work is the defect that surfaces later.

The complicationWhat it adds before marketing begins
Multiple owners, or an owner acting for anotherThe authority question, answered from documents rather than description — Lesson 04, Section 1.6
A trust, estate, or dissolutionAuthority, plus disclosure limits from a seller with little knowledge — Lesson 09, Section 1.6
Unpermitted or unrecorded improvementsVerification of what may be advertised, before a square footage or a room count is published — Lesson 06, Section 1.7
A tenant in possessionAccess rules, notice requirements, and disclosures the tenancy generates — Lesson 12
A property in a common-interest developmentAssociation documents ordered at the start, because the buyer's review period is what remains after delivery
Insufficient equityA different conversation entirely, with routing rather than pricing — Lesson 05, Section 4.6
A property with a hazard or insurance problemInsurance availability established early, because a buyer discovers it late otherwise — Lesson 09, Section 3.7
A thin comparable setA range with its reasoning rather than a number with false precision — Lesson 05, Section 1.6

The common structure across every row: the complication is knowable at intake and expensive after an offer. A licensee who takes the listing and resolves the complication later has moved the work to the point in the chronology where it collides with a buyer's deadlines, and Section 1.1 explains why that is the definition of a timing defect.

The professional posture: an intake that identifies three items requiring resolution before listing has done its job. The seller who wants to go on the market Friday is served, not obstructed, by being told which two of the three can run in parallel and which one cannot.

3. Offer and negotiation

3.1 Formation

An acceptance must match the offer. Any change to a material term is a counteroffer, which rejects the original and substitutes a new one — the original is gone, not suspended. This is true of a handwritten change on a signed counteroffer as much as of a formal document.

An offer may generally be revoked before acceptance even where it states a deadline, unless supported by consideration as an option.

3.2 Multiple offers

The seller chooses; there is no obligation to take the highest and no priority by order of receipt. Whether competing terms are disclosed is the seller's decision.

The line to hold: declining to disclose competing terms is permissible; inventing them is a misrepresentation. A client's instruction cannot authorize the second.

Where multiple counteroffers go out, the seller needs the protection of a final election, or the seller may be exposed on more than one acceptance.

3.3 Terms are the client's; advice is the licensee's

For a buyer waiving contingencies, a seller weighing a credit-heavy offer, or either party considering an escalation clause: explain in writing what the term does and what it exposes them to, then follow the informed decision.

Both failure modes are wrong — refusing to write what the client instructs overstates the licensee's role; writing it without comment abandons the advisory duty.

3.4 The failure that occurs here

Misrepresenting competing offers, and failing to document that a waiver was informed.

The control: never describe an offer that does not exist, and put the consequences of a waiver in writing before the client decides.

3.5 A worked negotiation

The situation. A listing at 849,000, eleven days on market, one showing-heavy weekend. Two offers arrive Sunday evening.

  • Offer A: 842,000, conventional financing with twenty percent down, standard periods, no repair requests contemplated, close in thirty days, preapproval from a lender the listing agent has not heard of.
  • Offer B: 855,000, ten percent down, investigation period shortened to ten days, appraisal contingency waived, close in twenty-one days, a letter about how much the buyers love the house, preapproval from a national lender.

What the licensee does first. Presents both. Lesson 08, Section 2.4 governs, and neither offer is characterized before the seller has seen them.

Then the analysis the seller needs, which is not a ranking.

On price: B is 13,000 higher nominally. The net comparison is what matters — Lesson 10, Section 5.3 — and the offers must be compared on proceeds after costs and credits rather than on the headline.

On the appraisal waiver: B's waiver is worth something only if the buyer can perform it. Ten percent down and a waived appraisal contingency is a combination that deserves scrutiny — if the property appraises 20,000 low, the buyer must produce that cash on top of his down payment, and the file gives no indication he has it. Lesson 08, Section 4.7 explains the mechanism, and Lesson 10, Section 1.6 explains what verifying capacity actually looks like.

On the lenders: the unfamiliar lender on A is not a defect. Lesson 10, Section 1.3 governs how to read a preapproval, and the reading is about what the letter says was verified, not about brand recognition. Treating a national name as inherently stronger is a shortcut, and it is the sort of shortcut that quietly disadvantages some buyers.

On the timelines: B closes in twenty-one days. Whether B's lender can close in twenty-one days is a question with an answer, obtainable by asking, and an aggressive date the lender cannot meet converts the buyer's strength into his default.

On the letter: it is set aside. Personal letters can convey protected characteristics, and Lesson 14's analysis applies — a seller's decision influenced by what a letter reveals about who the buyers are is exactly the decision fair housing law reaches. The listing agent's handling of these is a brokerage policy question, decided in advance rather than offer by offer.

What the licensee says. The analysis, the risks in each, the questions he recommends asking before the seller decides, and no recommendation about which buyer deserves the house. Lesson 03, Section 3.1: the terms and the decision are the seller's; the analysis is the licensee's.

What the licensee does not do. Tell either buyer's agent what the other offered. Lesson 08, Section 3.3 draws the line between confidentiality and misrepresentation, and "there's another offer at 855" is confidential negotiating information about the seller's position, disclosed to the party negotiating against him.

What the file records: both offers, the analysis presented, the questions asked and their answers, the seller's decision and its date, and the disposition of the offer not accepted — Lesson 07, Section 6.9.

4. Disclosure and investigation

4.1 Two independent duties

The seller discloses known material facts. The licensee conducts a reasonably competent visual inspection of accessible areas and discloses what it reveals. Neither discharges the other, and a professional inspection discharges neither.

Record observations as observations. "Brown staining approximately three feet across on the northwest bedroom ceiling; paint appears newer in that area" is what a licensee is qualified to state. "There was a roof leak that has been repaired" is an inference the licensee is not qualified to make and that the buyer may rely on.

4.2 Materiality

Measured by effect on a reasonable buyer's decision — not by the seller's view of importance, not by how often the condition appears, and not by whether the buyer could have seen it. A repaired condition is disclosed, because the adequacy of the repair is the question.

Every item answered; "unknown" where uncertain; never "no" where the seller does not know; never completed by the licensee on the seller's behalf.

4.3 Delivery is where rights attach

Content is half. Delivery and its date is the other half, because rights run from delivery.

Where a disclosure arrives after contingencies were removed, the correct response is to raise it immediately rather than proceed as though the transaction were firm. Delivery before closing does not cure late delivery, and moving the closing date does not address the buyer's right to respond.

4.4 Investigation

Recommend investigation matched to the property, not recited generically. Confirm the buyer engaged with the report; do not interpret it. Where a finding exceeds the licensee's competence, disclose the observation, recommend the specialist, and document the recommendation and the buyer's response.

Start the slow items first — insurance in mapped hazard areas, association documents, permit research — because a finding that arrives with two days left in the contingency period is a finding the buyer cannot act on.

4.5 Contingency removal

Under the standard California residential form, contingencies remain in effect until removed in writing. A passed date removes nothing. The seller's route is the contractual notice, which starts the period the contract specifies.

Read the contract's own mechanism rather than assuming deadlines are self-executing.

4.6 The failure that occurs here

Undated or missing delivery evidence, and assuming a passed date removed a contingency.

The control: evidence every delivery with its date, and build the deadline calendar from the contract's own terms.

5. Escrow, finance, and closing

5.1 Escrow

Neutral, acting only on mutual written instructions. It does not advise, does not decide disputes, and does not release funds because one party asserts entitlement. Where the parties disagree, escrow stops.

Escrow's instructions implement the purchase agreement; where they conflict, the parties' agreement governs and the discrepancy is reconciled by the parties in writing.

5.2 Title

The preliminary report states the terms on which the insurer would insure, including exceptions. An exception means the policy does not insure against that item — which is why exceptions are read rather than skimmed, and why the underlying documents matter.

Vesting is a legal and tax question. Refer it to counsel before escrow prepares the deed.

5.3 Financing

Lenders lend against the lower of appraised value and purchase price. A low appraisal is a financing event first: the buyer may bring funds, renegotiate, seek another resolution, or cancel if a contingency permits and its procedure is followed.

Where the appraisal contingency has been removed, lay out the practical options, state that the removed contingency limits his contractual rights, and recommend counsel before he decides to stop performing.

A credit is not a price reduction for appraisal purposes. Program limits constrain how large a credit can be.

Warn the buyer in writing at the start of escrow about the events that void a conditional approval — new credit, job changes, large unexplained deposits, moving funds — and about wire fraud, before any funds move.

5.4 Closing

Signing, funding, recording, disbursement — four events, in that order. Recording establishes the interests of record and generally makes the buyer the owner of record; disbursement follows confirmation of recording. Recording order establishes lien priority.

Possession transfers when the contract says, which may not be at recording. Any occupancy arrangement is documented with term, payment, condition, insurance, and a remedy for holdover.

5.5 The failure that occurs here

Changes made without a signed amendment. A date change agreed by email between agents does not change the contract, and if the transaction fails the parties are litigating on the original terms with correspondence between non-parties as the evidence.

The control: every change to a term is a written amendment signed by both parties and delivered to escrow.

5.6 The closing week

The last seven days of a transaction concentrate more failure modes than the preceding forty, because everything deferred arrives at once and everyone involved is working against the same date.

What should already be done by day minus seven, and the list is short because it is the list that cannot be compressed:

  1. All contingencies resolved, with the removals dated and delivered.
  2. All amendments signed and at escrow — Lesson 11, Section 1.6. Anything agreed and not transmitted does not exist to escrow.
  3. Loan clear to close, with any remaining conditions identified by name rather than described as "just a couple of things."
  4. Payoff demands obtained, with good-through dates that cover the actual closing.
  5. Association documents delivered and any transfer requirements started.
  6. Insurance bound, which is the item most often assumed and least often confirmed.
  7. The estimated closing statement reviewed against the agreement and the amendments — Lesson 10, Section 5.7.

What happens in the final days:

The walkthrough — Lesson 11, Section 4.7 — performed with enough margin before signing that a finding can be addressed. A walkthrough the morning of closing is a walkthrough that cannot produce a response.

Wire instructions, and the single non-negotiable discipline in this course: verify by voice, at a number obtained independently, before any funds move. Not the number in the email. Not by replying to the thread. The warning is given at the start of the transaction and repeated in the closing week, because that is when the attack arrives.

The signing, where the client will be handed documents he has not seen. The licensee's contribution is that nothing at the table is a surprise — which is a statement about the preceding six weeks rather than about the signing.

Possession, arranged, documented, and — if anyone will be in the property after closing — reduced to a written agreement rather than a courtesy. Lesson 11, Section 5.2.

The failure modes to watch for, each addressed elsewhere in the course:

The failureWhere it was prevented
A credit agreed and never transmittedLesson 11, Section 1.6
A condition surfacing on day 33Lesson 10, Section 4.7
A payoff demand expiring before closingLesson 10, Section 5.6
A walkthrough finding with no time to respondLesson 11, Section 4.7
Wire instructions from a compromised threadLesson 11, Section 4.6
Possession by handshakeLesson 11, Section 5.2

The pattern in all six: none is a hard problem. Each is an easy problem that arrived on the day it could no longer be solved, and the closing week is where the chronology's earlier shortcuts present their bill.

6. Complete file compliance audit

6.1 The standard

Reconstructability: could a person who was not there determine what happened, when, and on what basis?

6.2 The audit sequence

  1. Completeness — every required document present, including for the parts that did not go smoothly.
  2. Dates — everything dated; delivery evidenced where timing matters.
  3. Consistency — names, dates, amounts, and property descriptions reconcile.
  4. Chronology — the order makes sense against the contract and against the disclose-before-decide principle.
  5. Nothing improper present — no unexecuted draft mistaken for final, no superseded figure presented as current.
  6. Retention — filed where it will be found, for the required period.

Keep superseded versions. The sequence of versions is frequently what shows when a term changed and who agreed — which is the question a dispute usually asks.

6.3 A worked audit, ranked

A closed file contains:

  • (a) An agency disclosure signed and dated three days after the listing agreement.
  • (b) A confirmation naming the brokerage as buyer's agent, while the listing file shows it as seller's agent, with no dual agency consent anywhere.
  • (c) An email agreeing to move the closing date, with no signed amendment.
  • (d) A contingency removal, signed but undated.
  • (e) A transfer disclosure with two items left blank.
  • (f) A referral fee disclosure dated the day of closing, for a warranty the buyer bought six weeks earlier.

Ranked by seriousness:

1. (b) The undisclosed dual agency. Different in kind from the others. It describes the brokerage acting on both sides without either party's consent — a breach of duties actually owed, not a documentation failure.

2. (d) The undated removal. Rights ran from it and the file cannot show when. This is the defect a dispute is most likely to turn on.

3. (a) Disclosure after the agreement. The seller committed before being informed. The document exists; the protection it was meant to give does not.

4. (f) Late conflict disclosure. Delivered after the buyer acted, which is the one thing a conflict disclosure must not be.

5. (c) Unsigned date change. The operative contract still says the original date.

6. (e) Blanks in the disclosure. Ambiguous about whether the items were addressed.

The response: record each finding, escalate to the broker, obtain what legitimately exists, and address the processes that produced them.

6.4 The line

What must not happen: dating the removal now, completing the blanks now, producing an amendment now with a matching date, or creating the consent that was never obtained.

A documented gap is a compliance problem a brokerage can survive.

A manufactured document is falsification. It converts a defensible situation into a question about integrity, and it ends careers.

Corrections are made visibly, with the original preserved and the change attributable. A record that can be silently altered is not a record.

6.5 The course in four questions

When you meet a situation this course did not cover, four questions will usually produce the right answer:

  1. Whose decision is this? Price, terms, and whether to proceed belong to the client. The analysis and the advice belong to you. Legal effect belongs to counsel.
  2. Whose duty is engaged? Fiduciary duties run to the principal; honesty, fair dealing, and disclosure of material facts run to everyone.
  3. Does this decision have the information it requires? If not, something is out of order.
  4. What will the file show? If the answer is "nothing," create the record now, while it is contemporaneous and true.

6.6 The four questions, applied

Section 6.5 supplies four questions for situations this course did not cover. Their value is only demonstrable on situations it did not cover, so here are three.

Situation one. A past client calls. Her elderly father is selling his house and moving in with her. She wants you to list it. On the visit, the father is present but says little; the daughter answers for him, produces a power of attorney, and mentions that "he gets confused sometimes." She wants a fast sale at a price you think is well below the range.

Whose decision is this? The seller's. Which raises the question of who the seller is — the father, with the daughter acting under an authority whose scope is a document you have not read and whose validity is a legal question.

Whose duty is engaged? If you take the listing, fiduciary duties run to the principal. Determining who the principal is, and whether the person instructing you has authority to instruct you, is the first task and not a formality.

Does this decision have the information it requires? The price is below your range and the seller has not spoken. You do not know that the father knows what is being done.

What will the file show? Right now, nothing. The correct actions: read the authority, speak with the father directly and privately, deliver the pricing analysis to him, document what you observed, and take the whole thing to your broker — because elder financial abuse is a live concern with specific California frameworks, and this fact pattern is one of its recognized shapes. You do not accuse anyone. You escalate facts.

Situation two. Your buyer client, in contract, tells you he has decided to rent the property out rather than live in it, and asks you not to mention it because "the loan is an owner-occupied loan and it's none of their business."

Whose decision is this? His occupancy is his. What he tells his lender is not a matter of preference — occupancy is material to the credit decision.

Whose duty is engaged? Confidentiality runs to him. It does not extend to participating in a misrepresentation to a third party, which is the boundary Lesson 03, Section 3.2 draws in a different context and which holds here.

Does this decision have the information it requires? He may not know that occupancy is material or that a misstatement is loan fraud. Telling him is the first step and it frequently ends the problem.

What will the file show? Whatever you write down today. The correct actions: tell him plainly, tell him to inform his lender, decline to be part of any communication that states otherwise, and escalate to your broker. You do not resolve it by not asking again.

Situation three. A seller you represent tells you at the listing appointment that the neighbor's fence has been three feet onto his property for twenty years and "we've had an understanding." He would rather not raise it now.

Whose decision is this? Whether to resolve the boundary is his. Whether it is disclosed is not.

Whose duty is engaged? Disclosure of material facts runs to everyone. A boundary discrepancy affects what the buyer is acquiring, and it is squarely material.

Does this decision have the information it requires? No — and neither does the seller, because the legal effect of twenty years of an "understanding" is a question with real answers that neither of you knows.

What will the file show? The correct actions: disclose it, recommend the seller obtain legal advice about it before listing, recommend a survey, document the conversation the day it happens, and do not characterize the boundary yourself.

The pattern across all three. None was covered by a rule you memorized. All three resolved to the same four steps: identify the decision-maker, identify the duty, determine whether the decision is informed, and create the record. That sequence is what this course was building, and it will answer situations that have not happened yet.

6.7 Checkpoint

You should be able to take an unfamiliar transaction problem, apply the four questions, and produce both the action and the record it requires.

Practice problems

Problem 1

A licensee takes a listing from a woman living in the home. Title is held by her late mother's trust; she is one of three beneficiaries. The listing agreement is signed at the appointment; the agency disclosure is signed three days later; the property goes live the same week with photographs from the prior listing and a square footage figure copied from it.

Apply the ordering test to each event.

Solution. Four failures, and three of them are sequencing failures of the same kind.

Authority was never verified — the most common and most expensive intake failure. The trustee signs, within the authority the trust document grants; beneficiaries do not, however many and however clearly they benefit. This surfaces in escrow, where unwinding costs the most.

The agency disclosure came after the listing agreement. The seller committed before being informed. The document now exists and the protection it was meant to give does not.

The photographs and the square footage each precede the information they required: truthfulness is measured against the property's current condition, and copying a figure from a prior listing launders an unverified number rather than verifying it.

The ordering test disposes of all three: does this decision have, in front of it, the information it was meant to be made with?

Problem 2

A seller lists $150,000 above the licensee's supported range after receiving the analysis and written advice. Two weeks later the licensee revises her comparative market analysis upward so the file "matches the list price," and tells a buyer's agent that the price reflects the brokerage's opinion of value.

What was right, and what was wrong?

Solution. The listing at the seller's number was right. Price is the seller's decision; the analysis and the advice are the licensee's obligation. She discharged both — the analysis, the written advice, and the seller's informed decision are what the file needs.

The revision is the failure, and it is worse than having no analysis. An analysis that follows the price rather than informing it documents nothing. And representing the list price as the brokerage's opinion of value adopts the seller's reasoning as the brokerage's — the one thing the file must not do.

What the file should show: the comparables with sources as of the date, the adjustment grid with direction and basis, the reconciliation and its weighting, the comparables rejected and why, the market conditions evidence, the recommendation as a range with a most-probable point, the written advice, and the seller's decision.

Problem 3

A buyer offers $880,000. The seller strikes the price to $895,000, signs, and returns it. The buyer signs but writes a different closing date before returning it. Meanwhile the seller's agent tells two other buyers there are "several offers well above asking," when there is one, and counters three offers at $940,000 with no further provision.

State where the parties stand and identify every defect.

Solution. No contract. The seller's changes were a counteroffer, which rejected the $880,000 offer and substituted a new one — the original is gone, not suspended. The buyer's handwritten date change is a further counteroffer, however small; signing does not convert a modification into an acceptance. The buyer's counter is outstanding.

"Several offers well above asking" is a misrepresentation. Declining to disclose competing terms is permissible and is the seller's decision; inventing them is not, and a client's instruction cannot authorize it.

Countering three offers without a final election exposes the seller on more than one acceptance — the customary protection makes the seller's obligation contingent on subsequent written acceptance of the buyer's acceptance.

Worth adding for the competing buyers' agents: the seller chooses, there is no obligation to accept the highest, and there is no priority by order of receipt.

Problem 4

The listing agent observes ceiling staining with fresh paint. The seller says a pipe leaked, was repaired, and "there's nothing to disclose." The seller's disclosure comes back with two blanks. The transfer disclosure is delivered to the buyer nine days after he removed his contingencies, and the listing agent proposes extending the closing by a week if he objects.

Address each.

Solution. Both duties are engaged and neither discharges the other. The seller discloses the leak and the repair — a repaired condition is still material, because the adequacy of the repair is exactly what a buyer evaluates, and here there are no records. The agent separately records and discloses the observation: "brown staining approximately three feet across on the northwest bedroom ceiling; paint appears newer in that area." Not the inference that there was a leak that has been repaired.

The blanks are completed — every item answered, "unknown" where that is true, never "no" where the seller does not know, and never completed by the licensee on the seller's behalf.

The late delivery is raised immediately, not managed. Rights run from delivery and are not extinguished because the buyer acted earlier without the information; late delivery may revive or extend his ability to respond. Receipt before closing does not cure it, and moving the closing date does not address the right to respond.

Problem 5

The buyer's investigation contingency period ends on the 17th. Nothing is delivered. On the 18th the seller's agent tells the seller she is free to accept a better offer. The buyer's agent, separately, has not yet ordered insurance quotes for a property in a mapped fire area, with four days left in the period.

Correct the first and assess the second.

Solution. The contingency remains in effect. Under the standard California form contingencies end only by written removal; a passed date removes nothing, the buyer is not committed, and the seller is not free to sell elsewhere. The seller's route is the contractual notice, which starts the period the contract specifies — after which she may cancel per its procedure.

The insurance is the other half of the same lesson. Most failed transactions fail because a track started late, and in mapped fire and flood areas availability and cost have become a common cause of failure. A buyer who learns in week one that coverage will cost far more than assumed can act; one who learns it with four days left generally cannot.

The control for both: build the deadline calendar from the contract's own terms, share it in writing, and start the slow items first — insurance, association documents, permit research, specialist inspections.

Problem 6

The appraisal comes in $20,000 below the contract price on a transaction where the buyer waived the appraisal contingency. The buyer asks whether he can cancel and recover his deposit. His agent emails the appraiser three sales and adds that "the deal dies without another $20,000." The parties then agree by email to move closing back a week, and the buyer separately asks escrow to hold $3,000 from the seller's proceeds.

Assess each of the four.

Solution. The deposit question is legal. Lay out the practical options — bring funds, renegotiate, seek another resolution — state plainly that the removed contingency limits his contractual rights, and recommend counsel before he decides to stop performing. Telling him he may cancel and recover the deposit, or that he simply must perform, are both legal conclusions and both may be wrong.

The email to the appraiser mixes the permitted with the prohibited. Relevant sales through the proper channel are information and are fine. "The deal dies without another $20,000" is a target, and a value that moves after that conversation is worthless for the purpose the appraisal serves — including when the request serves the licensee's own client.

The email closing extension changes nothing. A change to a term is a written amendment signed by both parties and delivered to escrow. If the transaction fails, the parties litigate on the original date with correspondence between non-parties as the evidence.

Escrow cannot act on the holdback. It acts only on mutual written instructions; one party cannot redirect funds, and an agent's confirmation is not the other party's consent. Escrow stops — and the agents' job is to get a mutual instruction in place before the closing date.

Problem 7

Rank the defects in this closed file and state the correct response: (a) an agency disclosure dated three days after the listing agreement; (b) a confirmation naming the brokerage as buyer's agent while the listing file shows seller's agent, with no dual agency consent anywhere; (c) an email agreeing to move closing, no signed amendment; (d) a contingency removal signed but undated; (e) a transfer disclosure with two blanks; (f) a referral fee disclosure dated the day of closing for a warranty the buyer bought six weeks earlier.

Rank, and give the response.

Solution. 1. (b), the undisclosed dual agency — different in kind from every other item. It describes the brokerage acting on both sides without either party's informed written consent: a breach of duties actually owed, not a documentation failure.

2. (d), the undated removal. Rights ran from it and the file cannot show when. This is the defect a dispute is most likely to turn on.

3. (a), disclosure after the agreement. The seller committed before being informed.

4. (f), the late conflict disclosure. Delivered after the buyer acted — the one thing a conflict disclosure must not be, since information after the decision has no value.

5. (c), the unsigned date change. The operative contract still says the original date.

6. (e), the blanks. Ambiguous about whether the items were addressed.

The response: record each finding, escalate to the broker, obtain what legitimately exists, and address the processes that produced them rather than only the instances.

What must not happen: dating the removal now, completing the blanks now, producing an amendment with a matching date, or creating the consent that was never obtained. A documented gap is survivable; a manufactured document is falsification.

Problem 8

A licensee faces a situation this course did not cover: a seller's adult son, who holds a power of attorney, wants to accept an offer while the seller is hospitalized, and asks the licensee whether the power of attorney is sufficient and whether the seller's condition must be disclosed to the buyer.

Apply the four questions.

Solution. Whose decision is this? Not the licensee's, on either question. Whether the power of attorney authorizes conveyance of real property is a legal question — it depends on the instrument's terms and its current validity — and it goes to counsel, with escrow and title involved. Whether to accept the offer is the principal's decision, exercised by whoever actually holds authority.

Whose duty is engaged? Honesty and fair dealing run to the buyer; fiduciary duties run to the seller. The disclosure question is genuinely constrained on both sides — a seller's illness is subject to specific California limits, and this is squarely a do not volunteer, do not misrepresent, escalate situation rather than one to resolve at a signing table.

Does this decision have the information it requires? No. Acceptance cannot precede verified authority, and the licensee does not yet have it.

What will the file show? Right now, nothing. So the record is created contemporaneously: what was asked, what was said, what was escalated and when, and what was referred to counsel.

What transfers: the four questions are what the course leaves you with. They resolve situations nobody wrote a lesson about.

Chapter summary

A transaction is a chronology in which each step supplies the information the next requires. Every common sequencing error is a decision made before its information arrived, and the ordering test finds them.

Verify authority at intake against a source — trustee, entity representative, personal representative, all co-owners — and stop and escalate where it is unclear.

Disclose, elect, confirm, each preceding the decision it informs, with dual agency consent preceding the brokerage acting on both sides.

Compensation is negotiable; the termination date protects the seller; the protection clause is explained rather than initialed past.

The analysis and advice are the licensee's obligation; the price is the client's decision. Never revise the analysis to match the price.

Start the disclosure work at the listing appointment, and separate the negotiable preferences from the obligations openly.

Advertising is truthful against current condition, sourced, identified, property-focused, approved, and archived — with audience targeting examined as part of the advertisement.

A counteroffer rejects and replaces. An offer may generally be revoked before acceptance. Declining to disclose competing terms is permissible; inventing them is not. Counter multiple offers only with a final election preserved.

Terms are the client's; advice is the licensee's — put waivers and their consequences in writing before the decision, and then follow it.

Two independent disclosure duties, observations recorded as observations, every item answered, delivery evidenced with its date.

Contingencies end by written removal, not by a passed date, and the seller's route is the contractual notice.

Escrow acts only on mutual written instruction and stops when the parties disagree. An exception means the policy does not insure against that item. Vesting goes to counsel before the deed is drawn.

Lenders lend against the lower of value or price, a credit is not a price reduction for appraisal purposes, and the start-of-escrow warnings — credit, employment, moving funds, and wire fraud — are the cheapest protection available.

Every change is a signed amendment. Signing, funding, recording, disbursement, in that order.

Audit for reconstructability, keep superseded versions, obtain what exists, record what does not, and fix the process. A documented gap is survivable; a manufactured document is not.

And when the situation is unfamiliar, four questions: whose decision, whose duty, does this decision have its information, and what will the file show.

Required applied assignment

Prepare a full transaction audit memorandum for the file presented with this assignment. Sequence the transaction's compliance events and identify any that occurred out of order; identify each defect and rank the defects by seriousness with your reasoning; state, for each, what may legitimately be obtained now and what may not be created; identify the process failure behind each rather than only the instance; and state what you would escalate. The instructor version contains the scoring rubric, including the ranking rationale.

Knowledge check and remediation

Complete the five-item lesson quiz in the secure learning platform. The lesson mastery threshold is 80 percent — four of the five items. An unsuccessful attempt opens targeted remediation: an alternate explanation of the concepts, a second worked scenario, and a different five-item set covering the same concepts. The 80 percent threshold is a StateCourse instructional policy proposed for DRE review; it is not represented as a DRE-mandated score.

Assignment completion

Completion requires all six instructional segments, the applied audit memorandum, the quiz submission, and any triggered remediation. Assignment completion does not waive the separate final examination, minimum elapsed-time, identity, proctor, or approval requirements.
